# Sūrah al-Ḥajj (Chapter 22) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:1

> O humanity! Fear your Lord, for the ˹violent˺ quaking at the Hour is surely a dreadful thing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Pilgrimage Sūrah That Opens With an Earthquake**

The sūrah carries the name of the pilgrimage because the rite is proclaimed in it (22:27), yet its first sentence is not about a rite at all. Before anyone is told how to walk around a House, the ground itself refuses to hold still. That ordering makes a claim about worship. A ritual performed against a backdrop of permanent, indifferent nature is one thing; a ritual performed inside a world with an expiry date is another. Whoever reaches the pilgrimage verses of this sūrah has already been told, in its opening line, that the stage on which the rites are performed will be shaken apart.

The address is *yā ayyuhā al-nāsu* — O humanity — the widest form of address in the Qur'an's vocabulary. It is not *yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū*, the formula that opens most Medinan passages and that appears near the end of this sūrah (22:77). The terrors of the Hour are not announced to a community that already accepts them; they are announced to everyone, and the announcement is built to produce the fear it calls for.

**Taqwā Is a Shield, and Here You Can See What It Guards Against**

*Ittaqū* comes from the root *w-q-y*, to guard or to shield. Its noun, *taqwā*, names the state of having put something between yourself and a danger. Most occurrences in the Qur'an leave the danger general: reverence God, and the object of the reverence is God's own claim on you. This verse does something rarer. It names the danger in the same breath — reverence your Lord, because the quaking at the Hour is a dreadful thing.

The pairing is deliberate. What is commanded is not panic at a natural catastrophe; it is reverence aimed at the Lord who sends it. The earthquake is not a rival power to be appeased or bribed. Placing the two clauses side by side keeps the terror and the theology in the right order: the event is horrifying, and the event is not in charge.

**The Hour Arrives in the Text Already Familiar**

*Al-sāʿah* comes with the definite article. The term is not introduced, defined, or established by argument; it is used as something the audience already possesses. The same is true across the Qur'an, and that is why the word functions almost as a proper name for the end.

The ordinary sense of *sāʿah* is a short stretch of time — an hour, a moment. Pressing the word for a brief interval into service as the name for the end of history is a compression worth pausing on. The end arrives the way an hour arrives: not gradually, not negotiated, and not late. Suddenness belongs to the word before any verse attaches it to the thing.

**"The Quaking of the Hour": a Construct That Carries Two Readings**

The Arabic is *zalzalata al-sāʿati*, the quaking of the Hour. Two readings are available to this annexation, and the grammarians recorded both. One takes it as temporal: the quaking that occurs when the Hour comes. The other takes it as descriptive: the quaking that *is* the Hour's opening act. The difference is small on the page and large in implication. On the first reading the shaking is a herald; on the second it is the thing itself beginning.

Both readings survive because the verse declines to choose between them, and the refusal is part of the rhetoric. A reader who wants to fix the quaking on a timeline is given no peg to hang it on.

**"A Dreadful Thing" Refuses to Specify**

The closing predicate is indefinite: *shayʾun ʿaẓīmun*, a dreadful thing, a mighty thing. *Shayʾ* is among the vaguest nouns in Arabic — a thing, anything at all — and *ʿaẓīm* among the widest adjectives. The sentence could have said how long the quaking lasts, how many it kills, or which cities go down. It says none of these.

The vagueness works. Specific terror can be measured, and what can be measured can be planned around. An unmeasured *shayʾ* leaves the imagination nowhere to rest, which is precisely what a sūrah needs from its first line.

**A Word Built for Repetition**

*Zalzalah* comes from the doubled root *z-l-z-l*. In Arabic, doubling a root often signals repetition or intensity, and the pattern here is the same one found in *waṣwaṣa* (to whisper repeatedly) or *qalqala* (to shake about). The quaking is not a single jolt. The form of the word implies a shaking that keeps shaking.

The same word is used elsewhere in the Qur'an for a shaking that is not geological at all — the shaking of people under pressure: *“Then and there the believers were put to the test, and were violently shaken”* (33:11). That parallel matters for reading this sūrah. The word describes what happens to a community when its certainties are tested, as well as what happens to the ground. A sūrah that opens with *zalzalah* is announcing that the world will be shaken and that the people in it will be shaken with it — the outer convulsion and the inner one are named with one root.

**Why the Sūrah Starts Here Instead of at the Kaʿbah**

A religion can be practised as inheritance. The rites of verses 26–37 — the circuit, the standing, the sacrifice — could in principle be performed by a man who believes nothing about the end of the world, simply because his father performed them. The sūrah closes that option in its first verse. The worship it goes on to command is worship carried out inside a world that is going to be dismantled, and the dismantling is not a threat bolted onto the religion; it is the frame the religion is set in.

That is also why the sūrah ends where it does, with a summons to strive for God as He deserves (22:78) and a description of a community chosen and witnessed over. Between the earthquake of verse 1 and that summons, the sūrah builds a people who know what time they are living in.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:2

> The Day you see it, every nursing mother will abandon what she is nursing, and every pregnant woman will deliver her burden ˹prematurely˺. And you will see people ˹as if they were˺ drunk, though they will not be drunk; but the torment of Allah is ˹terribly˺ severe.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scale of the Terror Is a Human Scale**

Verse 1 called the quaking a dreadful thing and left the dread unspecified. Verse 2 supplies the measurement, and the measurement is not geological. Nothing here describes faults opening or buildings falling. What the verse describes is a mother letting go of her child.

That is how the Qur'an calibrates catastrophe throughout: by what it does to the bonds that ordinarily hold. The nursing mother is the figure in whom instinct and duty point the same direction; if that figure drops what she is holding, no social arrangement downstream of her survives either. The verse does not need to describe the ruined cities, because the ruin of a city is less alarming than the ruin of this.

**Drunk, and Yet Not Drunk**

The comparison is made and cancelled in a single sentence: you will see people as if they were drunk, though they will not be drunk. Staggering, slurred, uncomprehending — the outward signs of intoxication without any intoxicant. The cause is named in the clause that follows: the torment of God is severe.

The self-cancelling simile does real work. A description of drunkenness alone would have been read as a moral indictment, and the scene would have become a punishment for dissipation. A bare denial would have removed the image. Holding both at once keeps the scene in the register of terror rather than guilt: these people are not being punished for drinking; they are being unmade by fright, and fright can unmake anyone.

**Which Quaking? The Commentators Genuinely Differ**

The pronoun in "the Day you see it" has been referred to the quaking of verse 1 by most commentators, but the deeper disagreement concerns which event the quaking itself is. Three positions were recorded. Some take it as the portents that precede the end — the signs in the sky and the earth during the last age. Others take it as the destruction of the world itself. Still others place it on the Day of Resurrection, after the gathering has begun.

The stronger reading, in my judgment, is the second. The details point to a scene still inside time: pregnant women miscarrying and infants being abandoned belong to a moment at which bodies are still bodies and consequences still fall. Once the Resurrection is under way, miscarriage has no purchase — the dead are being raised, not born. The verse pictures the world ending while people are still doing ordinary things in it, which is what makes it frightful. The parallel passage in Sūrah al-Muzzammil describes the same moment with the mountains in motion: *“on the Day the earth and mountains will shake ˹violently˺, and mountains will be ˹reduced to˺ dunes of shifting sand”* (73:14).

**Why These Two Bodies**

The nursing mother and the pregnant woman are not chosen at random, and they are not a pair of examples of the same thing. The pregnant woman carries a future that has not yet arrived; the nursing mother sustains a life that has. Between them they cover the whole apparatus by which a generation hands itself on.

When the quaking comes, that apparatus is the first thing to fail. The verse is thus not only about fear but about a termination: what is interrupted is not merely individual life but succession. The end of the world in the Qur'an is never described as a slow dying out. It is an interruption, and interrupting the two bodies that carry continuity is the sharpest way to say so.

**A Note on the Participle**

The Arabic word for the nursing mother is *murḍiʿah*, the active participle — a woman who is *in the act* of nursing, not *mursiʿah*, one who has nursed in the past. The distinction is not pedantry. A woman who had finished nursing might forget her child for many ordinary reasons; the woman described here is at the breast when it happens.

The grammatical form therefore sharpens the point that the self-cancelling simile already made. This is not a lapse of character or a failure of affection. It is the strongest bond in the human animal, severed by an event large enough to sever it.

**"The Day You See It": When Seeing Arrives, Belief Is Too Late**

The Hour in this verse is something *seen* — *yawma tarawnahā*, the day you see it. Everything described in the verse is visual, and the seeing is placed at the front of the sentence before anything is described. The shift from hearing to sight is the shift the Qur'an marks as irreversible.

The principle is stated plainly elsewhere: *“On the Day your Lord’s signs arrive, belief will not benefit those who did not believe earlier or those who did no good through their faith”* (6:158). Faith is a response to what has not been seen, which is why the Qur'an calls the unseen its first article. Once the object is in front of the eyes, the act that was required becomes impossible — not forbidden, but impossible, because believing what you can see is not belief.

This gives the verse its urgency. It is not describing a spectacle that will be interesting to attend. It is describing the moment after which the thing that made a human being's life meaningful can no longer be performed. The nursing mother, the pregnant woman, the reeling crowd are all people for whom the time of decision has closed.

**The Last Clause Turns the Description Into an Address**

Up to the final clause, the verse has been a scene: women, children, staggering crowds. Then the register changes. "The torment of Allah is ˹terribly˺ severe" is not a detail of the scene; it is a statement to whoever is reading it, and it explains why the scene is being shown at all.

The Qur'an regularly breaks a description with an explanatory clause of this kind, and the effect is to prevent the reader from settling into spectatorship. The horrors are not tableaux for contemplation. They are *ʿadhāb* — torment — which means they are inflicted, which means there is a reason, and the reason is not chaos. Terror without a judge behind it would be tragedy; terror with a judge behind it is justice, and the verse insists on the second.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:3

> ˹Still˺ there are some who dispute about Allah without knowledge, and follow every rebellious devil.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Why the Argument Appears Next to the Earthquake**

The sūrah has just described the world coming apart, mothers dropping children and crowds reeling. Verse 3 turns, without transition, to a man holding an opinion: *wa-mina al-nāsi man yujādilu fī Allāhi bighayri ʿilmin* — and among the people is he who disputes about God without knowledge.

The abruptness is the connection. The Hour was announced in verses 1–2 as a fact; the fact has to be received or rejected; and the rejection is what verse 3 presents. Disputation about God is not a separate topic that happens to follow the eschatology. It is the form that rejecting the eschatology takes in a person who will not say plainly that he rejects it. The sūrah shows, in three verses, the sequence that runs through the whole Qur'an: the warning is given, the warning is argued with, and the arguing is what decides the man.

**The Indictment Names Ignorance, Not Error**

The charge is not that these people reach wrong conclusions. It is that their conclusions rest on nothing: *bighayri ʿilmin*, without knowledge. The Qur'an's complaint against its opponents is remarkably consistent on this point, and it is almost never that their reasoning fails. It is that the reasoning has no starting point — no revelation, no observation, no transmitted report — and therefore no way of being wrong, since a claim with no ground cannot be corrected.

This is why the criticism is sharpened in verse 8, where the same disputant is described as lacking three things at once: knowledge, guidance, and an illuminating scripture. The three are not synonyms. Knowledge is what a person can arrive at; guidance is what he is given; scripture is the external, checkable record. To have none of the three is to be arguing from taste.

**Who Is Meant: Two Candidates, and the Text Covers Both**

The commentators divide over the reference. One reading takes the verse as aimed at those who denied the Resurrection specifically — the people whose objection is preserved elsewhere in the Qur'an: *“When we are dead and reduced to dust and bones, will we really be resurrected?”* (37:16). On this reading the disputant here is the man of verses 1–2, the one who has just been told that the world ends and answers that bones do not come back.

The other reading, preferred by several later commentators, takes it as aimed at those who dismissed the revelation wholesale, of the sort reported saying: *“We have already heard ˹the recitation˺. If we wanted, we could have easily produced something similar. This ˹Quran˺ is nothing but ancient fables!”* (8:31).

The two readings need not be fought over. The phrasing is *wa-mina al-nāsi man* — and *among people* is one who — a partitive construction that describes a type rather than naming an individual. Some commentaries name particular men of Makkah as the occasion, though the wording is general and the sūrah treats the type as a recurring one. [UNVERIFIED: the specific ascription to named individuals in the occasion-of-revelation literature was not checked against a source in this session.]

**"Every Rebellious Devil": Shayṭān Is a Category, Not an Individual**

The disputant follows *kulla shayṭānin marīdin* — every rebellious devil. The indefinite, distributive "every" matters as much as the word itself. The Qur'an does not present one adversary with a plan; it presents a class of tempters to which a person can hand himself over wholesale.

*Shayṭān* in the Qur'an's usage covers the jinn who rebel, and it extends to human beings who play the same role. The word is built on a root conveying distance — the shayṭān is the one set at a distance from good, who works to set others at the same distance. A man who argues about God with no ground does not, on this verse, hold a private opinion. He has enrolled himself under whichever rebel happens to be calling, which is why the verse says *every* and not *a*.

**The Second Verb: Following**

The verse pairs ignorance with an activity: he disputes without knowledge *and follows* every rebellious devil. The two halves are cause and effect, and the order is fixed. A man without a ground for his belief does not become neutral; he becomes available.

*Yattabiʿu* is the verb of following, of walking behind. It is the same word used for following guidance, and that is the point. Every human being follows something. The question the verse poses is not whether a person is a follower — he is, by construction — but what he is walking behind. Deprived of knowledge, he does not stand still; he attaches himself to whichever rebel is nearest, and because the verse says *every* rebellious devil, the attachment is promiscuous rather than loyal.

The psychology here is exact. The person who has no way of testing a claim does not therefore withhold assent. He grants it more easily, and he grants it to the loudest voice. Ignorance in the Qur'an is not a vacuum; it is a liability that someone else will occupy.

**The Hidden Positive: Disputation With Knowledge Is Something Else**

The critics of this verse, from al-Qurṭubī to al-Rāzī, drew the inference the wording invites. If what is condemned is disputing *without knowledge*, then disputing with knowledge is a different act, and the Qur'an itself commands its opposite number — calling to the way of God on the basis of what has actually been revealed.

The distinction protects theology as a discipline. The verse is not a prohibition on argument about God; it is a prohibition on argument about God from nothing. What separates the two is not tone or politeness but sources: the man who has revelation, reasoning and a checkable text has somewhere to be corrected from, and the man in this verse has nowhere.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:4

> It has been decreed for such devils that whoever takes them as a guide will be misguided and led by them into the torment of the Blaze.

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Has Been Decreed, Precisely**

*Kutiba ʿalayhi* — it has been written for him, decreed for him. The verb carries the sense of a settled inscription, and the verse then states what the inscription says: whoever takes him as a guide, he misguides him and leads him to the torment of the Blaze.

Notice the shape of what is decreed. The decree is not a list of names. It is a conditional sentence: whoever does X, Y follows. What God has written is the *connection* between taking a devil as patron and ending in the Fire, not the identity of the people who will take one. The verse predestines a law, not a population.

That distinction is the whole answer to the objection the verse invites. If the decree were a roster of the damned, patience and effort would be theatre. As a conditional, it is the opposite: it is the strongest possible warning, because it tells the reader exactly which act carries which consequence and leaves the act to him.

**Two Antecedents for One Pronoun**

The pronoun in "it has been decreed for him" is grammatically available to two referents, and the commentators split accordingly.

The first reading takes it as the devil of the previous verse: it is decreed for every rebellious devil that he will misguide whoever follows him. The clause that follows then reads smoothly — "whoever takes him as a guide" refers back to the same devil, and the subject of "he misguides him" is the devil throughout.

The second reading takes it as the follower: it is decreed for the person who follows a devil that he will be misguided. This yields a harder, more predestinarian sense, and it is recorded by al-Rāzī among others.

The stronger reading is the first. The nearest antecedent in verse 3 is *shayṭānin marīdin*, the rebellious devil, and the pronoun in "takes him (*hu*) as a guide" has to be the devil for the sentence to make sense at all. Readings that make the follower the subject of the decree must supply that reference across a clause boundary, and they sit awkwardly beside verses that place the choice squarely with the human being: *“˹This is˺ the truth from your Lord. Whoever wills let them believe, and whoever wills let them disbelieve”* (18:29). The coherence of the Qur'an's moral language is a real argument here, and it favours the reading that keeps the decree a law rather than a list.

**The Irony in the Word "Guide"**

The verb used for leading to the Fire is from *h-d-y*, the same root that gives *hudā*, guidance. The devil "guides" his follower — to the torment of the Blaze.

The Qur'an uses this irony repeatedly and always to the same point: guidance is not a feeling of being led somewhere confidently. It is a destination. A man who is certain, directed, and utterly sure of his path has been guided in the verbal sense while being misguided in the sense that counts. The verse refuses to let *hudā* be judged by its manner. It is judged by where it ends — and the Blaze is where this one ends.

**Patronage: a Word Borrowed From the Marketplace**

The verb translated "takes as a guide" is *tawallāhu*, from the same root as *walī*. In pre-Islamic Arabia a *walī* was a protector — the man or clan to whom you attached yourself for safety, who answered for your blood and whose standing covered you. Clientage was a real institution with real consequences, and everyone hearing this verse understood the transaction.

Using that vocabulary for a spiritual relationship is a deliberate transfer. The disputant of verse 3 is not merely persuaded by a devil; he has entered into an arrangement with one, trading his dependence for protection. The Qur'an's point is that the arrangement is recognized — it is not a metaphor for influence but a description of who the man now belongs to.

What makes the transfer cutting is that clientage was entered into for safety, and this clientage delivers the opposite. The follower takes a protector because he wants cover, and what the protector delivers is the Blaze. Patronage that kills the client is not patronage at all, which is why the verse's conditional is so severe: the law it decrees is that protection sought outside God reverses itself.

**What the Devil Himself Will Say**

The relationship described here is a relationship of patronage: the man takes the devil as *walī*, a protecting friend and manager of his affairs. The arrangement is presented as if it were a contract with benefits.

The Qur'an lets the other party describe it later, after judgment has been passed, in words that undo the contract entirely: *“I did not have any authority over you. I only called you, and you responded to me. So do not blame me; blame yourselves”* (14:22). The patron of verse 4 turns out to have had no power to deliver anything, and his own testimony is that the follower walked in on his own feet.

Read together, the two verses settle the question of coercion from both ends. The decree of verse 4 guarantees that the path leads where it leads; the confession of 14:22 establishes that nobody was dragged along it.

**Fire as a Destination Rather Than a Mood**

The closing phrase is *ʿadhāba al-saʿīr* — the torment of the Blaze. *Saʿīr* is kindled fire, fire set going, and it is the Fire's most common proper name in the Qur'an's eschatological vocabulary.

The precision defuses a modern misreading. The Qur'an is not describing a state of mind or a absence; it is describing a place with a temperature, reached by a route. The verse's conditional structure and its concrete destination belong together: a law that connects an act to an end, and an end that is stated as plainly as the act.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:5

> O humanity! If you are in doubt about the Resurrection, then ˹know that˺ We did create you from dust, then from a sperm-drop, then ˹developed you into˺ a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then a lump of flesh —fully formed or unformed—in order to demonstrate ˹Our power˺ to you. ˹Then˺ We settle whatever ˹embryo˺ We will in the womb for an appointed term, then bring you forth as infants, so that you may reach your prime. Some of you ˹may˺ die ˹young˺, while others are left to reach the most feeble stage of life so that they may know nothing after having known much. And you see the earth lifeless, but as soon as We send down rain upon it, it begins to stir ˹to life˺ and swell, producing every type of pleasant plant.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Doubt Is Answered, Not Rebuked**

The verse opens by granting the doubt a name: *in kuntum fī raybin mina al-baʿthi* — if you are in doubt about the Resurrection. It does not call the doubter a liar or a rebel. It offers him an argument and asks him to follow it.

This is the Qur'an's standard procedure with the doctrine that its audience found hardest. Elsewhere the objection is quoted and answered with a comparison; here it is answered with something the objector already accepts — his own origin. The strategy is worth naming, because it is not the strategy of demanding assent. A person who doubts the second creation is asked to inspect the first one, which he has no grounds to doubt since it happened to him.

**Four Stages, and the Sequence Is the Argument**

The stages are given in order: dust, then a sperm-drop, then a clinging clot, then a lump of flesh. The parallel passage in Sūrah al-Muʾminūn lays out the same sequence with the bones made explicit: *“then We developed the drop into a clinging clot ˹of blood˺, then developed the clot into a lump ˹of flesh˺, then developed the lump into bones, then clothed the bones with flesh, then We brought it into being as a new creation. So Blessed is Allah, the Best of Creators”* (23:14).

Why the sequence matters more than the stages: each step is a transformation of the material preceding it, and none of them is explicable by the material alone. Dust does not arrange itself into a drop; a drop does not become a clot by chemistry the drop possesses. The Qur'an's point is not that the stages are unusual — every human being passes through them — but that they are ordinary and yet unaccountable. A God who brings a person through four such transitions has no difficulty with a fifth.

That the first stage is dust rather than nothing is also part of the argument. The Qur'an frequently pairs the two: *“He created him from dust, then said to him, ‘Be!’ And he was!”* (3:59). Creation from a substance is presented as the harder and stranger case, since something is being shaped; the Resurrection is described in this sūrah as the revival of what has already been shaped once.

**"Formed or Unformed" — the Lump That Decides**

*Mukhallaqatin wa-ghayri mukhallaqatin* — formed and unformed. The phrase describes the lump of flesh, and it has been read in two ways that are really one. Grammatically it qualifies the lump: the flesh at that stage may go on to be fully shaped, or it may not, and the difference is not visible at the time.

The theological weight sits in the clause that follows, about what God settles in the wombs for a stated term. Miscarriage is here placed inside the same decree as birth. The verse does not treat the unformed pregnancy as a failure of the creative process but as one of the two appointed outcomes of it, both decided by the One who decides. Commentators noted that this is why the phrase is attached to the lump specifically — the stage at which the outcome is still hidden from everyone but its Author.

**Two Endings for One Life: Death, or the Feeble Stage**

From birth the verse moves to maturity and then splits the road. Some die; others are returned to "the most feeble stage of life so that they may know nothing after having known much."

The second ending is the one the Qur'an dwells on, and it is a harder thing to contemplate than death. Sūrah al-Naḥl states it with the same words: *“And some of you are left to reach the most feeble stage of life so that they may know nothing after having known much. Indeed, Allah is All-Knowing, Most Capable”* (16:70). Sūrah Yā Sīn names the mechanism: *“And whoever We grant a long life, We reverse them in development. Will they not then understand?”* (36:68).

The choice of this as the second proof of the Resurrection is deliberate. A person who has watched a mind undo itself has watched a Creator take back what He gave, in the same body, over the same lifetime. If knowledge can be withdrawn from a living brain, it can be restored to a decayed one. The arc of weakness to strength and back again is drawn explicitly elsewhere: *“It is Allah Who created you in a state of weakness, then developed ˹your˺ weakness into strength, then developed ˹your˺ strength into weakness and old age”* (30:54).

**From the Womb to the Field**

The argument then leaves the body and takes up the ground: you see the earth lifeless, and when We send down water it stirs, swells, and puts out every kind of pleasant growth.

This is the Qur'an's single most repeated analogy for the Resurrection, and the repetition is not decorative. Sūrah al-Rūm states the conclusion in the same breath as the observation: *“See then the impact of Allah’s mercy: how He gives life to the earth after its death! Surely That ˹same Creator˺ can raise the dead. For He is Most Capable of everything”* (30:50). Sūrah al-Aʿrāf draws the line explicitly: *“Similarly, We will bring the dead to life, so perhaps you will be mindful”* (7:57).

The analogy works because both halves are the same kind of event: something dry, dead, and dispersed receives what it cannot produce for itself and becomes a living, ordered thing. The earth does not rain on itself. Neither does a grave.

**"For an Appointed Term": the Report Behind the Phrase**

On the appointed term in the womb, a ḥadīth of ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd is central to the commentary tradition. The Prophet ﷺ said that each person is gathered in his mother's womb for forty days as a drop, becomes a clot for a like period, then a lump of flesh for a like period, after which an angel is sent to write four things — his provision, his lifespan, his deeds, and whether he will be wretched or blessed — and then the soul is breathed into him. It is recorded in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (3208) and *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (2643).

The report and the verse illuminate each other. Where the verse says only that God settles what He wills for a stated term, the ḥadīth fills in the term's texture — a fixed span, known and written, inside which a life is shaped. Nothing in the report suggests the span is arbitrary; it is measured, and the One who measures it is the One who will end it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:6

> That is because Allah ˹alone˺ is the Truth, He ˹alone˺ gives life to the dead, and He ˹alone˺ is Most Capable of everything.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That" Points Back at the Whole Argument**

*Dhālika bi-anna* — that is because. The demonstrative does not refer to the last clause of verse 5 alone. It gathers the entire demonstration: the dust, the drop, the clot, the lump, the womb, the birth, the two endings of a life, and the field that greens when water reaches it.

The grammatical point is worth making because it is the verse's structure. Verse 5 gave evidence; verse 6 gives the principle that makes the evidence evidence. Without verse 6, verse 5 is a list of remarkable natural processes. With it, the list becomes a proof, because a proof needs a premise connecting the observed fact to the conclusion drawn — and here the premise is stated three times over, in three different forms.

**The First Premise: God Is the Truth**

*Al-Ḥaqq* is one of the divine names, and its semantic range is wider than "truthful." It means the Real, that which truly is, that which does not fail or dissolve. Applied to God, it sets up the contrast that verse 62 of this same sūrah makes explicit: God is the Truth and what is invoked besides Him is falsehood.

The relevance to the Resurrection is direct. If the source of all being is itself perfectly real — not derivative, not contingent, not subject to decay — then what it produces is not a trick of appearances. A world made by *al-Ḥaqq* is a world in which acts have weight and promises are kept. Denying the Resurrection, on this premise, is not an intellectual error about biology; it is a failure to take the reality of the Real seriously enough to suppose that He means what He says.

The Qur'an makes the same move when it grounds judgment in the divine attribute rather than in a threat: punishment is not vindictiveness, it is reality catching up.

**The Second Premise: He Gives Life to the Dead**

The second clause narrows from metaphysics to the specific power in question. God gives life to the dead — *yuḥyī al-mawtā*. The verb is in the present tense, denoting what He does, not only what He will do once at the end.

That present tense is the argument. The objector grants that God gives life in the ordinary course — every pregnancy, every spring. What he denies is the exceptional case. The verse collapses the distinction: the One who gives life gives life, and the dead He revives include both the field in autumn and the person in the grave. Sūrah al-Rūm states the inference in so many words: *“See then the impact of Allah’s mercy: how He gives life to the earth after its death! Surely That ˹same Creator˺ can raise the dead”* (30:50).

**The Third Premise: Power Over Everything**

The third clause is the widest: *wa-annahu ʿalā kulli shayʾin qadīr* — and He is over everything capable. If the first two premises establish that God is real and that He is the giver of life, the third removes the last refuge of the doubter, which is to grant the general power while denying the particular instance.

Three premises, in ascending order of generality, is a deliberate rhetorical shape. The argument in verse 5 moved from the particular to the general — one embryo, then one field. The argument in verse 6 moves from the general to the absolute — God's reality, His life-giving, His power. The reader is walked from something he can inspect with his hands to something he can only affirm.

**The Logic of the Greater and the Smaller**

Underlying the whole passage is an inferential form the Qur'an deploys repeatedly in this context and always in the same direction: the harder creation is the one already granted, so the easier one cannot reasonably be denied. *“Can the One Who created the heavens and the earth not ˹easily˺ resurrect these ˹deniers˺?” Yes ˹He can˺! For He is the Master Creator, All-Knowing”* (36:81). The same form closes Sūrah al-Qiyāmah: *“Is such ˹a Creator˺ unable to bring the dead back to life?”* (75:40).

The form works because it uses the opponent's own concession. No one in the Qur'an's audience argued that the heavens were self-created; the argument was about the Resurrection alone. The Qur'an simply points out that the disputed case is the smaller one, and that a person who accepts the larger while rejecting the smaller is not reasoning at all — he is protecting a conclusion.

**A Demonstrative of Distance**

The verse opens with *dhālika* — that, the demonstrative used for what is far away rather than *hādhā*, this. The grammarians observed that *dhālika* is regularly used in the Qur'an for things God alone fully grasps: revelation, the Hereafter, the divine attributes.

The choice is not decoration. The Resurrection is a thing the reader must hold at a distance before he can hold it at all, because his own experience of death gives him no purchase on it. The far demonstrative keeps the object of the argument properly large. A near demonstrative would have brought the Resurrection down to the scale of the evidence, and the evidence — a womb, a field — is what is near. The conclusion is not.

This also explains why the verse repeats the particle *anna* with each clause: *because* God is the Truth, *and because* He gives life to the dead, *and because* He is capable of everything. Three separate causal phrases rather than one compound sentence. Each is a complete reason on its own, so that a reader who stumbles at the metaphysics of the first may still be held by the observation of the second or the plain assertion of the third.

**Why the Verse Ends Without a Threat**

The Resurrection has now been argued from embryology, from ageing, from agriculture and from the divine attributes. There is no threat attached.

That silence is consistent with the sūrah's opening move in verse 5, which granted the doubt rather than punishing it. The audience is being reasoned with, and the reasoning is complete in itself. The warnings in this sūrah fall on people who have already heard the argument — verse 9 will describe the man who turns away *after* the signs have reached him. The sequence matters: proof first, then the verdict, and never the verdict alone.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:7

> And certainly the Hour is coming, there is no doubt about it. And Allah will surely resurrect those in the graves.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Is Grammatically the Tail of the One Before It**

The Arabic of verse 7 is a continuation: *wa-anna al-sāʿata ātiyatun lā rayba fīhā* — and *because* the Hour is coming, no doubt in it. The same causal particle *anna* that opened all three clauses of verse 6 opens this one. In the grammar, verses 6 and 7 are a single sentence: that is because God is the Truth, because He gives life to the dead, because He has power over everything, and because the Hour is coming.

So the Resurrection is not added to the argument after the argument is finished. It is the fourth premise, listed in the same breath as the divine attributes. The effect is to make the Hour a matter of what God *is* rather than a matter of what will happen on a schedule. A God who is *al-Ḥaqq*, who gives life, who is capable of everything, is a God before whom graves are not a difficulty. Denying the Hour on this reading is not an error about the future; it is a mismatch in one's account of God.

**Where the Argument Stops and the Assertion Begins**

Verses 5 and 6 argued. Verse 7 stops arguing and asserts: the Hour is coming, there is no doubt in it.

The move is characteristic and worth naming, because it looks like a gap and is not. Reasoning brings a person as far as the conclusion that the Resurrection is coherent and more than coherent — grounded in what he already accepts about embryos, fields and the divine power. Reasoning cannot supply certainty about a future event, and the Qur'an does not pretend otherwise. It supplies the assertion instead, and it supplies it after the reasoning so that the assertion does not have to do the reasoning's work.

This ordering protects both halves. Without verse 5, verse 7 would be an unargued claim. Without verse 7, verse 5 would be an interesting observation about biology.

**"No Doubt in It" Removes the Doubt, Not the Difficulty**

*Lā rayba fīhi* — no doubt in it. The phrase is used in the Qur'an's opening for the Book itself, and its transfer to the Hour is a deliberate equivalence. The Book and the Hour are the two things the Qur'an describes this way, and the shared formula ties them together: the text that cannot be doubted announces the event that cannot be doubted.

The negation also tells us where the doubt actually sits. It is not said that the Hour is easy to conceive, or that the mind finds it natural. What is denied is that the doubt has an object. A person who doubts the Hour is not responding to a genuine uncertainty in the evidence; he is responding to something else entirely, which the sūrah named two verses earlier as disputing without knowledge (22:3).

**"Those in the Graves"**

The second assertion is *wa-anna Allāha yabʿathu man fī al-qubūri* — God will raise those who are in the graves. The word is *qubūr*, the ordinary plural for graves, not a poetic term for the underworld.

That plainness is the point. The Qur'an's claim is not about a general immortality of the soul or a survival of influence; it is about the contents of specific holes in the ground. Sūrah al-ʿĀdiyāt puts the same vision in a single line: *“Do they not know that when the contents of the graves will be spilled out”* (100:9). Sūrah Fāṭir uses the same phrase to mark the limit of the Prophet's own mission: *“Indeed, Allah ˹alone˺ makes whoever He wills hear, but you ˹O Prophet˺ can never make those in the graves hear ˹your call˺”* (35:22).

The pairing of the two texts is instructive. Human calling stops at the grave; the divine raising begins there. The same bodies that cannot hear a prophet will hear the summons that ends the world, and the verse in front of us is the promise that it will happen.

**The Hour Is Coming in the Present Tense**

*Ātiyatun* is an active participle: coming, arriving, on its way. Not *will come* in the flat future sense, but *is coming* — already in motion toward its appointment.

The grammatical form does the work that a whole paragraph of exhortation would otherwise have to do. A thing on its way has a direction and a clock; the interval between this sentence and that arrival is not an open-ended postponement but a distance being covered. The sūrah that opened with the world shaking apart now says that the shaking is in transit.

**Doubt Is Traced as a Condition Throughout This Sūrah**

The word *rayb* does not appear here by accident. It is the same noun the sūrah used in verse 5, when it addressed people *in doubt* about the Resurrection, and it will return at verse 55, where the disbelievers remain in doubt *“until the Hour takes them by surprise, or the torment of a terminating Day comes to them”* (22:55).

Read together, the three occurrences describe a progression rather than a repeated word. In verse 5 the doubt is a starting point that can be argued with and is argued with, at length. In verse 7 the doubt is denied any object: there is nothing in it. By verse 55 the doubt has become permanent, and the thing that ends it is not an argument but the arrival of the very event that was doubted.

That is the sūrah's diagnosis, and it is more precise than a simple condemnation of scepticism. Doubt, in this account, is not the absence of evidence but a settled posture that evidence does not move. The difference between the audience of verse 5 and the people of verse 55 is not that one group had better arguments. It is that one group was still listening.

**Resurrection Precedes Judgment in the Sūrah's Order**

Notice where this verse falls in the sūrah's architecture. The court scenes — the garments of fire, the boiling water, the maces of iron — do not appear until verses 19–22, and the great judgment passage is reserved for verses 56–57. The Resurrection is established first, on its own, with no description of what follows it.

The order is a teaching decision. Judgment is only dreadful to someone who expects to stand in it, which requires first believing that the dead are raised. A sūrah that opened its judgment scenes before it established the Resurrection would be describing a courtroom to people who do not believe they will attend it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:8

> ˹Still˺ there are some who dispute about Allah without knowledge, guidance, or an enlightening scripture,

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Portrait, and Two Words Heavier**

Verse 3 described a man who disputes about God without knowledge and follows every rebellious devil. Verse 8 describes him again — and the second portrait is heavier than the first by two nouns. What was *bighayri ʿilmin* has become *bighayri ʿilmin wa-lā hudan wa-lā kitābin munīr*: without knowledge, and without guidance, and without an illuminating scripture.

The escalation is not padding. Verse 3 established the defect in the man's reasoning; verse 8 establishes that he has no resources of any kind behind the reasoning either. A person can be ignorant and still be well-directed, or be ignorant of the details and still hold a reliable text. The verse removes all three in a single line, and the triple negation is the strongest form available in Arabic for a total absence.

**Three Negations Cover the Three Ways of Knowing**

The three terms are not synonyms, and the care with which they are chosen is the substance of the verse.

*ʿIlm* is knowledge arrived at — what a person works out, observes, or is taught and verifies. *Hudā* is guidance received — direction given from outside oneself, the thing a person is granted rather than achieves. *Kitāb munīr* is an illuminating scripture: a written, public, checkable source that gives light and can be consulted by anyone.

Together they exhaust the field. If a person lacks what he can reason out, what he has been given, and what he can look up, there is no fourth category left to appeal to. The verse is therefore not merely an insult; it is a criterion. It tells the reader what a defensible position about God requires, and by negation it defines what legitimate theology is.

**Why Guidance Is Listed Separately From Knowledge**

The second negation is the one most easily skimmed past. If a man lacks knowledge, what does it add to say that he also lacks guidance?

It adds a judgement about his state rather than his information. Knowledge in the Qur'an is something a person can hold, lose, or refuse; guidance is something granted, and its absence is not a neutral gap but a privation. A man without knowledge is uninformed. A man without guidance is unreached. The verse says both, which means the disputant's condition is not simply that he has never been taught — it is that the teaching has not landed on him.

The sequence also rules out a comfortable reading. A person might concede that his knowledge is thin while assuming his intentions are sound and that God will supply the rest. Listing *hudā* as a second, separate absence forecloses that. Ignorance plus sincerity would still leave a man with guidance, and this verse describes a man who has neither.

**"Enlightening Scripture": One Word Doing Two Jobs**

*Munīr* is the active participle of *anāra*, to shine or give light. It describes something that is itself luminous and, at the same time, something that illuminates what is around it. Both senses are in play.

The adjective is rare in the Qur'an and appears in a narrow set of contexts, two of which concern earlier revelation. Messengers before Muhammad ﷺ came *“with clear proofs, divine Books, and enlightening Scriptures”* (3:184), and the same formula is repeated for the peoples who denied them: *“Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, divine Books, and enlightening Scriptures”* (35:25).

The implication for this verse is sharp. The disputant is being measured against a standard that previous communities were given and met, or were given and rejected. Illumination is not withheld for want of a lamp; it is available, in the form of a text that both shines and shows. The man in verse 8 has walked past a working light.

**Why the Sūrah Returns to This Man**

Repetition of this kind is often read as editing, and it is not. The sūrah names this figure twice within six verses because the figure is not one man with a name. The construction is *wa-mina al-nāsi man* — and among people is one who — a partitive that describes a standing type rather than an individual.

The two appearances also do different work, which is why the repetition is not a duplication. In verse 3, the disputant follows devils, and the emphasis falls on where his opinion comes from. In verse 8 the emphasis falls on what he lacks, and the verse that follows — verse 9 — will say what he *does*: he turns aside in pride to lead others away. The sūrah is moving through a sequence, and the second portrait belongs to the middle of it.

**The Remedy Implied by the Diagnosis**

A verse that lists three missing things is implicitly a syllabus. Knowledge is to be acquired, guidance is to be sought, and the scripture is to be read — and all three are available.

This is why the classical commentators drew a positive ruling out of a verse of condemnation. If the fault is disputing *without* these, then disputing *with* them is a different act, and one the Qur'an commands elsewhere in the strongest terms: calling to the way of God on the basis of what has actually been revealed, with wisdom and fair argument. The dispute the Qur'an forbids is the one conducted from preference; the dispute it commands is the one conducted from a source the other party can also consult.

The practical test the verse leaves behind is unusually simple. Before arguing about God, a person should be able to say what he knows, what he has been given, and which text he is reading.

Luqmān's Version of the Same Line

The same indictment appears in Sūrah Luqmān, addressed to a man who cannot name what he is relying on: *“Have you not seen that Allah has subjected for you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, and has lavished His favours upon you, both seen and unseen? ˹Still˺ there are some who dispute about Allah without knowledge, or guidance, or an enlightening scripture”* (31:20). There the dispute is set against the backdrop of visible provision, which makes the ignorance harder to excuse: the evidence is in front of the man, and he argues anyway.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:9

> turning away ˹in pride˺ to lead ˹others˺ astray from Allah’s Way. They will suffer disgrace in this world, and on the Day of Judgment We will make them taste the torment of burning.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Body Language of Arrogance**

*Thāniya ʿiṭfihi* — turning his side. The phrase is a physical description before it is a moral one: a man bending his neck, presenting his side, turning his shoulder toward something he will not face. The Qur'an uses the gesture for contempt because contempt has a posture, and everyone who has watched a person dismiss an argument knows it.

The translators render it as pride, and that is right, but the vocabulary carries more than an internal attitude. What is described is a body turned away while a text is being recited. The man is not weighing the claim and rejecting it; he has physically arranged himself so that it cannot reach him, and he has done it in public.

**From Error to Mission**

Verses 3 and 8 described a man who disputes. Verse 9 says what the disputation becomes: *li-yuḍilla ʿan sabīli Allāh* — to lead others astray from God's path. The little particle *li-* marks purpose. He is not mistaken and therefore, incidentally, misleading. Leading astray is what he is doing it for.

The shift is theologically graded, and the Qur'an grades it consistently. Holding a wrong view is one thing; working to make others hold it is another, and the second carries what the first does not: the corrosion is now being transmitted. A private error dies with the man who made it. A propagated one keeps working after he is in his grave, which is part of why the verse's punishment is doubled.

**Disgrace in This World, Then Burning in the Next**

*Khizyun fī al-dunyā* — disgrace in the present life — followed by the torment of burning on the Day of Resurrection. The two are not alternatives and not a sequence of coincidence. The Qur'an pairs them as a standard form wherever it describes organized opposition to the revelation.

Those who prevent God's name from being mentioned in His places of worship are told: *“For them is disgrace in this world, and they will suffer a tremendous punishment in the Hereafter”* (2:114). The same pairing is given to those who wage war against God and His messenger (5:33), to those whose tongues believe while their hearts do not (5:41), and in the plain statement of Sūrah al-Zumar: *“So Allah made them taste humiliation in this worldly life, but far worse is the punishment of the Hereafter, if only they knew”* (39:26).

The regularity of the formula answers the question a reader might raise. Is the disgrace in this world the real punishment, with the Hereafter an afterthought? No: the pattern is always that the first is partial and the second is the thing itself.

**Disgrace Here Can Be Lifted; the Other Cannot**

That said, disgrace in this life is not fixed in the way the punishment of the next is fixed, and the Qur'an says so. Sūrah Yūnus singles out the one community in history whose belated belief changed their earthly sentence: *“If only there had been a society which believed ˹before seeing the torment˺ and, therefore, benefited from its belief, like the people of Jonah. When they believed, We lifted from them the torment of disgrace in this world and allowed them enjoyment for a while”* (10:98).

The exception proves the rule and explains the logic. Humiliation in this world is a warning delivered inside a period when warnings still work; the punishment of the Hereafter is delivered after that period has closed. This is the same principle the sūrah established four verses earlier with the Hour that is *coming* (22:7): there is an interval, and the interval is the point.

**"We Will Make Them Taste"**

The verb is *nudhīquhu*, We will make them taste. The Qur'an's vocabulary for punishment is overwhelmingly sensory, and tasting is among its most frequent choices.

Taste is the sense that cannot be refused at a distance. You can decline to look at something and you can stop your ears, but a taste on the tongue is registered whether or not it was wanted, and it is registered completely in an instant. The word also carries a portion-size: one *tastes* a small amount of what one will then receive in full.

Sūrah Fuṣṣilat uses the same verb for the same double sentence: *“So We sent against them a furious wind, for ˹several˺ miserable days, to make them taste a humiliating punishment in this worldly life. But far more humiliating will be the punishment of the Hereafter”* (41:16). The early taste is a sample, and the sample is meant to be read as a sample.

**A Path, Not a Position**

The object of the man's campaign is *sabīli Allāh* — the way of God. The word is *sabīl*, a road. The Qur'an's standing image for the religion is not a set of propositions but a route, and the verb used for what the man does to others — *yuḍilla*, to cause to stray — is the verb for losing a road.

The image carries a specific implication about method. You cannot refute a road; you can only walk someone off it. Misleading in the Qur'an is therefore rarely pictured as a superior argument winning a debate. It is pictured as distraction, delay, and the offer of an alternative route that looks shorter. The man in this verse is not described as out-arguing anyone; he is described as turning his own shoulder while making sure other people's shoulders turn too.

That is why the offence is measured by its effects on others rather than by the content of his view. A wrong opinion held quietly is a private loss. Turning people off a road is an act with victims, and the verse's doubled sentence — disgrace here, burning hereafter — matches the doubled nature of the harm: he ruined his own direction and worked to ruin other people's.

**Why the Verse Names the Effect Before the Cause**

One last feature of the ordering. The verse states what the man does — turning aside to mislead — and then states his sentence, without pausing to say why the sentence is just. That is what verse 10 supplies. Here the emphasis falls entirely on the act, and the reader is left with the picture of a man with his shoulder turned, working to make sure other people's shoulders are turned as well.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:10

> ˹They will be told,˺ “This is ˹the reward˺ for what your hands have done. And Allah is never unjust to ˹His˺ creation.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**These Words Are Spoken to a Face**

The verse is not a description of judgment; it is a line of dialogue inside it. The translation supplies *they will be told*, and the Arabic makes the same move by putting the sentence in direct speech: *dhālika bimā qaddamat yadāka* — that is for what your two hands sent ahead.

The shift from third person to direct address is doing real work. Everything before this has been about them; this is said *to* them. A verdict pronounced in someone's absence can be disputed, appealed, or ignored. A verdict pronounced into a person's face, at the moment of his standing, closes the matter — and the Qur'an stages the closing rather than reporting it.

**"What Your Hands Sent Ahead"**

The idiom is worth taking apart, because it has been worn smooth by repetition. *Qaddamat yadāka* — what your hands put forward. Deeds are not described as accumulated, weighed, or remembered; they are described as *shipped*.

Two ideas sit inside the metaphor. The first is that a deed is a transfer: a person does something here, and the thing itself arrives there. The second is that the sender does not travel with the shipment and cannot alter it after dispatch. What arrives at the judgment is not a report about the man but the man's own consignment, and he meets it as cargo he packed.

The wording is nearly identical to a verse in Sūrah Āl ʿImrān, which is why the two are always read together: *“This is ˹the reward˺ for what your hands have done. And Allah is never unjust to ˹His˺ creation”* (3:182). There the sentence is addressed in exactly the same form, and the repetition across two sūrahs confirms that this is a fixed formula of the judgment rather than a description tailored to one group.

**"Hands" Specifically**

The dual *yadāka*, your two hands, is not a synonym for the self. Hands are the instruments by which a person changes the world, and the Qur'an's use of them for attribution is precise: what counts at the judgment is what a person *did*, in the concrete sense, with the parts of himself that act.

This matters for how the verse is read. The disputant of verses 3 and 8 was condemned for what he said, and the sentence he receives is for what his hands sent ahead. Speech in the Qur'an's accounting is a deed — it is produced by a body, in a place, with effects — and the idiom keeps that connection visible.

**The Clause That Carries the Verse**

The second half is the one the verse exists for: *wa-anna Allāha laysa bi-ẓallāmin li-l-ʿabīd* — and God is not unjust to His servants.

Note what is being denied. Not that the punishment is severe — the previous verse called it burning. What is denied is that it is *unearned*. The sentence is embedded in the announcement of the sentence, said at the worst possible moment for the person hearing it, and it is said first-person plural by the One pronouncing it.

The Qur'an's insistence on this point at the moment of punishment is not defensive. It is the answer to the objection that every punished person makes, and the Qur'an records the objection being made elsewhere — precisely so that the answer can be seen to address it.

**Why the Record Is Outside the Person**

The machinery behind "what your hands sent ahead" is the record, and the Qur'an is unusually concrete about it. *“And We have everything recorded precisely”* (78:29). At the judgment the record is produced as an object: *“Read your record. You ˹alone˺ are sufficient this Day to take account of yourself”* (17:14).

The external record is what makes the second clause of this verse meaningful. If judgment were a matter of divine recollection, the claim of justice would rest on the reliability of a memory; the Qur'an instead places the account outside both parties, legible to the accused, and has him read it himself. Sūrah al-Kahf describes the moment: *“And the record ˹of deeds˺ will be laid ˹open˺, and you will see the wicked in fear of what is ˹written˺ in it. They will cry, ‘Woe to us! What kind of record is this that does not leave any sin, small or large, unlisted?’”* (18:49).

The person holding the record is the only one who can dispute its contents, and the verse reports what he says instead: not a denial, but an admission of how complete it is.

**"Servants" — the Word That Carries the Argument**

The final word is *li-l-ʿabīd*, to the servants. Not to people, not to humanity, not to creatures: to those who belong to Him.

The choice is an argument in itself, and the classical theologians took it that way. Injustice is not a random defect; it has motives. A person wrongs another because he wants what the other has, or because he fears him, or because he does not know what he is doing. None of the three can apply to the Owner of everything toward what He already possesses. Wrongdoing requires a lack, and the one lacking does not wrong; the one who lacks nothing has no reason to.

Stated in that form, the claim is not consoling poetry but a deduction from the divine attribute the sūrah has already asserted three times over in verse 6 — that God is the Truth, that He gives life, that He has power over everything. A being of that description has no deficit for injustice to fill.

The word also protects the verse against its own misuse. Saying that God is never unjust to His servants is not saying that whatever happens to a person is fair by definition; it is saying that the verdict in front of him was produced by his own shipment, examined against his own record, and read out by his own account. The servanthood is what makes the justice legible rather than merely asserted.

**The Two Halves Together**

Put the two halves side by side and the verse is a complete theory of punishment in one line. The first half says that what is happening to this person was authored by him — the shipment is his. The second half says that the one inflicting it owes him no grievance. Responsibility and justice, stated in that order, and stated to his face.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:11

> And there are some who worship Allah on the verge ˹of faith˺: if they are blessed with something good, they are content with it; but if they are afflicted with a trial, they relapse ˹into disbelief˺, losing this world and the Hereafter. That is ˹truly˺ the clearest loss.

**Expanded Commentary**

**On a Brink: the Geography of Half-Faith**

The Arabic says he worships God *ʿalā ḥarfin* — on an edge. The word *ḥarf* means the edge or border of a thing, and it is the same word used for the edge of a cliff and, by an easy extension, for a letter of the alphabet, the edge of a sound. The man is standing on the boundary of the religion, not inside it.

That spatial image is the whole diagnosis, and it is worth unfolding. A person on a brink has not left; he is still technically on the same ground as those who entered. But he has positioned himself where the smallest movement tips him off, and because he is on an edge, nothing about his position is stable. He is one shove from being outside, and he chose to stand there.

The preposition does more work than a description of weakness would. This is not a man whose faith is small; it is a man whose faith has a location, and the location is external. He has taken up a position relative to the religion rather than letting the religion take up a position in him.

**The Test Runs in Both Directions**

The verse then gives the mechanics, and they are symmetrical in form and asymmetrical in outcome. If good comes to him, he is content with it. If a trial strikes him, he flips over onto his face.

The symmetry is in the events: blessing and trial are the ordinary alternations of any human life, and both are described as things that *befall* him, not things he brings about. The asymmetry is in his response. Contentment with the good is not called faith — it is called *iṭmaʾanna*, being at ease, a settling. The trial produces *inqalaba ʿalā wajhih*, a turning over onto the face, the posture of a man who has fallen.

The wording exposes the transaction. What he wanted was ease, and he was willing to accept God as the supplier of it. When the supply stopped, so did the acceptance. His contentment was never in the Giver; it was in the gift, and the verse reports that he was content *with it* — with the thing, not with the One who sent it.

**Al-Ṭabarī's Bedouins, and the Wider Reading**

Two readings of the reference are recorded. Al-Ṭabarī took the verse as describing a specific social type of the Prophet's time: Bedouins who would come to Madīnah, embrace Islam while there was material advantage in it — a share of the spoils, a marriage, a season of good grazing — and revert to their old ways when the advantage ran out. On this reading the verse's test is economic and literal: wealth and sons confirm the man in his profession of faith, hard seasons and daughters undo it.

Other commentators took the verse as a general description of the hypocrites, whose religion is built on a shallow calculation of what is good for them, and whom the slightest discomfort topples.

The general reading is the stronger one, for two reasons. The wording is *wa-mina al-nāsi man* — and among people is he who — the same partitive formula the sūrah has already used twice for types rather than individuals (22:3, 22:8). And the reported occasion does not restrict the meaning even if it is historically accurate, since the point of the verse is the mechanism, not the men. The mechanism is that a faith held for its benefits collapses when the benefits do, and that mechanism is not confined to seventh-century Arabia.

**Why the Loss Is Double**

The verse says he loses *al-dunyā wa-l-ākhirah* — this world and the next — and the claim deserves scrutiny, because it is not obvious. A man who walks away from a costly religion to keep his trade might appear to have kept this world and forfeited only the next.

He does not. The goods he was protecting were never guaranteed to him, and by staking his faith on them he has made his peace of mind a function of the most volatile thing in his life. The losses he feared he would suffer by remaining a believer are the losses he is now defenceless against, because the calculation that made him leave is the calculation that offers him nothing when the trial arrives anyway. Trials arrive regardless; the question is what a person is standing on when they do.

And the Hereafter is lost in the plain sense: he turned away from the thing that would have carried him through it. The verse calls this *al-khusrānu al-mubīn*, the manifest loss — manifest because both halves of the ledger are visible in a single lifetime, and because the man can see the first half of his loss while there is still time to reverse the second.

**What Islam Does to a Man Who Wants It to Pay**

The commentary tradition preserves a report that fits this verse exactly. A man complained to the Prophet ﷺ that his new religion had brought him no worldly good, and the Prophet answered him with a simile: Islam is not a spoken thing; Islam melts a man the way fire melts the impurities out of iron, gold, and silver. [UNVERIFIED: this report is cited in the commentaries without a chain or collection number in the material available to me; I have not been able to verify its grading this session, so it is cited by sense rather than by number.]

The point of the simile is the answer to the man on the brink. He assumed the religion was an insurance policy, and he was auditing the premiums against the payouts. What the report says is that the religion is a refiner's fire, which is to say its first effect on a life is to burn things out of it. A man who reads the burning as evidence of a bad bargain has misunderstood what the fire is for.

That is the saving difference between the two figures in these verses. Suffering is the same event for both. For one it is the furnace that purifies; for the other it is the proof that the contract was a fraud.

The Same Wager Tested in Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt

The Qur'an returns to this type and puts a sharper edge on it: *“There are some who say, ‘We believe in Allah,’ but when they suffer in the cause of Allah, they mistake ˹this˺ persecution at the hands of people for the punishment of Allah. But when victory comes from your Lord, they surely say ˹to the believers˺, ‘We have always been with you’”* (29:10). The man of that verse misreads the source of his own trouble — he takes the hostility of people for the displeasure of God — and then reappears to claim the credit when things improve. The brink of 22:11 and the confusion of 29:10 are the same failure seen from two sides: a religion held on the condition that it must pay.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:12

> They call besides Allah what can neither harm nor benefit them. That is ˹truly˺ the farthest one can stray.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Prayer Directed at the Wrong Address**

The verb is *yadʿū* — he calls upon, he invokes, he prays to. The Qur'an uses the same verb for calling on God, and that shared vocabulary is what makes the verse an indictment rather than a sociological note. This is not a man with an unusual hobby. He is doing the thing a human being does when he needs help, and he is doing it at the wrong number.

The object of the verb is *min dūni Allāh* — from below God, short of God, in place of God. The phrase is standard and mild-sounding, and its frequency in the Qur'an is part of how the Qur'an argues: it treats the substitution rather than the existence of the substitutes. The worshipper is not being mocked for having recourse. He is being asked what he expects to happen next.

**"Neither Harms nor Benefits": the Qur'an's Working Test for a God**

The criterion stated here is functional, and it is stated in the same words across the Qur'an. Ibrāhīm puts it to his people in the form of a question: *“Do you then worship—instead of Allah—what can neither benefit nor harm you in any way?”* (21:66). The same formula is put into the mouth of the Messenger as a test his own community should apply to themselves: *“Should we invoke, other than Allah, those ˹idols˺ which cannot benefit or harm us, and turn back to disbelief after Allah has guided us?”* (6:71). It recurs as a flat description of the practice: *“Yet they worship besides Allah what can neither benefit nor harm them”* (25:55). Ibrāhīm's audience is asked the same question in another form: *“Or can they benefit or harm you?”* (26:73).

The repetition across the whole Qur'an is deliberate, and the reason repays attention. The test is not philosophical. It does not ask whether the idol is eternal, whether it has a mind, or whether it is worthy. It asks a question any worshipper can answer from experience: has this thing ever done anything for you? A criterion that anyone can apply, applied to a claim anyone can check, is the kind of argument that does not require a scholar to adjudicate.

**They Were Not Thought to Be Creators**

The obvious objection is that nobody in Makkah believed a carved stone had made the sky. The Qur'an knows this, and it says so: *“They worship besides Allah others who can neither harm nor benefit them, and say, ‘These are our intercessors with Allah’”* (10:18).

That verse is the key to this one. The role assigned to the idols was intercession — influence with the real power, a word put in with someone who can act. The Qur'an's reply in 10:18 is withering precisely because it takes the claim seriously: are you informing God of something He does not know?

Verse 12 of this sūrah dismantles the arrangement from the other end. An intercessor who can neither harm nor benefit has nothing to intercede with. If he cannot move a single atom on his own, his access to the One who moves atoms is worthless, since the whole point of the access was to produce an effect. A patron with no capacity is not a weaker patron; he is no patron at all.

**"That Is the Farthest Straying"**

The verdict is *wa-huwa al-ḍalālu al-baʿīd* — and that is the far straying, the straying at a distance.

The metaphor is distance, not depth. The Qur'an could have called it the deepest error or the gravest; it chose the word for remoteness, and the choice fits everything the sūrah has been building. The man of verse 11 was on the brink, in position but not committed. The man here has travelled. He is not near the edge of the truth having wandered slightly; he is at a measurable distance from it, and the distance was covered by steps that each seemed reasonable — recourse, intercession, a little help from a friend closer to the throne than you are.

The definite article matters too. *The* far straying, as though there were a recognized extreme and this were it. The superlative is not a rhetorical flourish but a classification: of all the ways a person can be wrong about God, taking a powerless thing as a route to the Powerful is the furthest out.

**Why the Criterion Is Benefit, Not Power**

Two things are worth drawing out about the test itself.

The first is that it is mercifully low. The Qur'an does not open its case against idolatry with metaphysics or with a demand for moral purity. It asks whether the thing has ever helped anyone. Every person who has ever prayed to an idol and received nothing already knows the answer, which means the argument is not being imported from outside the worshipper's experience. It is being drawn out of it.

The second is that the test cuts in the positive direction as well. If the mark of a false god is that it cannot harm or benefit, then the mark of the true one is that He can — and the whole of this sūrah, from the embryo in the womb to the rain on the dead field to the raising of the graves, has been establishing exactly that about God. The criterion of verse 12 is the criterion the sūrah's first eleven verses were written to satisfy.

**Two Ways of Getting This Wrong**

The verse's economy also protects it against two opposite misreadings, and both were live in the audience that first heard it.

The first is the view that the objects of worship are dangerous powers — real, malevolent, requiring appeasement. Verse 12 rules that out directly: a thing that can neither harm nor benefit is not a threat, and treating it as one is the superstition the Qur'an is dismantling. Fear of an idol is as misplaced as hope in it.

The second is the view that idolatry is harmless because the idols are nothing — that a person can keep his stones and his monotheism at the same time, since the stones do nothing either way. That is precisely the position verse 13 will confront, and the sūrah's answer there is that the worship itself does damage regardless of whether the object is a nullity. Futility in the object does not produce neutrality in the act.

Between the two, the Qur'an holds a narrow line that is easy to state and hard to keep: the idol is nothing, and worshipping it is a catastrophe.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:13

> They invoke those whose worship leads to harm, not benefit. What an evil patron and what an evil associate!

**Expanded Commentary**

**From "Neither" to "Harm"**

Verse 12 said the object of worship can neither harm nor benefit. Verse 13 moves the ground: he calls on what harms him rather than benefits him. The escalation is small in wording and large in implication, and it is the sort of movement the Qur'an makes often within a pair of verses.

The first verse established the idol's nullity — nothing comes of it either way. That is the sceptical point, and it is sufficient to show the futility of the practice. Verse 13 goes further and says the practice is not merely futile but costly. A neutral object would at least waste a person's time; this one inflicts damage.

Read in sequence, the two verses describe a progression the worshipper himself would not accept. He would agree that his god does him no good — every devotee has had unanswered prayers, and the Qur'an's question in verse 12 is designed to be unanswerable. What he would not agree to is that the worship is actively harming him. That is the claim the sūrah makes, and it is the claim that explains the double exclamation at the end.

**What the Harm Consists In**

The commentators were careful here, and their caution is instructive. The harm is not magical. No idol emits a curse; the stone does nothing because it can do nothing, which verse 12 has just established. Al-Rāzī's reading is the one to follow: the worship of something other than God is harmful *in itself*, from the religious point of view, because it redirects the worship a human being owes and thereby dismantles the relationship that makes him whole.

On that account the damage is constitutional rather than punitive. A person is built to orient himself toward his source, and misdirecting that orientation is not a harmless hobby with an unfortunate ending; it is the deformation of the thing he is. The harm happens at the moment of the prostration, not afterwards.

There is a second, plainer harm in the social register, and it is the one the two closing nouns point to: a man has put his trust in a patron who cannot deliver. The damage is what happens to a person who discovers, at the moment he needs help, that his insurance was never underwritten.

**Two Words for a Bad Friend**

The verse ends by naming the relationship twice: *labi'sa al-mawlā wa-labi'sa al-ʿashīr* — what an evil *mawlā*, and what an evil *ʿashīr*.

Both terms are institutions before they are insults, and the choice of these two in particular is exact. A *mawlā* is a patron or client, the party to a bond of protection — the arrangement by which a person without a tribe attached himself to one, answered for by it and answerable to it. An *ʿashīr* is a close companion, the person you mix with, the associate of your daily life and, in the tribal vocabulary, a kinsman of your own generation.

Between them they cover the two relationships in which a pre-Islamic Arab located his safety: formal protection and informal company. The verse declares both of them evil in this instance. The god he took as a *mawlā* cannot protect him; the god he took as a daily companion, the one whose company he keeps, turns out to be worse than nobody.

**Why the Verdict Is Shouted Rather Than Argued**

The form is *labi'sa*, a fixed Arabic construction of censure — how evil is such-and-such. The Qur'an reserves it for a small set of judgements, and it always closes an argument rather than opening one.

That placement is the point. Verses 12 and 13 have already done the reasoning: the criterion was stated, the criterion was applied, the nullity was established, and the harm was named. What remains is not a further premise but a verdict, and the verse gives it as a verdict — an exclamation, not a syllogism.

The rhetorical effect is worth noticing because it is unusual in the Qur'an, which so often argues patiently with its opponents. Here the patience ends. A person who has been shown that his patron is powerless and still keeps the patron is not going to be moved by another argument, and the verse does not offer him one. It pronounces.

**The Verse Is Also a Warning About Company**

*ʿAshīr* deserves a second look. A *mawlā* is chosen deliberately, with formalities. An *ʿashīr* is acquired almost without noticing — the people you sit with, whose opinions become weather. The verse names both because the damage enters by both routes.

This is the practical edge of the passage. A person can renounce every idol he can name and still keep company that quietly reorients him, because companionship works by habit rather than by decision. The Qur'an treats association as a substantive act, one of the few things that shapes a person more than his stated beliefs do, and it will say so explicitly elsewhere when it describes a man biting his hands on the Day of Resurrection over the friend he chose.
