Naming the *ʿashīr* as evil therefore completes the diagnosis. The idolater's error is not only that he bowed to a stone; it is that he built a life around things that could not help him, and he did it in company, which is how such lives are always built.

**The Damage Is Available to Be Seen Now**

One last feature of the pairing. Both nouns describe relationships in this world: a patron and a companion are things you have while you are alive. The harm the verse names is therefore not deferred to a judgment day, though the sūrah will return to that judgment soon enough.

The man in verse 11 lost both worlds, and the man here is losing this one already — his protection is fictional, his companion is worthless, and the discovery can be made by anyone who asks what his patron has ever actually done. The sūrah's next verse will turn immediately to the people whose patron is real.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:14

> Indeed, Allah will admit those who believe and do good into Gardens, under which rivers flow. Surely Allah does what He wills.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise That Answers the Failure**

Verses 11 to 13 described three figures: the man on the brink, the man calling on what cannot help, and the man whose patron is worse than nothing. Verse 14 answers all three with a single sentence about a different person.

The structure is one the Qur'an uses constantly and this sūrah uses structurally: the failed case and the successful case are placed side by side so that the difference between them is visible without commentary. The believers are not praised in detail here. They are set next to the man on the brink, and the reader supplies the contrast. What separated them was not the events — both were tested — but where each was standing when the test arrived.

The sequence also answers the specific losses. The man of verse 11 lost this world and the next; the believers here gain the next in the most concrete available image. The man of verse 13 had a fictional patron; these people have a real one who admits them.

**"Those Who Believe and Do Good"**

The formula is the Qur'an's standard abbreviation for the whole of the religion, and it is worth resisting the habit of reading past it. Two conditions, always joined, never separated in this construction.

*Āmanū* — they believed, they made themselves secure in a trust. *Wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti* — and they did the deeds that are sound, that work, that hold up. The pairing matters in both directions. Belief without works is the condition of the man in verse 11, who said the words and was toppled by the first trial. Works without belief would be a moral life with no orientation, which the Qur'an does not recognize as a category.

The first occurrence of the promise in the Qur'an's arrangement uses the identical phrasing and the identical reward: *“Give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to those who believe and do good that they will have Gardens under which rivers flow”* (2:25). The stability of the formula across the whole text is the point of it. Salvation is not described differently for different groups; the same two conditions and the same promise are on offer to everyone.

**"Gardens Under Which Rivers Flow"**

The reward is *jannātun tajrī min taḥtihā al-anhār* — gardens beneath which rivers run. The preposition is *taḥta*, beneath: the water is described as flowing below the garden, irrigating from underneath, at the roots.

For an audience whose wealth was measured in wells and whose fears were measured in drought, the image needs no explanation. A garden watered from below cannot fail, because it does not depend on rain arriving. It is the exact inversion of the field in verse 5, which lies dead and brown until water comes down on it from outside. The paradise promised here is the field that never needs to wait.

The word *jannah* itself means a garden, a concealed place, something walled and planted. What is promised is not a state of consciousness and not a set of delights listed in the abstract. It is a place, described by the one feature of a place that matters most to a desert people: it is watered, permanently, from underneath.

**"God Does What He Wills"**

The verse closes with *inna Allāha yafʿalu mā yurīd* — God does whatever He intends.

Placed after a promise, the clause is not a threat and not a hedge. A promise is only as good as the power and freedom of the one making it, and this clause states that the Promiser is unimpeded. No one can block His decision, no rival can overrule it, and no shortage can prevent it. The garden is as good as the power behind it.

The clause is also the sūrah's quiet answer to the patron of verse 13. An evil *mawlā* is one who cannot deliver; this verse describes the One whose will is not obstructed. The two verses are three apart and they are talking about the same transaction.

**The Sūrah's Alternating Rhythm**

It is worth stepping back to see where this verse falls in the sūrah's architecture. The passage has been alternating: warning (vv. 1–4), argument (vv. 5–7), the opponents (vv. 8–10), the failures (vv. 11–13), and now the promise. Verse 23 will repeat the promise with more detail, verse 50 will repeat it again, and verse 56 will restate it at the judgment.

The alternation is a method of instruction, not a sign of repetition fatigue. Every warning is followed by what the warning is for, and every promise is preceded by what the promise is rescue from. A reader who takes either half alone gets a distorted religion: warning without promise produces despair, promise without warning produces presumption. The sūrah refuses to let a reader have either one in isolation, which is why the brink-dweller of verse 11 and the garden-dweller of verse 14 are separated by only two verses.

**What "Admit" Presumes**

The verb is *yudkhilu* — He will cause them to enter. Entry is caused, and the cause is not them.

That is the final thing the verse says about the people it describes. They believed and they did sound deeds, and still the language of the reward is that they were brought in. The two conditions are real and required, and the entry is a favour. Holding both at once is the plain sense of the sentence, and it is the settlement of the question that has run through these verses since the man on the brink decided his works entitled him to a smooth life.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:15

> Whoever thinks that Allah will not help His Prophet in this world and the Hereafter, let them stretch out a rope to the ceiling and strangle themselves, then let them see if this plan will do away with ˹the cause of˺ their rage.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse With More Than One Door**

This is the most difficult verse in the passage, and the commentaries say so by disagreeing. The disagreements are worth setting out because they turn on identifiable features of the Arabic rather than on taste.

The first question is who the pronoun refers to. Most commentators take "will not help him" as referring to the Prophet ﷺ: whoever supposes that God will not give His Prophet victory in this world and the next. Others read it as referring to the presumptuous man himself — whoever thinks that God will not help *him*.

The second question is who is being addressed. Readings include the Prophet's enemies, who were enraged by his success; the tribes of Asad and Ghaṭafān, who expected him to fail; and, on one reading, a group of believers so impatient for victory against the idolaters that their frustration had curdled. The wording is general — *man kāna yaẓunnu*, whoever thinks — and generality is what allows the readings to coincide.

**"A Rope to the Sky"**

The third question is the image. The Arabic is *fal-yamdud bi-sababin ilā al-samāʾ* — let him stretch out a rope to the *samāʾ*. That word means sky, heaven, or, in ordinary speech, the ceiling of a room. Both senses are live, and both were used by the commentators.

On the ceiling reading, favoured by the tafsīr attributed to Ibn ʿAbbās and followed by many others, the picture is a man in his house fixing a rope overhead. On the sky reading, he stretches a rope upward to reach the place the help comes from and cut it off. A third reading takes *sabab* in its extended sense of a means or a stratagem rather than a physical rope: let him use whatever means he can devise, reach as high as he can, and try.

The verb that follows, *thumma l-yaqṭaʿ* — then let him cut — is what makes the ceiling reading the more natural one. A man who has stretched a rope above him and then severs something is most simply read as having cut his own breath off. Yusuf ʿAlī's note on the two renderings puts the point well: on the ceiling reading the enemies are told to choke with rage; on the sky reading they are told to climb up and try to intercept the help, which would make them fools.

**What the Verse Is Not Saying**

Some readings have taken the verse as licensing despair, and that misreading needs to be closed off explicitly. The verse is not a command, and it is not advice. It is a *reductio*, delivered in the imperative mood the way a person says "go ahead, then" — the form of an instruction used to expose the futility of the thing instructed.

Nothing here permits suicide, which the Qur'an forbids in the plainest terms elsewhere. The force of the sentence depends on the absurdity of what it describes: a man so consumed that he would hang himself to stop being angry, and even that not working. The absurdity is the argument. If the extreme remedy fails, no lesser scheme will succeed.

**Rage Is the Thing Being Diagnosed**

The last clause names the condition: *fa-l-yanẓur hal yudhhibanna kayduhu mā yaghīẓ* — let him see whether his scheme removes what enrages him. The word is *ghayẓ*, the hot anger that a person cannot act on, and the verse treats it as the illness rather than the symptom.

This is where the verse connects to what precedes it. Verse 11 described a man whose religion collapsed under a trial; verse 13 described a man whose patron was worthless; verse 15 describes a man who is furious, and the fury is about God's help going to someone else. The sūrah is tracing a single condition through several faces, and the face here is resentment — the conviction that God is backing the wrong side, or backing nobody, and that the speaker has been cheated.

Theological resentment has a signature, and the verse names it precisely: the sufferer believes that if he could just reach the source and cut the line, he would feel better. The sūrah's response is to grant the attempt and ask him to check the result. God's relationship with His Prophet will be what it is, in this world and the next, and no rope will alter it.

**Despair Is the Thread That Ties the Passage Together**

Read as a group, verses 11 to 15 form one argument about what happens when a person's faith is conditional on outcomes. The man on the brink wanted benefits. The man with the idols wanted a patron who delivers. The man here wants a different result and is enraged that he cannot get it.

What they share is a religion that is a transaction. Each one has decided in advance what God owes him, and each one is undone when the debt is not paid on schedule. The sūrah's answer to all three is the same, and it is not comfort: the help of God is not a negotiable item, and the correct response to not receiving it on your timetable is not rage, not desertion, and not a rope.

Verse 16 supplies the other half of the answer immediately. The revelation comes down as clear signs, and God guides whom He wills — which means the outcome the resentful man is contesting was never a matter of his approval in the first place.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:16

> And so We revealed this ˹Quran˺ as clear verses. And Allah certainly guides whoever He wills.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Thus" — a Demonstrative With a Long Memory**

The verse opens with *wa-ka-dhālika* — and thus, and in that way. The demonstrative reaches back over everything preceding it: the disputants, the brink-dwellers, the useless patrons, the furious man reaching for a rope.

That is the sentence's first claim, and it is a strange one at first reading. In that way — in the way just described, amid that opposition — We sent it down. The revelation is not described as having arrived into a neutral or receptive setting. It arrived into exactly the resistance the preceding verses have catalogued, and the manner of its arrival is presented as the fitting response to it. Clear signs are what you send to people who are arguing in the dark.

The demonstrative also reaches forward. The word *ka-dhālika* is used in the Qur'an to mark a pattern rather than a one-off: thus does God do, thus have We sent, thus will it be. The sending down of clear signs is being described as the standing method, not a single event.

**What "Clear Signs" Means, and What It Does Not**

*Āyātin bayyinātin* — signs that are clear, evident, self-explaining. The Qur'an uses the phrase for previous revelation as well as for its own: *“Indeed, We have sent down to you ˹O Prophet˺ clear revelations. ˹But˺ none will deny them except the rebellious”* (2:99), and in the description of what scripture does: *“He is the One Who sends down clear revelations to His servant to bring you out of darkness and into light”* (57:9).

The adjective describes the signs, not the reception. This distinction is the one most often collapsed, and collapsing it produces the objection that a clear revelation ought to produce universal agreement. Clarity in the Qur'an's usage is a property of the message: it is unambiguous about what it is asking and what it is warning of. It is not a guarantee about the audience.

That is why the same word can be used for the revelation and, in verse 8 of this sūrah, for the scripture the disputant does not have. The illumination is real and the failure to see it is also real. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān makes the same point in its own vocabulary: *“Let there be no compulsion in religion, for the truth stands out clearly from falsehood”* (2:256). The standing-out belongs to the truth; whether a person looks is another matter.

**"God Guides Whom He Wills" — the Standard Misreading**

The closing clause is the one readers stumble over. Taken in isolation it sounds like a decree issued at random, and the Qur'an's own usage rules that reading out.

The clause sits at the end of a passage about people who argue without knowledge, worship on a brink, and reach for ropes. Guidance in this context is not being distributed arbitrarily; it is being withheld from a described type. The will of God in the Qur'an is never presented as a substitute for reasons — it is presented as the ground of a decision whose reasons are also given, in this case at length.

Sūrah al-Kahf states the same pairing without any suggestion of arbitrariness: *“Whoever Allah guides is truly guided. But whoever He leaves to stray, you will never find for them a guiding mentor”* (18:17). The emphasis there falls on the consequence for the person left, not on an unexplained selection.

**Clarity and Guidance Are Two Different Gifts**

The most useful thing this verse does is hold two facts together that readers tend to separate.

The first is that the signs are clear, which means no one is condemned for failing to understand something that was never explained. Accountability in the Qur'an always follows a message that actually arrived, and this verse guarantees that it did.

The second is that guidance is God's to give, which means clarity does not compel belief. A person can see a clear sign and refuse it, and verses 8 to 13 of this very sūrah are a catalogue of people doing exactly that. If clarity automatically produced faith, the disputant of verse 8 could not exist — and he plainly does, and the sūrah spends six verses describing him.

So the verse is not a puzzle to be solved but a pair of truths to be held: the message is sufficient to leave no one with an excuse, and the response is a gift rather than a mechanical effect. The Prophet's own limits are described elsewhere in these terms; he cannot guide whom he loves, but the revelation he brought is clear. Between those two facts runs the whole of the Qur'an's account of why people believe and why they do not.

**The Sending Down Is Continuous, Not Only Historical**

The verb *anzalnāhu* is in the first-person plural past — We sent it down. In the Qur'an's usage the sending is not confined to a single descent; the same verb describes the rain that revives the dead earth in verse 5 of this sūrah.

The parallel is not accidental. Both are described as something sent down from above onto ground that cannot produce the result by itself, and in both cases the effect depends on what the ground does with what it receives. A field that has been rained on and stayed brown is the image the sūrah used for the dead earth; the disputant of verse 8, who has the clearest possible signs in front of him and argues anyway, is the same field in human form.

This is why the verse pairs the clarity of the signs with the freedom of the guidance. The rain is sufficient, and it does not coerce the harvest.

**Where the Passage Lands**

Verses 8 to 16 form a single movement: the disputant, his sentence, the man on the brink, the false patrons, the rage, and finally the nature of the revelation itself. Verse 17 will widen the frame to all the religious communities, and the sūrah will not return to this intimate scale for some time.

The placement of this verse is therefore a hinge. Before it, the sūrah has been describing individuals in their failure. After it, the failures are set against a revelation whose clarity is not in question, and against a guidance that is not in human hands. That is the last word on the matter, and the sūrah delivers it in eleven words before moving on.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:17

> Indeed, the believers, Jews, Sabians, Christians, Magi, and the polytheists—Allah will judge between them ˹all˺ on Judgment Day. Surely Allah is a Witness over all things.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A List of Six, and the Last Name on It**

The verse names six groups: those who believe, the Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians, and those who associate others with God. The first four appear together elsewhere in the Qur'an in a formula of promise. The addition here is the last two, and their addition changes the sentence's function entirely.

The familiar four-name list is a list of communities with scriptures, or with claims to one. By placing *al-mushrikīn* — the associators, the polytheists — at the end of the same grammatical series, the verse does two things at once. It refuses to grant the associators a standing the earlier list would have implied, and it refuses to settle the question of who among the named groups is right. Everyone on the list is on the same footing before the thing that matters, which is not membership but judgment.

That flattening is the verse's most striking feature. A Jew, a Christian, a Zoroastrian, and a Makkan idolater are enumerated with the same conjunction between them, and the sentence that governs them all is that God will decide.

**The Sabians: an Identity the Commentators Could Not Settle**

*Al-ṣābiʾūn* appears in the Qur'an alongside the Jews and Christians as a community with a claim to revelation, and the commentators disagreed sharply about who they were — a baptismal sect near the marshes of southern Iraq, a star-worshipping group in Harrān, or people who had left one religion without entering another.

The disagreement matters less than what it shows. The Qur'an names a community whose precise identity was already uncertain to its earliest readers, and the uncertainty has never been resolved to everyone's satisfaction. What the text cares about is not the taxonomy but the principle: a group that stands outside the familiar scriptural categories is still named, still counted, and still within the scope of the divine judgment rather than outside it.

The legal consequence drawn by the jurists from the parallel verse in Sūrah al-Māʾidah was that membership in a named community is not what saves. *“Indeed, the believers, Jews, Sabians and Christians—whoever ˹truly˺ believes in Allah and the Last Day and does good, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve”* (5:69). The condition inside that sentence is belief, the Last Day, and sound action — not the label.

**Majūs: a Word That Occurs Once**

*Al-majūs* appears nowhere else in the Qur'an. The word is generally understood to derive from the Greek *magos*, which came from Old Persian *magu*, referring originally to a hereditary priestly line among the Persians. In later usage it was applied to Zoroastrians generally, and that is how Muslim writers have almost always understood it.

The history of the term is worth a moment, because it explains a legal argument that ran for centuries. If the Magians were a priestly class rather than a religion, they could not be People of the Book. If they were a scripture-owning community, the rules governing marriage, slaughtered meat, and the poll tax would follow. The jurists split: many, including al-Shāfiʿī, treated Zoroastrians as People of the Book; others did not; and the practical rulings diverged accordingly — some authorities forbade intermarriage with Zoroastrians while permitting the purchase of their meat, and others ruled the reverse.

The Qur'an itself does not settle that question here. What it does is place the Magians inside the list of communities whose differences will be adjudicated, which is a different and prior claim: they are not outside the reckoning.

**Judgment Deferred Is Not Judgment Denied**

The verse's main assertion is that God will judge *between* them on the Day of Resurrection. The preposition is *bayna*, between — a dispute with parties on both sides, and a decision that has not yet been issued.

This is the Qur'an's standard posture on inter-religious disagreement, and it is worth stating plainly because it is so often misrepresented. The Qur'an does not say that all communities are equally correct. It says where the adjudication will take place. The communities themselves make counter-claims that cannot both be true — *“The Jews say, ‘The Christians have nothing to stand on’ and the Christians say, ‘The Jews have nothing to stand on,’ although both recite the Scriptures”* (2:113) — and the text's response is not to declare a winner in the abstract but to name the court.

Deferral here is not evasion. It is the claim that the dispute is real, that its resolution requires knowledge no party possesses, and that the resolution will actually happen.

**"God Is Witness Over All Things"**

The verse closes with *inna Allāha ʿalā kulli shayʾin shahīd* — God is, over everything, a witness.

The attribute is what makes the deferred judgment coherent. A court needs evidence, and the evidence in a dispute about what people believed and why is their interior life, which no human tribunal can examine. The claim that God is *shahīd* — present, observing, able to testify — is the claim that the record exists, that it is complete, and that nothing in any of these communities' histories is missing from it.

It is also, quietly, a warning to the reader. The same sentence that says the religious disputes of history will be settled also says that everything has been seen. Nobody in the list of six is being told that his case has gone unnoticed in the interval.

**The Verse's Political Consequence**

A text that defers adjudication to the Day of Resurrection is also a text that declines to authorize coercion in the meantime, and the early community read it that way. Sūrah al-Bayyinah describes the People of the Book and the associators as waiting for *“the clear proof”* to come to them (98:1), and the proof's arrival is what divided them — not the sword.

Sūrah al-Māʾidah states the principle in its most explicit form: *“To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you with what He has given ˹each of˺ you. So compete with one another in doing good”* (5:48). Plurality is described as the test, and the competition is in good works rather than in verdicts.

That is the temper of this verse too. The six names are listed, the dispute is acknowledged as genuine, and the only thing asserted is that it will be settled — by the Witness, on the appointed day.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:18

> Do you not see that to Allah bow down ˹in submission˺ all those in the heavens and all those on the earth, as well as the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, and ˹all˺ living beings, as well as many humans, while many are deserving of punishment. And whoever Allah disgraces, none can honour. Surely Allah does what He wills.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Catalogue Runs Downwards, and Then Stops at You**

The verse opens with the widest possible claim — everything in the heavens and everything on the earth prostrates to God — and then particularizes: the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees, the moving creatures, and many of mankind.

The order is a descending one, and it is worth following. It moves from the greatest luminous bodies to the fixed features of the landscape to growing things to animals, and then it arrives at human beings, where the grammar changes. For everything before, prostration is asserted outright. For humanity, the verse splits: many prostrate, and many have the punishment become due against them.

The structure is the whole point. Human beings are the only item on the list that can do otherwise, and the verse marks that difference by breaking its own pattern at exactly the place where the reader is standing.

**How a Mountain Prostrates**

The obvious question is what prostration means when the subject is a star or a stone, and the commentators answered it in two directions that are not mutually exclusive.

One reading takes *sujūd* in its literal sense of bowing down and holds that every creature performs an act proper to its own mode of being, which we do not perceive. Sūrah al-Raʿd is the controlling text: *“To Allah ˹alone˺ bow down ˹in submission˺ all those in the heavens and the earth—willingly or unwillingly—as do their shadows, morning and evening”* (13:15). Sūrah al-Naḥl adds the angels to the same list (16:49), and Sūrah al-Raḥmān includes the stars and the trees in a single line: *“The stars and the trees bow down ˹in submission˺”* (55:6).

The other reading takes the prostration as the creature's total submission to the laws it cannot break — a star's obedience is its orbit. Sūrah al-Isrāʾ holds the two together and explains why the language is not ordinary description: *“There is not a single thing that does not glorify His praises—but you ˹simply˺ cannot comprehend their glorification”* (17:44).

The stronger reading keeps both: the submission is real, and it is not the kind of thing a human observer could confirm. The verse is making a claim about what creation is, not about what can be measured.

**Why the Sun Is Listed and the Angels Are Not**

A detail that has drawn comment: the great bodies mentioned are precisely the ones human beings have worshipped. The sun, the moon and the stars were the objects of the oldest cults known to the Arabs and to their neighbours.

The verse's move is to enlist them on the other side. The things people bowed to are themselves bowing, and they have been doing it the whole time. This is the same argument Ibrāhīm makes in Sūrah al-Anʿām when he watches a star, then the moon, then the sun and rejects each in turn because it sets — the argument being that a thing which is itself subject is not a thing to be subject to.

That is why the list is not decoration. Every item on it is a witness for the prosecution.

**The Reader Is Asked to Prostrate Too**

This is one of two verses in this sūrah at which the Qur'an's reciters bow, and the practice is attested in the ḥadīth literature. ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir reported that he asked the Messenger of Allah ﷺ whether Sūrat al-Ḥajj contains two prostrations, and was told that it does, and that whoever does not perform them should not recite them — recorded in *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (1402), where al-Albānī graded the chain weak, though others have accepted it. A related report from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, also in Abū Dāwūd (1401), counts fifteen prostrations in the Qur'an including two in this sūrah.

The fit between verse and practice is exact. A reader who has just recited a list of everything in creation prostrating is invited to join it. The alternative is to be the exception the verse names — the one who hears the catalogue and remains upright.

**The Split Inside Humanity**

*Wa-kathīrun mina al-nāsi wa-kathīrun ḥaqqa ʿalayhi al-ʿadhāb* — and many of mankind, and many against whom the punishment has come due. The repetition of *kathīr*, many, sets the two groups in balance, and the word for the second group's condition is *ḥaqqa*, became due — a claim that has matured, the way a debt does.

Both readings of that clause deserve their weight. On one, the division is between those who prostrate and those who refuse. On the other, the second *kathīr* is a continuation of the first: many of mankind prostrate, and many of mankind have punishment owed against them — meaning the same broad category described from two angles, since a person can be both.

What is clear is that humanity is the only part of creation described as internally divided. The stars are not split; the trees are not split. The creature made to bow freely is the creature that can be counted on both sides of the line.

**"Whoever God Disgraces, None Can Honour"**

The closing sentence is the sūrah's third use of this form, and its logic is the same each time: if God abases someone, no one can confer honour on him instead.

The point is sovereignty, and it is stated without qualification for the same reason it is stated in Sūrah Āl ʿImrān: *“If Allah helps you, none can defeat you. But if He denies you help, then who else can help you?”* (3:160). Honour in this life is a commodity that human beings trade in and believe they control. The verse places its source somewhere no one can touch, which is simultaneously a warning to the proud and a consolation to the humiliated.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:19

> These are two opposing groups that disagree about their Lord: as for the disbelievers, garments of Fire will be cut out for them and boiling water will be poured over their heads,

**Expanded Commentary**

**"These Two Adversaries" — How Wide Is the Reference**

*Hādhāni khaṣmāni ikhtaṣamū fī rabbihim* — these two adversaries disputed concerning their Lord. The phrase is indefinite in form and dual in number, and the commentators split over how specific it is.

The general reading, which most prefer, takes the two adversaries as any two parties who dispute about God: believers and disbelievers, in every generation, and the verse that follows describes the two outcomes. A second reading takes it as the Muslims and the People of the Book, since the latter claimed antiquity and nearness to God and argued from it. A third holds that the dispute is between the Garden and the Fire themselves, over what each was created for.

The general reading is the strongest, and the reason is grammatical as much as theological. The dual *khaṣmāni* is used in Arabic for two sides rather than two men, and what follows is a description of two destinies rather than of two named parties. A specific occasion does not exclude the general sense even where a specific occasion can be identified.

**Why Badr Was Proposed**

The most frequently cited occasion is the opening of the Battle of Badr, when three champions came forward from each army: ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, Ḥamzah and ʿUbaydah ibn al-Ḥārith for the Muslims, and three fighters from Quraysh against them.

The choice is not arbitrary. Badr was the first open battle of the new community, and it was fought by people who had argued about God for a decade before they argued about anything else. Whatever else was at stake that morning — trade routes, clan honour, the wells — the dispute the verse names is the one that had produced the quarrel: who is right about the Lord.

Reading the verse against that occasion gives the opening phrase its bite. Two sides, both claiming God, and the claim is going to be settled twice: once in the valley, and once in the terms this verse sets out.

**"Garments of Fire Cut Out"**

*Qutṭiʿat lahum thiyābun min nārin* — garments of fire have been cut out for them. The verb is from *qaṭṭaʿa*, to cut into pieces, to cut to shape. It is the vocabulary of a tailor working to a pattern, and the preposition *lahum*, for them, marks the garment as made to measure.

Every element of the image is pointed. Clothing is what a person uses to cover himself, to appear, and to be ranked by; the Qur'an's paradisal vocabulary uses garments the same way, as the visible sign of a state. Here the covering is fire, and it has been tailored to fit the wearer — the punishment is not a uniform issued to a class but a garment cut for a person.

The detail also works as irony. The disbelievers' dispute about their Lord was, in large part, a dispute about status, and the first thing the verse gives them is something to wear.

**Boiling Water Poured Over the Head**

*Yuṣabbu min fawqi ruʾūsihim al-ḥamīm* — scalding water is poured from above their heads. The verb is passive; who pours is not said, which leaves the machinery of the punishment as impersonal as the machinery of the world.

The detail that the water comes from *above the head* rather than from any direction is deliberate. Poured from above, it runs over everything: face, shoulders, chest, and the whole body in turn. A punishment aimed at one part could be endured by protecting the rest; this one reaches all of it at once, and the next verse describes what it does on the way down.

The word *ḥamīm* is the Qur'an's standard term for the boiling liquid of punishment, and it carries a second sense that sharpens it: *ḥamīm* also means an intimate friend, a close companion. The same root that gives the punished their comrades gives them their scalding.

**Why the Punishment Comes Before the Reward**

Verses 19 to 22 describe the destination of one group; verses 23 and 24 describe the destination of the other. The reward is mentioned second, and the ordering is a teaching choice rather than a preference for bad news.

The sūrah has been operating this way since verse 1. The earthquake comes before the argument, the argument before the promise, and the threat before the rescue. A promise placed first would be heard as a bribe; a punishment placed first establishes what is at stake, and the reward then reads as deliverance from something rather than as an entitlement.

The Qur'an does not keep this order rigidly — verse 14 gave the promise of the Gardens before any of these punishments were described — but where the two are set side by side as matching pictures, as they are here, the severity is stated first. The reader is meant to feel the weight of what he is being spared.

**When the Garden and the Fire Spoke**

On the reading that takes the two adversaries as the Garden and the Fire themselves, the report is a famous one and it is well attested. Abū Hurayrah narrated that the Prophet ﷺ said that Paradise and the Fire argued: the Fire said, "I have been given the arrogant and the tyrants," and Paradise said, "Why is it that only the weak and the humble enter me?" God then said to Paradise, "You are My mercy, by which I show mercy to whom I will," and to the Fire, "You are My punishment, by which I punish whom I will, and each of you will be filled." It is recorded in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (4850, in the Book of Tafsīr) and in *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (2846), in the chapter headed "the arrogant will enter the Fire and the humble will enter Paradise."

The report explains the verse's phrasing on that reading — *ikhtaṣamū fī rabbihim*, they disputed concerning their Lord, meaning they laid their cases before Him — and it explains why the sūrah goes on to describe the two destinations in matching terms. The Fire is told it will be filled; the inhabitants described in this verse are its filling.

Even without that report, the reading is instructive, because the sūrah will go on to describe the two destinations in matching terms — garments of fire here, silk and bracelets in verse 23. The symmetry is the argument of the whole passage, and this verse is where it opens.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:20

> melting whatever is in their bellies, along with their skin.

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Sentence, Two Surfaces**

The verse is a single clause and it does one thing: it says what the scalding water of the previous verse does. *Yuṣharu bihi mā fī buṭūnihim wa-l-julūd* — what is in their bellies is melted by it, and the skins too.

The pairing is total by design. The belly is the inside and the skin is the outside, and between them there is nothing left. The Qur'an does not say "their bodies," which would have allowed the reader to imagine a limit. It names the innermost cavity and the outermost membrane, and the punishment is described as operating on both at once.

**Why the Skin Is Named Separately**

The skin deserves its own note, because it is not listed as a covering but as an organ. The skin is where a person feels. Damage that stops at the skin is superficial by definition; damage that reaches the skin is damage that cannot be ignored.

This is why the Qur'an's punishments are so often described at the surface of the body, and why the same tradition renders the reward in the same register. Sūrah al-Kahf uses the identical vocabulary for the punishment of those who reject the truth: *“When they cry for aid, they will be aided with water like molten metal, which will burn ˹their˺ faces”* (18:29). The face is named there for the same reason the skin is named here — not to be lurid but to locate the suffering where a person actually lives, at the place where he meets the world.

**The Belly: What Is Inside Is Also Reached**

The inside is named with a relative clause — *mā fī buṭūnihim*, whatever is in their bellies — rather than with an organ. Nothing is specified, and the vagueness is doing what the vagueness of verse 1 did: it refuses to let the imagination find an edge.

Theologically, the inside matters for a specific reason in this sūrah. The disbelievers' offence has been described as a matter of the heart and the interior: disputing without knowledge (22:3), worshipping on a brink (22:11), arguing from preference rather than from a source (22:8). A punishment that reaches only the surface would leave the actual site of the disease untouched. The water goes in.

**Why the Description Is Given in Public at All**

A reasonable objection to verses of this kind is that they are addressed to the living, who are not in the Fire, and who therefore cannot be being threatened with what is described. If the punishment were meant only as information about the next world, it could have been stated in one line.

The Qur'an's answer is implicit in where these verses sit. They are given to people who still have time, and their function is not to satisfy curiosity about the next world but to change conduct in this one. A warning that cannot be pictured does not restrain anybody; a warning with a garment and a temperature does.

This also explains the sūrah's restraint elsewhere. The descriptions are physically specific about instruments and locations and almost entirely silent about the experience of suffering itself. What is being offered is enough to be feared and not enough to be contemplated — the exact balance a warning needs.

**What Melting Does to the Idea of a Self**

The verb *yuṣharu* describes a dissolution — what is in the bellies liquefies, and the skin with it. Applied to a person, the image attacks the boundary that makes a person a person.

A human being's sense of himself depends on his remaining contained: an inside that is his and a surface that marks where he stops. The Qur'an's punishments are not randomly chosen, and this one strikes at exactly that structure. The sufferer is not destroyed — destruction would end the suffering — but he is unmade and kept, dissolved while still conscious of being dissolved.

That is why the verse pairs the inside and the outside rather than choosing one. A punishment that reached only the interior would leave the person intact as an object; a punishment that reached only the skin would leave him intact within. Melting both leaves nothing in him that holds a shape.

**A Punishment for Bodies, Because the Resurrection Is of Bodies**

The concreteness of this verse is an argument, and it is worth stating as one. The Qur'an's account of the afterlife is not a set of images for a state of consciousness; it is a claim about what happens to the person who was buried.

That claim was argued at length in the opening of this sūrah — from the embryo, from the field, from the raising of those in the graves (22:5–7). Verses 19 to 22 are the pay-off of that argument, and they are as physical as the argument was. A doctrine of resurrection that produced a merely spiritual afterlife would not need garments cut to measure or water that melts what is in a belly.

The sūrah is consistent from end to end: the body that was created from dust and raised from the grave is the body that is punished, and the matching promise in verse 23 will dress a body in silk.

**Nothing in the Description Is Remedial**

The last thing to notice is what the verse does not say. It does not say the water cleanses, or that the melting is temporary, or that the sufferer is being prepared for something better.

The Qur'an elsewhere uses fire-language for purification — the report cited earlier in this sūrah compared Islam to a fire that draws the impurities out of metal — and that makes the contrast sharper here. The same image, in the same sūrah, in two opposite applications: fire that refines a person in this life, and fire that does nothing to him but damage in the next. The difference is not the fire. It is whether there is anything left in the man for the fire to purify.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:21

> And awaiting them are maces of iron.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence With No Verb**

*Wa-lahum maqāmiʿu min ḥadīdin* — and for them are hooked rods of iron. The Arabic is a nominal sentence: a prepositional phrase, then a noun. There is no verb in it at all.

The absence is the point. Everything in verses 19 and 20 was described as happening — garments cut, water poured, insides melted. Here nothing happens. The rods simply *are*, and they are *for them*, in the same way a possession is. The punishment is being described as a standing provision rather than as an event, and the shift in grammar is what carries it. A verb would have placed the beating in time. The nominal sentence places it outside time.

**What the Word Means**

*Maqāmiʿ* is the plural of *miqmaʿa*, an iron implement with a hooked or angled end. The lexicographers describe it as the bar used to strike a stubborn animal and the hook used to drive or restrain one; the root *q-m-ʿ* carries the sense of subduing, of breaking a thing's resistance.

The image is therefore not simply a weapon. A *miqmaʿa* is a tool for dealing with something that will not go where it is put. That is precisely the condition of the people described in the preceding verses, whose defining action in verse 22 will be that they keep trying to leave.

Commentators took the rods as instruments of beating, and the plain sense supports them. What the word adds, beyond the beating, is the element of being driven — the humiliation of being managed like an animal that refuses to move.

**Why Iron**

The material is named, and in the Qur'an's vocabulary iron is never a casual detail. Sūrah al-Ḥadīd describes it as something sent down: *“And We sent down iron with its great might, benefits for humanity, and means for Allah to prove who ˹is willing to˺ stand up for Him and His messengers without seeing Him”* (57:25).

That verse gives the metal a double character, and both halves are present here. Iron is the material of human strength — tools, weapons, the things that build and the things that break — and it is named in the sūrah that bears its name as a means by which God tests who stands up for Him. Used as an instrument of punishment in this sūrah, it carries the same double edge: the strongest material of the world that people put their trust in becomes the material of their subduing.

There is a physical aptness too. Iron is heavy, hard, and cold, and it does not soften in a fire of the kind the Qur'an is describing. The implement outlasts what it strikes.

**The Instrument Fits the Sin**

The people described in this passage are the ones who turned their shoulders away in pride (22:9), who disputed without knowledge (22:8), and who were enraged at the thought that God would help someone other than them (22:15). Their defining posture was that they would not be managed.

The implement named here is the one used on what will not be managed. There is an exactness to that which the Qur'an achieves repeatedly and which is easy to miss: the punishment is not an arbitrary tariff but a mirror. The arrogant are ruled by the instrument of compulsion; the man who would not be led is driven.

**What the Verse Refuses to Describe**

Notice how much is left out. The rods are named; the beating is not. No blow lands in this verse, nobody is struck in front of the reader, and no sound is reported.

The restraint is characteristic of the Qur'an's eschatological vocabulary and worth contrasting with what it *does* specify. The Qur'an is precise about the instruments — garments, water, rods, chains, depths — and almost entirely silent about the violence done with them. What is described is the equipment of the place and the state of the people in it, which is quite different from narrating their pain.

The effect is that the reader's imagination is given materials and then left alone with them, which is more forceful than any description could be. A text that spelled out the beating would have set a limit to it. A text that says *and for them are hooked rods of iron* sets no limit at all.

**The Allegorical Reading and Its Limits**

Some commentators, among them al-Aj, read the rods as a description of the soul's condition rather than of a place: the malicious soul, unable to see past the surface of this world, is beaten back from rising by the hooked irons of its own belief in its self-sufficiency, and returned continuously to suffering by its pride.

Read as application, that interpretation is sound and pastorally useful. Read as a replacement for the plain sense, it fails the sūrah's own argument, and it is worth saying why. Verses 5 to 7 of this sūrah were spent establishing that the dead are raised bodily, from dust, from wombs, from graves. A commentary that spiritualizes the punishment is in tension with a sūrah that has just expended that much argument on the resurrection of bodies.

The two readings can be held in the right order. The Fire is real, and the self-exaltation that puts a person there is also real, and the second is what makes the first intelligible rather than arbitrary.

**The Four Verses as One Picture**

Verses 19 to 22 belong together and are best read as a single movement with four beats. First the garment: something to wear, cut to fit. Then the water: poured from above, reaching the inside and the outside. Then the rods: the standing equipment of the place. Then the attempt to leave and the return.

The progression is from the personal to the total. A garment is on the skin; water gets under it; the rods are what is waiting regardless; and the last verse closes the door. None of the four contradicts the others, and together they answer the objection that the Qur'an's hell is vague. It is not vague. It is specific about everything except the thing readers most want specified, which is an ending.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:22

> Whenever they try to escape from Hell—out of anguish—they will be forced back into it, ˹and will be told,˺ “Taste the torment of burning!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Desire to Leave Never Stops**

*Kullamā arādū an yakhrujū minhā min ghammin* — every time they want to get out of it, out of anguish. The construction is *kullamā*, whenever, every time: a repeated action, not a single attempt.

That little word is the verse's most frightening detail, and it is easy to read past. The punishment is not described as a state into which a person settles and grows accustomed. It is described as a loop: the desire rises, the attempt is made, the return happens, and the desire rises again. Whatever else the Fire does, it does not dull the wish to leave it.

*Ghamm* is the word for grief that presses in, the distress of being enclosed. It is the emotion of a trap, and its presence here explains the attempt. These are not people exploring; they are people trying to get out because they cannot bear to be in.

**Returned, and by Whom?**

*Uʿīdū fīhā* — they are returned into it. The verb is passive, in the plural, with no agent named.

The grammar is worth pausing on. Elsewhere in the Qur'an the keepers of the Fire speak and act, and al-Ṭabarī takes the accompanying command as theirs. Here, however, the return is stated without a guard, without a blow, and without a hand. The Fire is described as running on its own logic: a person who wants out is in, and wanting is not a mechanism of exit.

That impersonality is part of what makes the description unbearable. A prison with a warder can be negotiated with, resisted, or escaped. A state of affairs cannot.

**The Words That Accompany the Return**

Then comes the address: *dhūqū ʿadhāba al-ḥarīq* — taste the punishment of the burning. Al-Ṭabarī and others take this as spoken by the custodians of the Fire, which fits the pattern of the Qur'an's judgment scenes, where the announcement is made to the person rather than merely happening to him.

The imperative *dhūqū* picks up the vocabulary the sūrah used in verse 9, where God said *We will make them taste* the torment of burning. The same verb, in the same sūrah, eight verses apart — once as a warning of what is coming, once as the thing itself, now being said to a face.

The address is also, in its way, an explanation. The word *ḥarīq* is fire that burns and spreads. What is being tasted is not a temperature but a verdict, and the sentence tells the sufferer what he is experiencing so that he cannot experience it as accident.

**Why an Escape Attempt Appears in the Text At All**

Modern readers sometimes treat the attempt-and-return as a narrative detail, as though the Qur'an were describing a scene with a doorway in it. It is not that. The function of the *kullamā* construction is to state permanence in the only way a language can state it without a flat assertion.

The Qur'an could have said that they will remain there forever. It says instead that every attempt to leave is followed by a return, and it says it in a form that implies the attempts never end. The reader draws the conclusion, and drawing it is more forceful than being told. The same device appears throughout the Qur'an's descriptions of both destinations: rather than abstract statements of eternity, a repeated action that never resolves.

**"Returned" Is the Resurrection's Own Word**

*Uʿīdū* — they are returned — is from the same root the Qur'an uses for the resurrection of the dead. These people were returned to life, and now they are returned to the Fire.

The echo is deliberate and it is bleak. The verb that names the great hope of the sūrah's opening verses — that God will raise those in the graves (22:7) — reappears here with a different destination attached. The same act that brought them back now holds them in.

Read that way, the verse is the furthest point the sūrah reaches from its opening promise, and the distance is measured in one repeated root.

**The Keepers Who Ask Questions**

The Qur'an's Fire has custodians, and their words are recorded in two passages that illuminate this one. At the arrival, *“its keepers will ask them: ‘Did messengers not come to you from among yourselves, reciting to you the revelations of your Lord and warning you of the coming of this Day of yours?’ The disbelievers will cry, ‘Yes ˹indeed˺! But the decree of torment has come to pass against the disbelievers’”* (39:71). Sūrah al-Mulk records the same exchange in a harder form: *“almost bursting in fury. Every time a group is cast into it, its keepers will ask them, ‘Did a warner not come to you?’”* (67:8).

The question is always the same, and it is never "why did you do this?" It is "did anyone warn you?" The exchange establishes the thing that makes the punishment just: a warning arrived, was heard, and was refused. The confession recorded in 39:71 is an admission that it arrived.

That is the context in which the command to taste should be read. It is not the first word spoken to these people. It is the last.

**Anguish Is the Punishment, Not Its Side Effect**

The final thing to draw out is the placement of *min ghammin*, from anguish, inside the clause about wanting to leave.

The punishment here is not only what the Fire does to a body. It is the frustration of a will that keeps operating and never gets what it wants. The sufferer is intact enough to desire, to attempt, and to be disappointed — and that is the horror the verse is built around. A person who had been reduced to nothing would not be trying to get out.

This is consistent with the sūrah's whole anthropology. Human beings were described in verse 18 as the only part of creation that can be on either side of the line, and in verse 11 as creatures whose response to events decides them. What is preserved in the Fire is the thing that made them human to begin with: a will, still reaching, still refused.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:23

> ˹But˺ Allah will surely admit those who believe and do good into Gardens, under which rivers flow, where they will be adorned with bracelets of gold and pearls, and their clothing will be silk,

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Is Built as a Mirror of Verses 19 and 20**

The passage has been describing the disbelievers' sentence: garments of fire cut to measure, scalding water poured over the head, melting the insides and the skin. Verse 23 answers item by item.

Where they had garments cut out for them, these have garments of silk. Where boiling water ran over their heads, these have rivers running beneath them. Where their skins were melted, these are adorned with gold and pearl. The symmetry is exact and it is the whole design of the passage: two destinations described in the same vocabulary, so that the reader measures the difference rather than being told about it.

This is why the sūrah gave the punishment in such physical detail. It was never description for its own sake; it was the setting-up of a contrast, and the contrast is the point.

**Gold and Pearl**

*Yuḥallawna fīhā min asāwira min dhahabin wa-luʾluʾan* — they are adorned in it with bracelets of gold, and pearl. The verb is passive: they are adorned. Nobody in the Garden decorates himself.

The two materials carry a history. Gold, in this world, is what a person hoards; pearl is what a person dives for at risk. Neither is useful — you cannot eat gold and a pearl is a wound that hardened — and both are valued precisely because they are useless and hard to get. That is what makes them ornaments, and the verse puts them on the inhabitants as ornaments rather than handing them over as assets. The transfer from possession to decoration is the difference between wealth and honour.

The parallel descriptions elsewhere confirm the register: *“They will enter the Gardens of Eternity, where they will be adorned with bracelets of gold and pearls, and their clothing will be silk”* (35:33), and in the fuller account, *“There they will be adorned with bracelets of gold, and wear green garments of fine silk and rich brocade, reclining there on ˹canopied˺ couches”* (18:31).

**Silk**

*Wa-libāsuhum fīhā ḥarīr* — and their clothing in it is silk. The word is *ḥarīr*, and the same fabrics are named in the descriptions of the Garden elsewhere: *“dressed in fine silk and rich brocade, facing one another”* (44:53), and *“The virtuous will be ˹dressed˺ in garments of fine green silk and rich brocade, and adorned with bracelets of silver”* (76:21).

Two observations are worth making. The first is that clothing in the Qur'an is consistently the outward sign of an inward state, which is why the garments of fire in verse 19 and the silk in this verse are doing the same work in opposite directions. The second is that the material is described by texture rather than by cut or colour. What is promised is how it feels against the skin — the exact inversion of a punishment delivered to the skin.

**Why the Reward Is Described in the Language of This World**

The obvious philosophical difficulty is that a spiritual reward is being described in material terms, and the obvious answer is the one the tradition gives: the Qur'an can only describe what it has vocabulary for. A pleasure never experienced cannot be named, so the text names the nearest known thing and marks the description as approximate.

There is a second reason, though, and this sūrah has earned it. A sūrah that has argued for the resurrection of the body from embryos and dry fields cannot then describe the reward in terms that have nothing to do with bodies. The consistency is deliberate: the same creature that was raised is the creature that is dressed.

The descriptions are therefore neither literal in the flat sense nor merely symbolic. They are the truth about a state, rendered in the only images available, and addressed to a creature who will have a body when he gets there.

**How the Qur'an Builds These Pairs**

The matching-description technique used here is not unique to this sūrah, and recognizing it elsewhere sharpens the reading here. Sūrah al-Dukhān puts the two destinations four verses apart using the same method: for the punished, *“Like molten metal, it will boil in the bellies, like the boiling of hot water”* (44:45–46); for the rewarded, *“dressed in fine silk and rich brocade, facing one another”* (44:53). Belly and silk, in that order.

The consistency of the method tells us something about what the descriptions are for. They are not reports filed from another world; they are a rhetorical instrument for making a decision visible. A reader who is told only that the godly are rewarded and the wicked punished has received information. A reader who sees the garment of fire and then the garment of silk has been shown a difference he can hold in his mind, and the Qur'an prefers the second.

It also guards against a common error. Because the two descriptions are built from the same materials, no reader can suppose that one of them is literal and the other figurative. They stand or fall together.

**"Admit" — the Verb Returns**

*Yudkhilu* — He causes them to enter. The same verb the sūrah used in verse 14, in the same form, with the same condition attached: those who believe and do sound deeds.

The repetition settles a question that the preceding verses might have raised. The punishment of verses 19 to 22 was described almost entirely in the passive — garments cut, water poured, no agent named — which leaves a reader wondering who is doing this. The reward is not left in that form. God is named as the one who brings them in, and the asymmetry is intentional: judgment is the machinery, mercy has an author.

That is also why the verse's conditions have not changed since verse 14. Belief and sound action, and entry as something done to you by the One who wills it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:24

> for they have been guided to the best of speech, and they have been guided to the Commendable Path.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Guided to Speech**

*Wa-hudū ilā al-ṭayyibi mina al-qawli* — and they were guided to the good of speech. The verb is passive and the object is speech itself. What they have been given is not only a destination but a vocabulary.

The commentators proposed several referents, and the list is worth having because the disagreement is about emphasis rather than substance. Al-Ṭabarī took the guidance as inspiration toward these things in general. Others specified the testimony of faith — *lā ilāha illa Allāh* — or the phrase *al-ḥamdu li-Llāh*, praise belongs to God, or the Qur'an itself. Still others took the good speech as what will be spoken *in* the Garden: the greeting of welcome the inhabitants receive, and the words of the angels to them.

The readings are not exclusive, and the verse's phrasing is wide enough to hold all of them. What matters is the claim they share: the people of the Garden are people whose speech was set right. They said the true thing in this life, they will hear the true thing in the next, and both are described as guidance rather than achievement.

**The Good Word Elsewhere**

*Al-ṭayyib mina al-qawl* is a phrase with a history in the Qur'an. Sūrah Ibrāhīm builds a parable on it: *“Do you not see how Allah compares a good word to a good tree? Its root is firm and its branches reach the sky”* (14:24). The good word is pictured as something with roots and height, which is to say something alive and load-bearing rather than ornamental.

The paradisal application is made explicit in the scenes of arrival. *“And those who were mindful of their Lord will be led to Paradise in ˹successive˺ groups. When they arrive at its ˹already˺ open gates, its keepers will say, ‘Peace be upon you! You have done well, so come in, to stay forever’”* (39:73). The gates are already open, and the first thing that happens is speech.

**What the Inhabitants Say to Each Other**

The Qur'an's descriptions of the Garden are unusual among ancient accounts of the afterlife in one respect: they specify the conversation. Not only what is eaten and worn but what is said.

The inhabitants are greeted: *“the Gardens of Eternity, which they will enter along with the righteous among their parents, spouses, and descendants. And the angels will enter upon them from every gate”* (13:23). They are welcomed with a word — *“where they will be greeted with ‘Peace!’”* (14:23). The greeting is named as a divine greeting on the Day of meeting (33:44) and as a greeting from the Merciful Lord Himself (36:58). And what is absent is named as precisely as what is present: *“There they will never hear any idle talk—only ˹greetings of˺ peace”* (19:62), and *“never to hear any idle talk or lying therein”* (78:35).

The negative description is the more revealing half. A great deal of human misery is verbal — slander, lying, the noise of people competing — and the Qur'an promises its absence as a positive feature of the reward. Being guided to good speech means, among other things, being removed from bad speech permanently.

**"The Path of the Praised"**

*Wa-hudū ilā ṣirāṭi al-ḥamīd* — and they were guided to the path of the Praised.

*Al-Ḥamīd* is a divine name: the Praised, the One to whom praise belongs and who is praiseworthy in Himself. The commentators took "the path of the Praised" as the path of God, meaning the route that ends at the Garden — which is how the verse functions here, as the second half of a description of how these people got where they are.

The choice of this name over the others is not incidental. A path described as belonging to the Praised is a path whose end is praise: the praise the inhabitants will offer, and the praise they will be given in the greeting of peace. The destination is named by the sound that will fill it.

**The Verse Closes a Circle Opened in Verse 19**

Verses 19 through 24 form a single unit, and verse 24 is its last line. The punishment was described in four verses and the reward in two, and the reward's description ends not with a picture of the Garden but with an explanation of how the inhabitants got there: they were guided, to the right words and to the right road.

That ending is deliberate. If the passage had closed on silk and bracelets, the reader's last impression would be of the reward as a possession. Closing on guidance makes the last impression a relationship — these are people who were brought, twice over, to the thing they received.

It also completes the circle the sūrah opened in verse 16, where clear signs were sent down and guidance was said to be God's to give. Six verses of punishment and reward have been the illustration of that sentence.

**Why Guidance Is Mentioned Twice**

The verb *hudū* appears in both halves of the verse, and the repetition is the verse's argument.

Guidance, in the Qur'an's usage, is not the information a person receives once at the start. It is a continuous relation — and here it is said to extend to a person's words and to his direction, which between them cover what comes out of him and where he is going. Both are attributed to guidance rather than to his own management.

This is also the verse's quiet link back to verse 16, where the sūrah said that God guides whom He wills. Those who have arrived in the Garden are described in exactly those terms: they were guided, twice over, and the thing they were guided to was speech and a road. The disputants of verse 8 were described in the same grammar — without guidance — and the sūrah has spent the intervening verses showing where the two roads end.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:25

> Indeed, those who persist in disbelief and hinder ˹others˺ from the Way of Allah and from the Sacred Mosque—which We have appointed for all people, residents and visitors alike—along with whoever intends to deviate by doing wrong in it, We will cause them to taste a painful punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Charges, and the Second One Is Active**

The verse names two offences joined by a conjunction: disbelief, and turning people away from the way of God and from the Sacred Mosque.

The first is a state. The second is an activity, and the Arabic for it, *ṣadda ʿan*, carries an ambiguity the commentators noticed. It can mean that a person is himself diverted, or that he diverts others. Both senses fit the Makkan situation, and both are probably meant: the Quraysh were themselves turned from the revelation and worked to turn others from it, and the same verb covers the refusal and the campaign.

The sūrah has met this pattern before. Verse 9 described a man who turned his side away in order to lead others astray. The sin in that verse, as here, was not the wrong belief as such; it was the propagation of it, and the doubling of the sentence matches the doubling of the harm.

**"Which We Made Equal for All People"**

The parenthesis is the verse's most consequential clause: the Sacred Mosque, *which We appointed for mankind, equal are the resident in it and the visitor*.

This is a claim about ownership, and in seventh-century Makkah it was a radical one. Quraysh controlled the sanctuary, kept the keys, distributed the water of Zamzam, and derived both income and prestige from the traffic of pilgrims. The verse says that none of that gives them a title. The House is God's, and the man who lives next to it has no more right in it than the man who walked six hundred miles to reach it.

The sentence also does something subtler: it removes the custodians' standing to expel anyone. If the resident and the visitor are equal in the precinct, then barring the visitor is not an exercise of a right but a usurpation, and that is exactly the grievance the sūrah will name in verse 40.

**"Whoever Intends Deviation Therein with Wrongdoing"**

The third clause extends the offence from acts to intentions: *wa-man yurid fīhi bi-ilḥādin bi-ẓulmin* — and whoever intends, in it, deviation through wrongdoing.

The commentators differed over what the deviation is. Many read it as idolatry and the worship of anything other than God inside the precinct. Others took it as certain kinds of dishonest commercial dealing within the sanctuary. Both readings are credible, and the wording is general enough to hold both: *ilḥād* is a swerving, a leaning away from what is straight, and *ẓulm* is the wrong that results.

The striking feature is the verb. What is punished is *intending*. A religion of outward conformity would have required the act; this verse names the will. Theological and legal consequences were drawn from it in both directions — some holding that a resolved intention formed in the sanctuary is itself the offence, whether or not it is carried out — and the dispute turns on how literally the verb is pressed. [UNVERIFIED: I have not checked the specific positions attributed to the early authorities on whether the intention alone is punishable in the sanctuary; the point is cited here without a source.]

**Why the Sanctuary Makes the Offence Worse**

The logic of a *ḥaram* — a precinct set apart — is that it is a place of guaranteed safety. Hunting is forbidden inside it, fighting is forbidden inside it, and a man who has blood on his hands is supposed to be secure there.

Offences committed in such a place are therefore not merely offences with an unfortunate address. They are offences against the institution of safety itself, and the Qur'an treats them on that footing. Committing idolatry anywhere is the gravest wrong; committing it in the House that was purified for the worship of one God (22:26) is a different order of thing, because it uses the sanctuary for the precise purpose the sanctuary exists to exclude.

That is why the verse names the Sacred Mosque as an object of the hindrance rather than merely its location. The people being turned away are being turned away from a place as well as from a path.

**Who Was Listening When This Was Revealed**

The verse's threat lands differently once the setting is recalled. The believers had been expelled from Makkah and barred from the Sacred Mosque; the pilgrimage they were commanded to perform was being performed by their enemies at a sanctuary filled with idols.

A verse that says the precinct was made equal for resident and visitor alike, and that those who bar people from it will taste a painful punishment, is not a general maxim in that context. It is a verdict on the specific arrangement, and it contains an unstated promise: the people being barred will be the ones who return.

That is why the sūrah can move from this verse to the pilgrimage rites without a transition. The hearers were being told, in effect, that the House they could not reach was going to be theirs to purify.

**The Punishment Tasted**

The verse closes with *nudhiqhu min ʿadhābin alīm* — We will make him taste a painful punishment. The same verb the sūrah used in verse 9 for the man who turned aside to mislead others, and it will be repeated for the disbelievers in verse 57.

The consistency is the point. The sūrah has now named, in three separate places, the same offence of active diversion, and each time it answers with the same verb. Tasting, as noted earlier, is the sense that cannot be declined: this is a punishment that will be fully registered by the person it is administered to.

And the adjective is *alīm*, painful — the Qur'an's most frequent description of punishment, and one it applies to the Hereafter rather than to this life. The verse's threats are real in history — the sanctuary was cleared — but the sentence it announces is not a political forecast. It is the verdict that comes after.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:26

> And ˹remember˺ when We assigned to Abraham the site of the House, ˹saying,˺ “Do not associate anything with Me ˹in worship˺ and purify My House for those who circle ˹the Ka’bah˺, stand ˹in prayer˺, and bow and prostrate themselves.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Site Given, Not Chosen**

*Wa-idh bawwaʾnā li-Ibrāhīma makāna al-bayti* — and when We settled Abraham at the place of the House. The verb *bawwaʾa* means to give someone a place to alight, to install him somewhere and make it habitable for him.

The significance of the verb is that the location is not Abraham's discovery. He did not survey the valley and choose a site; he was placed at one. The sūrah that has already said that the Sacred Mosque was appointed for all people (22:25) now says that its founder was assigned its position, and the two together remove any human claim of ownership over it. The man who built it did not select it, and the people who inherited the custodianship did not build it.

The instruction that follows is therefore addressed to a custodian rather than to a proprietor, which is why the first command is not about construction at all.

**The Site Existed Before the Building Did**

The verse says that Abraham was settled at the *place* of the House — *makāna al-bayti* — not at the House itself. The building came later, and the Qur'an records its construction elsewhere in a phrase that makes the sequence explicit: *“And ˹remember˺ when Abraham raised the foundation of the House with Ishmael, ˹both praying,˺ ‘Our Lord! Accept ˹this˺ from us’”* (2:127).

The distinction matters for how the sanctuary is understood. What was assigned to Abraham was a location, and the location was assigned before a single stone was laid. The holiness of the place is therefore not a property of the structure, and it is not something a builder conferred on it. It survived the building's destruction and rebuilding more than once in the centuries that followed.

The verse that names the House as the first established for humanity puts the same point positively: *“Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah—a blessed sanctuary and a guide for ˹all˺ people”* (3:96).

**The First Command Is Not About Building**

*An lā tushrik bī shayʾan* — that you associate nothing with Me. Before any stone is laid, before any rite is specified, the founder is told to keep the worship of the place pure.

The priority is instructive for reading the rest of the passage. The House is not being established as a shrine that will then need to be defended against corruption; it is being established as a place whose entire purpose is the exclusion of rivals, and everything else — the circuit, the standing, the sacrifice — is secondary to that. When the sūrah speaks a few verses later about shunning the filth of idols (22:30), it is returning to this first instruction rather than adding a new one.

The parallel passage in Sūrah al-Baqarah records the same commission in the same order, with the purification commanded first and the worshippers named: *“We made the House a place of return and a sanctuary for the people, ˹saying,˺ ‘Take the standing-place of Abraham as a site of prayer.’ And We entrusted Abraham and Ishmael to purify My House for those who circle it, those who meditate in it, and those who bow and prostrate themselves”* (2:125).

**"Purify My House"**

*Wa-ṭahhir baytī* — and purify My House. The possessive is emphatic in the Arabic: the House is Mine.

The command to purify implies that the House can become unclean, and the commentators specified what the uncleanness is: idolatry first, and with it the doubts and innovations that attach themselves to a holy place. Purification is not a one-time act performed at the founding; it is the standing work of anyone who holds the place in trust.

The verse is also, read historically, a prophecy. The Kaʿbah that Abraham purified was filled with idols by the time of the Prophet ﷺ — three hundred and sixty of them by some accounts — and the command to purify was addressed to a man who had been dead for two and a half millennia. The sūrah is telling its audience that the restoration they are witnessing is the original commission being carried out again.

**Three Postures, and What They Cover**

*Li-l-ṭāʾifīna wa-l-qāʾimīna wa-l-rukkaʿi al-sujūd* — for those who circle, those who stand, and those who bow and prostrate.

Three groups are named, and between them they cover the two great forms of Islamic worship. The circling is the distinctive rite of the pilgrimage, the movement around the House that no other religion shares. The standing, bowing and prostrating are the components of the canonical prayer.

Putting the two in one sentence does something theologically important: it places the pilgrimage rite and the daily prayer on the same footing, as worship addressed to the same One, and it places both under the same condition — that the House be purified and no partner associated with God. A circumambulation performed among idols is not the rite this verse commissions.

The last pair, *al-rukkaʿ al-sujūd*, are also the words used of the whole creation in verse 18 of this sūrah. Everything in the heavens and the earth prostrates; the people who come to this House do it deliberately.

**Whose House It Remains**

The verse's possessive has a consequence worth stating directly. If the House is God's, then no one has the authority to determine who may enter it — which is precisely the principle the previous verse asserted when it declared the resident and the visitor equal in it.

The two verses are a unit. Verse 25 denies the custodians a property right in the precinct; verse 26 explains why, by showing that the founder himself was given a site and told to prepare it for anyone who would come and worship in the three named postures. Ownership never entered the transaction, at either end of the history.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:27

> Call ˹all˺ people to the pilgrimage. They will come to you on foot and on every lean camel from every distant path,

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Command Given to a Man With Nobody to Hear Him**

*Wa-adhdhin fī al-nāsi bi-l-ḥajji* — and proclaim the pilgrimage among the people. The verb is from the root related to calling out publicly, the language of a herald's announcement rather than of private instruction.

The circumstance is the difficulty the commentators dwelt on: Abraham was standing in an uninhabited valley. The reports preserved in the exegetical literature say that he asked how his voice could reach people who were not there, and was told to make the call and that the conveying of it was God's business. [UNVERIFIED: this account is reported by al-Ṭabarī and others without a chain I can verify in this session; it is cited as commentary rather than as established report.]

Whether or not one accepts the miraculous version, the structure of the verse is clear enough on its own. The command is issued, and the response is stated in the same breath as though it had already happened. The sūrah does not wait to see whether anyone comes.

**"They Will Come to You"**

*Yaʾtūka* — they will come to you. The future tense describes an event that, at the time of the command, had no visible cause in existence.

The grammatical confidence is the point of the verse. A pilgrimage is not a gathering that a person organizes; it is a gathering that is summoned into being by a call whose effectiveness does not belong to the caller. Every pilgrim who has arrived in that valley since is, on this verse's account, an answer to a command issued before there was anyone to answer it — which is why the tradition can speak of the pilgrims of every century being present in Abraham's call.

The sūrah also uses the address carefully: they come *to you*, Abraham. The founder is told that the traffic will arrive at him, and the promise is personal before it is institutional.

**"On Foot"**
