*Rijālan* — on foot, walking. The word is the plural of a man on his own feet, and it is placed first in the list of arrivals.

Several commentators drew from it the ruling that walking to the pilgrimage is more excellent than riding, and the inference is a natural one: the word is doing more than describing transport. Walking is the arrival of a person who has spent himself to get there, and the Qur'an's habit is to value the difficulty of an act as part of the act. A pilgrimage that costs nothing is not the thing this verse describes.

The detail also guarantees that the pilgrimage is not an elite observance. Anyone who can walk can come, and the poor arrive in the same manner as everyone else — a point the following verses reinforce by commanding that the sacrificial meat be fed to the destitute.

**"Every Lean Camel"**

*Wa-ʿalā kulli ḍāmirin* — and upon every lean one. The commentators took the animal as the camel, and the leanness as the effect of the journey: a beast arrives thin because it has come a long way.

The image is precise and quietly moving. What is promised to Abraham is not a procession of sleek animals but a procession of exhausted ones, each carrying a person who has already spent weeks getting there. The leanness is evidence, not decoration; it is how the verse says, without saying it, that the call will be answered from farther away than anyone could travel comfortably.

There is a rhetorical effect too. The valley had no water and no pasture. The promise of camels arriving is the promise of a route being opened across country that had none.

**What the Call Was Answering**

It is easy to read the command as though the pilgrimage were summoned into existence for its own sake. Abraham's own prayer, recorded in Sūrah Ibrāhīm, supplies the reason the valley was inhabited at all: *“Our Lord! I have settled some of my offspring in a barren valley, near Your Sacred House, our Lord, so that they may establish prayer. So make the hearts of ˹believing˺ people incline towards them and provide them with fruits, so perhaps they will be thankful”* (14:37).

The call of verse 27 is the answer to that prayer. A man left his family in an unwatered valley for the sake of the prayer, and asked that people's hearts be made to incline toward them. The pilgrimage is the mechanism by which the request was granted: hearts incline, and the valley fills.

That framing changes what the gathering is for. Pilgrims do not come to make a desert town prosperous; they come for the prayer and the rites, and the provision follows. The next verse will name both halves in a single sentence — benefits, and the mention of God's name — which is exactly the order of Abraham's own petition.

**"From Every Distant Pass"**

*Yaʾtīna min kulli fajjin ʿamīq* — they come from every deep ravine, every distant mountain pass. *Fajj* is a gap between mountains, a defile; *ʿamīq* is deep or far.

The geography is the theology here. Makkah sits in a hollow ringed by mountains, and the only way in is through passes. The verse pictures those passes filled, one after another, with people who set out from places the speaker could not have named.

That is what makes this the most universal sentence in the passage. The command in the first clause was *fī al-nāsi* — among humanity, all of it — and the response in the last clause matches it: not from every tribe of Arabia, but from every distant defile. The pilgrimage is described from its founding moment as a gathering of the whole inhabited world at a place chosen by nobody in it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:28

> so they may obtain the benefits ˹in store˺ for them, and pronounce the Name of Allah on appointed days over the sacrificial animals He has provided for them. So eat from their meat and feed the desperately poor.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That They May Witness Benefits for Themselves"**

*Li-yashhadū manāfiʿa lahum* — so that they may witness benefits belonging to them. The purpose of the pilgrimage is stated before any rite is described, and the word used is *manāfiʿ*, benefits: the ordinary term for what is useful and advantageous.

The commentators were explicit that the benefits are of both kinds. Al-Ṭabarī took the phrase as covering the trade and the markets that gather around the pilgrimage, and the attainment of God's good pleasure and reward in the Hereafter. Both are in the word, and nothing in the verse requires a choice between them.

That inclusion surprises readers who expect a religious gathering to be set apart from ordinary commerce, and the surprise is the point. The pilgrimage is not described as a withdrawal from ordinary life but as a place where ordinary life is transacted under better conditions: people meet, buy, sell, settle disputes, marry, and earn — and all of it is called a benefit they were summoned to witness.

**The Pilgrimage Is Not a Retreat From Commerce**

The Qur'an's willingness to name economic activity as part of the rite distinguishes it from the assumption that holiness requires distance from the market.

Sūrah al-Baqarah makes the same point from the other direction, in a verse that defends trade during the pilgrimage against those who considered it sinful. The logic of both texts is identical: what would corrupt a gathering is not buying and selling but the loss of the remembrance that frames them. The pilgrim is commanded to remember God *during the appointed days* (2:203), which presupposes that he is doing something else during those days from which the remembrance must not distract him — or rather, which the remembrance must pervade.

The practical consequence is a religion with no monastic withdrawal in it. The holiest days of the Islamic year are also, by this verse's own account, days of lawful gain.

**"Known Days"**

*Wa-yadhkurū isma Allāhi fī ayyāmin maʿlūmātin* — and mention the name of God during days that are known. The phrase is definite in the Arabic: days that are *known*, meaning identified, fixed, and established by the practice rather than specified here.

That is why the commentators had to supply the identification from elsewhere, and why they disagreed. Some took the known days as the first ten days of Dhū al-Ḥijjah, the month in which the pilgrimage falls. Others took them as the days of *tashrīq*, the eleventh, twelfth and thirteenth of the same month, which are named in Sūrah al-Baqarah: *“And remember Allah during ˹these˺ appointed days. Whoever departs swiftly on the second day is not sinful, neither are those who stay behind ˹till the third—seeking additional reward˺, so long as they are mindful ˹of their Lord˺”* (2:203).

The disagreement is not over doctrine but over which span the rite occupies, and both readings place the remembrance inside a defined window. The point of the phrase is that the religion is not left to private timing: there are days for this, and they are known.

**"Over What He Provided Them"**

*ʿAlā mā razaqahum min bahīmati al-anʿāmi* — over what He provided them of the grazing livestock. Two features of the phrasing deserve attention.

The first is the verb: *razaqa*, to provide. The animal is described as provision before it is described as property. A person who slaughters a sheep at the pilgrimage is not disposing of his own asset; he is returning a gift to its giver, and the verse's vocabulary keeps that relationship in view. Ownership language would have made the sacrifice a transaction. Provision language makes it a response.

The second is the restriction to *bahīmat al-anʿām*, the grazing livestock — camels, cattle, sheep and goats. The sacrifice is drawn from the domestic animals that a household depends on, not from game or from anything wild. What is offered is what a person lives by.

**"So Eat of It"**

*Fa-kulū minhā* — so eat of it. The command is in the imperative, and it follows directly on the sacrifice.

The background explains the force of it. Pre-Islamic practice among some of the Arab groups included a refusal to eat the meat of animals they had slaughtered themselves for sacrifice, treating it as given over. The verse overturns that: the sacrificer eats.

The ruling is permission rather than obligation, as the commentators note — the pilgrim may eat and is not required to — but the permission is itself the point. A religion in which the worshipper consumes part of his own offering is making a claim about the offering: it is not a payment to a hungry deity, and it is not destruction for its own sake. It is a meal.

**"And Feed the Poor and Wretched"**

*Wa-aṭʿimū al-bāʾisa al-faqīra* — and feed the wretched, the poor. Two nouns where one would have served, and they are not synonyms.

*Al-bāʾis* is the one overcome by hardship, the person whose distress shows; *al-faqīr* is the one without means. The first describes a condition that has befallen someone, the second a standing lack. Together they name the person who is both afflicted and broke — the pilgrim's opposite number, since the person standing at the sacrifice has arrived with an animal to slaughter and the recipient has nothing.

This is why the command is attached to the sacrifice rather than placed alongside it as a separate charity. The animal is killed, the family eats, and the destitute eat from the same animal on the same day. The rite produces the meal that feeds them.

**The Two Halves of a Single Act**

Taken together, the last two clauses of the verse describe one action with two outcomes: eating and feeding. Neither is optional in the verse's rhetoric, and neither makes sense without the other.

A sacrifice from which only the sacrificer eats is a dinner. A sacrifice from which only the poor eat is a donation. What the verse describes is a single table at which both sit, which is why the pilgrimage's central act is neither a private devotion nor a general alms. It is a meal shared by the person who could afford the animal and the person who could not, on days fixed by God, over an animal described as His provision.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:29

> Then let them groom themselves, fulfil their vows, and circle the Ancient House.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Then Let Them End Their Untidiness"**

*Thumma l-yaqḍū tafathahum* — then let them do away with their *tafath*. The word is unusual, and al-Rāzī made a point of saying so: *tafath* is not known from any source outside the exegetical literature. He reports that a Bedouin, asked what it meant, equated it with filth.

A word that the Arabs of the desert had to be asked about is a word whose meaning was fixed by the rite rather than by general usage, and the commentators' glosses agree on the substance: it refers to the state of physical neglect that belongs to the pilgrimage — uncut hair, untrimmed nails, the dust of the road — and to its removal at the end.

That reading is confirmed by the parallel legislation in Sūrah al-Baqarah, which describes the same moment in procedural detail: *“And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination”* (2:196). The shaving is the release, and the animal's arrival is what triggers it. The two verses together give the shape of the rite: a period of deliberate untidiness, ended by a specified act, at a specified time.

**What the Release Involves**

The commentators took the removal to cover the cutting or shaving of the hair and the trimming of the nails, along with whatever else belongs to leaving the state of consecration.

The sequence carries a meaning. For the duration of the pilgrimage the pilgrim does not attend to his appearance; he is, in a visible way, not at home in ordinary life. The release reverses that: he cleans himself and returns to the world he came from. The rite therefore has a shape that can be described in one sentence — you leave your ordinary condition, you stand before God in it, and you come back changed but presentable.

The physicality of it is the point. Nothing in this is a feeling. A person knows whether he has shaved his head, and the rite is built so that the markers of the sacred period are on the body rather than in the mind alone.

**"And Let Them Fulfil Their Vows"**

*Wa-l-yūfū nudhūrahum* — and let them fulfil their vows. The pilgrimage in the pre-Islamic and early Islamic periods was frequently undertaken in discharge of a vow: a person promised that if a thing were granted, he would go.

The command is general, and the commentators took it to include the vow of the pilgrimage itself as well as any vow made during it. The Qur'an's standing rule about vows is that they bind: a promise made to God is not a figure of speech, and this verse places the discharge of it inside the rite rather than before it.

There is a psychology here that is worth naming. A vow is a promise made under pressure, and the natural history of such promises is that they are forgotten once the pressure lifts. Putting the fulfilment inside the pilgrimage — the thing the person is already doing — and naming it as one of the three closing acts makes it harder to leave undone.

**"And Let Them Circle the Ancient House"**

*Wa-l-yaṭṭawwafū bi-l-bayti al-ʿatīq* — and let them go around the ancient House. The verse ends where the pilgrimage's central rite is: the circumambulation.

The adjective is *ʿatīq*, and it carries three senses at once, all of which the commentators noted. It means very old. It means freed or set free. And it means excellent, noble, choice — the sense used of a fine horse or a choice piece of property.

The first sense is the one the Qur'an states elsewhere: *“Surely the first House ˹of worship˺ established for humanity is the one at Bakkah—a blessed sanctuary and a guide for ˹all˺ people”* (3:96). The House is ancient in the specific sense that it is the first, which means the religion practised at it is not a new sect but the restoration of the oldest thing.

**"Freed" — and the Report Behind It**

On the second sense, several commentators preserve a report that the Kaʿbah was named the Freed House because no tyrant ever prevailed against it. [UNVERIFIED: this report is cited by Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī in the material available to me without a chain or number I could verify this session.]

Whether or not that etymology is accepted, the claim it makes is historically striking and largely borne out: the assault most famously repulsed is the one the Qur'an records in Sūrah al-Fīl, the expedition of the elephant from Yemen in the year of the Prophet's birth, which the sūrah says was turned back. The building has survived conquest, siege, fire, flood and the collapse of every dynasty that claimed it.

The theological reading of *ʿatīq* as "freed" therefore makes a claim about ownership that runs through this whole passage: the House is not the property of whoever holds the keys, and no power has ever successfully taken it.

**The Order of the Three Commands**

The three imperatives — end the untidiness, discharge the vows, circle the House — are given in an order that matches the rite's own movement.

First the body is restored: the state of consecration is closed and the pilgrim is clean again. Then the outstanding obligation is settled: what was promised is paid. Then the circuit is made: the pilgrim returns to the House, clean and unindebted, for the act that the sūrah's founder was told to prepare the House for in verse 26.

Nothing in the sequence is arbitrary, and its shape is the shape of the whole religion as this sūrah presents it: purification, obligation discharged, and then worship. The sūrah will restate the first and last of the three in different words before the passage closes.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:30

> That is so. And whoever honours the rituals of Allah, it is best for them in the sight of their Lord. The ˹meat of˺ cattle has been made lawful for you, except what has ˹already˺ been recited to you. So shun the impurity of idolatry, and shun words of falsehood.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"That Is So" — a Seal on the Passage**

*Dhālika* — that is how it is. The demonstrative closes everything from verse 26 onward: the assignment of the site to Abraham, the purification of the House, the call to the pilgrimage, the arrival on foot and on lean camels, the sacrifice, the release and the circuit.

The same word closed verse 1's argument in verse 6 and will close the passage on the symbols of God in verse 32. Each time it does the same work: it declares the matter settled. What has just been described is not a proposal or a preference; it is how things are, and the reader's relationship to it is one of acceptance rather than assessment.

Placed here, the word also separates the rites from what follows. Verses 31 onward are general instructions to the community; verses 26 to 29 were the pilgrimage's founding charter. *That* is how it is; now for you.

**"Whoever Magnifies the Sacred Things of God"**

*Wa-man yuʿaẓẓim ḥurumāti Allāhi* — and whoever magnifies the inviolable things of God. The word *ḥurumāt* is the plural of *ḥurmah*, and it carries two senses that are both in play: things that are sacred, and things that are forbidden — since what is sacred is precisely what must not be violated.

Al-Ṭabarī's definition gathers both: the sacred things are the pilgrimage, the lesser pilgrimage, Makkah and its precincts, and by extension everything that is prohibited in those places and times. The verse that follows two verses later speaks of the *shaʿāʾir*, the rituals or emblems, in almost the same sentence, and the two are best read together as one idea: outward markers whose inviolability is owed to God.

The verb is *yuʿaẓẓim*, to make great, to treat as great. Reverence here is an act performed by the worshipper rather than a property of the object. The sacred months, the precinct, the sacrificial animal — none of these is impressive in itself. What makes them *ḥurumāt* is that a person has been told to treat them as such.

**"It Is Better for Him With His Lord"**

*Fa-huwa khayrun lahu ʿinda rabbihi* — it is better for him, with his Lord.

The prepositional phrase carries the weight. The benefit is located *ʿinda rabbihi*, in God's keeping, not in the visible outcome. That is an honest qualification, because magnifying the sacred things often costs something: a pilgrimage costs money and months, a scruple about the sacred months costs advantage, a refusal to violate the precinct costs a tactical opportunity.

The verse does not pretend otherwise. It says the return is better and places the return where it cannot be audited in this life. A religion that promised immediate and visible profit for every observance would be making a claim that experience refutes daily. This one puts the ledger somewhere else.

**Cattle Permitted, With the Recited Exceptions**

*Uḥillat lakum al-anʿāmu illā mā yutlā ʿalaykum* — the grazing livestock have been made lawful to you except what is recited to you.

The exception points backward to revelation already given, and the prohibitions are listed in full in Sūrah al-Māʾidah: *“Forbidden to you are carrion, blood, and swine; what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah; what is killed by strangling, beating, a fall, or by being gored to death; what is partly eaten by a predator unless you slaughter it; and what is sacrificed on altars”* (5:3). The shorter statement in Sūrah al-Baqarah names the core: *“He has only forbidden you ˹to eat˺ carrion, blood, swine, and what is slaughtered in the name of any other than Allah”* (2:173).

The placement of this permission directly after the rites of sacrifice is not incidental. Having just commanded slaughter, the verse clarifies what may be slaughtered and eaten, and the emphasis falls on the name pronounced over the animal: *“Do not eat of what is not slaughtered in Allah’s Name. For that would certainly be ˹an act of˺ disobedience”* (6:121). The meat is lawful; the invocation is what makes it so.

**"Shun the Filth of Idols"**

*Fa-jtanibū al-rijsa mina al-awthāni* — so avoid the filth of the idols. The word *rijs* is the term used for filth that defiles, and it is applied here to the objects themselves rather than to the act of worshipping them.

Sūrah al-Tawbah draws the legal consequence from the same vocabulary: *“Indeed, the polytheists are ˹spiritually˺ impure, so they should not approach the Sacred Mosque after this year”* (9:28). The impurity is not physical dirt — idols are often well-kept objects — but a defilement that attaches to the person who has contact with them, and the verb *ijtanibū*, keep away from, prescribes distance rather than careful handling.

**"And Shun False Speech"**

*Wa-jtanibū qawla al-zūri* — and avoid the speech that is false. The same verb governs both objects, which places the two offences on the same footing.

That pairing is the verse's most striking move. Idolatry is the gravest sin in the Qur'an; lying about God would seem to belong to a different scale entirely. The verse puts them together, and the reason is that the two are the same act in different registers. Idolatry is a false claim about God rendered in stone; false speech is the same claim rendered in words, and the words are what keep the stones standing.

The Qur'an gives specimens of the speech it means: *“those who take other lords besides Him, ˹saying,˺ ‘We worship them only so they may bring us closer to Allah’”* (39:3), and *“And they attribute ˹angels as˺ daughters to Allah—glory be to Him!— the opposite of what they desire for themselves”* (16:57). Neither is a lie about a neighbour. Both are lies about God, told to justify an arrangement.

A verse that commands purity in worship and truthfulness in speech in a single breath is describing a religion with two non-negotiable sides: the direction of the worship, and the honesty of the account given of it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:31

> Be upright ˹in devotion˺ to Allah, associating none with Him ˹in worship˺. For whoever associates ˹others˺ with Allah is like someone who has fallen from the sky and is either snatched away by birds or swept by the wind to a remote place.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ḥanīf: a Word the Qur'an Had to Reclaim**

The verse opens by describing the required posture as that of *ḥunafāʾa lillāh* — ḥanīfs for God. The word is one of the Qur'an's most consequential borrowings, and its history is part of its meaning.

In pre-Islamic usage a *ḥanīf* appears to have denoted someone who turned away from the established cult toward the worship of the One God — a seeker outside the tribal system, and outside Judaism and Christianity as well. The Qur'an adopts the term and binds it to Abraham: *“Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘No! We follow the faith of Abraham, the upright—who was not a polytheist’”* (2:135).

The significance is that the word describes a direction rather than a membership. A ḥanīf is not a person who has joined something; he is a person who has turned away from something. That is exactly the sense this verse needs, since it follows five verses of pilgrimage legislation and is about to insist that the rites mean nothing without it.

**The Condition Stated Twice**

*Ghayra mushrikīna bihi* — not associating partners with Him. The phrase is a second negation added to a first: be upright for God, *not* associating. The Qur'an regularly states monotheism twice, positively and then negatively, and the redundancy is not stylistic.

The reason is that the positive statement alone can be satisfied by a person who affirms one God and keeps his other objects of reliance. The negation closes that gap. It is possible to say that God is one and to live as though He were not, and the second phrase is what catches that.

Here the double statement is doing specific work: it is addressed to people who are about to perform the rites of the pilgrimage, a rite inherited from Abraham, at a sanctuary that had been filled with idols. Performing it correctly requires both the action and the refusal.

**The Parable of the Fall**

*Fa-ka-annamā kharra mina al-samāʾi* — it is as though he fell from the sky. The comparison is to a falling body, and the point of the image is helplessness.

A man who falls from a height has no control over what happens to him next. He did not choose the descent once it began, he cannot alter its speed, and he cannot decide how he lands. The commentators took the image in exactly this sense: the associator has put himself into a state in which he is acted upon rather than acting.

The choice of falling, rather than of being wounded or being lost, also carries a claim about the starting point. You cannot fall from the ground. The parable assumes a height the person was at — an original uprightness, a natural orientation toward the One God — and the association is what dislodged him from it.

**Two Endings, and Neither Is a Landing**

Then the image forks: *fa-takhṭafuhu al-ṭayru aw tahwī bihi al-rīḥu fī makānin saḥīq* — the birds snatch him away, or the wind sweeps him down into a far-off place.

The first ending was read by al-Rāzī and others as the condition of a soul exposed after its fall: vulnerable, seized by whatever is circling, torn apart by devils that had no claim on him until he descended. The second was read as the soul carried further by its own caprice and appetites, blown along past the point where it could have stopped.

The two endings are not alternatives in the sense of a choice. They are the two things that happen to a person who has detached himself. Outside the protection of the orientation he was made for, he is either taken or drifted — and a place that is *saḥīq*, remote and deep, is somewhere no one arrives at by accident.

**Why the Image Comes After the Rites**

The placement is the verse's final argument. Verses 26 to 30 gave the pilgrimage: the site, the call, the arrival, the sacrifice, the release, the circuit, the sacred things to be honoured and the false speech to be shunned.

Verse 31 says what all of it is worth without the turning. A person can perform every one of those rites and be falling, which is why the ḥanīf language was necessary: the religion being described is not a set of observances with monotheism attached, but a turning to which the observances are secondary.

**The Parable Describes a Condition, Not a Crime**

Notice what the associator is *not* compared to. He is not compared to a rebel, an enemy combatant, a criminal or a liar — though the sūrah has used all of those categories for other people.

He is compared to a man who has fallen and is now helpless. That is a diagnosis rather than an indictment, and the difference has consequences. A rebel is someone you fight; a falling man is someone you try to catch. The Qur'an's missionary posture toward idolaters follows from this parable: the associator is in mortal danger and, until the fall is complete, is still reachable.

This is consistent with the sūrah's own method. The argument of verses 5 to 7 was addressed to people *in doubt*, and reasoned with them rather than pronouncing on them. The parable of verse 31 does the same thing in imagery: it says what has happened to such a person, and leaves the door open for him to be told.

**The Wind and the Birds Are Not Agents**

One last detail matters here: in both endings, what carries the man off is not a person. The birds and the wind are the ordinary hazards of the air, and the verse does not populate the parable with a captor.

That absence is theologically important. Nobody is dragging the associator anywhere. He fell, and what happens to him afterwards is what happens to anything that falls. The punishment in this parable is not inflicted; it is what a fall is.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:32

> That is so. And whoever honours the symbols of Allah, it is certainly out of the piety of the heart.

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Second Seal on the Passage**

*Dhālika* — that is how it is. The same demonstrative closed verse 30, and here it closes the second half of the same instruction: the lawfulness of cattle, the impurity of idols, the falsehood of speech, and the parable of the falling man.

The repetition is structural. The sūrah is marking the end of a unit the way a scribe marks a paragraph, and both seals are followed by a statement of principle rather than by further regulation. Verse 30 ended with what to avoid; verse 32 ends with where the observance actually lives.

**Shaʿāʾir: Emblems, Not Merely Rituals**

*Wa-man yuʿaẓẓim shaʿāʾira Allāhi* — and whoever magnifies the emblems of God. The word *shaʿāʾir* is broader than the word rendered "rituals."

Al-Qurṭubī connected it to the root *sh-ʿ-r*, which carries the sense of knowing and of being conscious of something, and on that basis described the *shaʿāʾir* as those things in which God has placed a means by which He makes Himself known or teaches. That is why the term covers not only the rites themselves but the places at which they are performed and the animals with which they are carried out.

Sūrah al-Baqarah gives a concrete instance: *“Indeed, ˹the hills of˺ Ṣafa and Marwah are among the symbols of Allah. So whoever performs the pilgrimage or minor pilgrimage, let them walk between ˹the two hills˺”* (2:158). Two low hills are called emblems of God, which settles the question of what kind of thing an emblem is. It is not intrinsically impressive. It is a marker that points.

**"It Is From the Reverence of Hearts"**

*Fa-innahā min taqwā al-qulūb* — indeed it is from the reverence of hearts. The sentence is the verse's whole claim, and the sūrah has been building toward it since verse 30's command to shun false speech.

The tradition locates reverence in the same place. Abū Hurayrah reported that the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said, "Reverence is here," and pointed to his chest three times; in the same report he said that God does not look at your bodies or your faces but looks at your hearts. It is recorded in *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (2564).

The placement of *qulūb*, hearts, at the end of the sentence gives it the emphasis. The question the verse answers is where an action's value is registered, and the answer is not the action. Magnifying an emblem is outward; what makes it count is the state of the person doing it, and that state is invisible.

**The Sūrah's Running Argument With Ritualism**

The sūrah has been circling this point since verse 11. The man on the brink performed the outward acts of the religion and was toppled by the first trial. The man of verse 12 called upon something with the right vocabulary and the wrong object. Verse 9 described a man who turned his shoulder away to lead others astray.

All three are people whose religion was entirely available on the surface, and the sūrah has been dismantling that possibility one figure at a time. Verse 32 states the principle the figures illustrated: what God receives from a rite is the state of the person performing it.

The warning has a particular edge here because the rites in question were inherited. The pilgrimage was not invented by the revelation; it was Abraham's, and the Arabs had been performing a corrupted version of it for centuries. A restored rite performed by habit is the most likely failure mode of any religion, and this verse is the sūrah's guard against its own success.

**What Magnification Looks Like in Practice**

The commentators disagreed usefully over what magnifying the emblems means concretely, and both readings are worth keeping.

Al-Rāzī took it as an attitude: to magnify the rites is to treat the worship of God through the pilgrimage as a weighty matter rather than a formality. Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī took it more materially, in the context of the sacrificial animals: that they should be well fed, sound and good-looking, and that a sick or injured animal should not be offered.

The second reading is the more searching, because it tests the first. A person who believes the rite is weighty will bring an animal he is not ashamed of, and the verse that was rejected in Sūrah al-Baqarah sets the standard: *“He replied, ‘Allah says, “It should have been used neither to till the soil nor water the fields; wholesome and without blemish”’”* (2:71). What is offered is measured by what it cost.

**The Verse's Warning About Outward Performance**

Read in context, verse 32 is the sūrah's guard against its own legislation. Verses 26 to 31 have given a great deal of outward detail: a site, a call, a circuit, a sacrifice, days, animals, a place of slaughter, and a prohibition on false speech. Every one of those can be performed by somebody whose heart is somewhere else entirely.

The sūrah knows this, which is why it interrupts itself. The rites are real and required, and the thing that makes them worship is not in the rites. A pilgrimage performed without reverence is a journey; a sacrifice performed without it is a slaughter.

The point is not that the outward act is dispensable — the sūrah has just spent six verses insisting on it — but that the inward state is what the outward act is for, and a religion that gets the first without the second has the shell of the thing it was given.

There is a corollary that runs in the other direction, and the sūrah states it by implication. If reverence is what reaches God, then the person who possesses the reverence but cannot perform the rite is not excluded from what the rite was for. The sūrah opens with the Hour and closes with the command to strive; in between it legislates an enormous amount of outward detail, and it interrupts itself here to prevent any reader from concluding that the detail is the substance.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:33

> You may benefit from sacrificial animals for an appointed term, then their place of sacrifice is at the Ancient House.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Animal Has a Life Before It Has a Destination**

*Lakum fīhā manāfiʿu ilā ajalin musamman* — for you in them are benefits, until a stated term. The sentence grants use before it imposes an end.

The commentators took the benefits as the ordinary yield of a living animal: its milk, its wool, its back to ride. The sacrificial animal is not withdrawn from use the moment it is dedicated. It continues to work, feed and carry until the day it is slaughtered, and that interim is described as a benefit belonging to the owner.

The ruling corrects a specific practice. Pre-Islamic custom includedAnimals set aside for idols — the *baḥīrah*, *sāʾibah*, *waṣīlah* and *ḥām* — whose milk and backs were declared forbidden to their owners. Sūrah al-Māʾidah rejects these categories outright: *“Allah has never ordained the ˹so-called˺ baḥîrah, sâ'ibah, waṣîlah, and ḥâm camels. But the disbelievers just fabricate lies about Allah, and most of them lack understanding”* (5:103).

**A Typo in the Custom, and What the Verse Does About It**

The pre-Islamic prohibition was not simple superstition; it was superstition with an economic shape. An animal dedicated to a deity was removed from the human economy while still consuming pasture, and the more a family dedicated, the poorer it became — which is precisely the mechanism the Qur'an dismantles here.

The verse's answer is to reverse the direction of the dedication. The animal is not taken out of use; it stays in use, and the use is called a benefit rather than a theft. What belongs to God in the transaction is the slaughter at the end, not the animal's whole life.

This is consistent with the vocabulary of verse 28, where the sacrificial animal was described as *what He provided them*. Provision is for use. A gift that may not be touched is not provision, and the Qur'an's legislation throughout refuses to create categories of untouchable things.

**"Until a Stated Term"**

*Ilā ajalin musamman* — to a term that is named, appointed, specified. The phrase appears throughout the Qur'an for deaths, debts, pregnancies and nations, and its use here puts the animal's life inside the same framework.

The term is stated, which means it is known: the animal is dedicated to a particular sacrifice on a particular occasion. Dedication in this religion is not a vague aspiration. It creates a dated obligation, and the benefit clause is bounded by it — you may use the animal *until*, and the *until* is what stops use from becoming indefinite postponement.

**"Then Their Place of Sacrifice Is at the Ancient House"**

*Thumma maḥilluhā ilā al-bayti al-ʿatīq* — then its place of release is at the Ancient House. The word *maḥill* is the place where something becomes permissible, where the prohibition lifts and the act is completed.

Two things are being said at once. The first is spatial: the sacrifice happens at the sanctuary and not anywhere else. The second is temporal and legal: the animal, which has been set apart, reaches its moment of release only there. Until then it is, in a qualified way, not entirely ordinary property.

The concentration of sacrifice at one place is unusual and worth reflecting on. A rite performed at a single sanctuary cannot be performed privately, which means it is public, regulated, and shared — a single location makes the act visible to a community rather than to a household.

**What the Benefit Clause Excludes**

The permission is for benefits, and the tradition was careful about which ones. Milk, wool, riding and similar yields were included; sale was not, and neither was substituting another animal for the one dedicated.

The distinction is not arbitrary. A benefit is something the animal produces while remaining the animal that was dedicated. A sale converts it back into ordinary wealth, and a substitution changes what was vowed. Both undo the dedication rather than use the dedicated thing, and the verse's boundary — benefits *until a stated term* — is drawn to permit the first and refuse the second.

There is a principle here that runs through the sūrah's treatment of property generally. Nothing a person owns is his in the absolute sense: cattle are lawful *except what has been recited* (22:30), wealth is *what We provided them* (22:35), and a dedicated animal may be used but not liquidated. Ownership in this sūrah is always real, always limited, and always referred back to the giver.

**A Term Appointed, Like Everything Else Here**

The phrase *ajal musammā* — a stated term — has already appeared in this sūrah, in verse 5, for the developing child in the womb. The repetition is not incidental.

The sūrah's world is built out of appointed terms. Pregnancies run to one, nations run to one (7:34), lives end at one, and now a dedicated animal's usefulness expires at one. Nothing in this sūrah exists in an open-ended present; everything is moving toward a date, and the date is what gives the interval its shape.

That is why the permission to use the animal is framed as it is. The benefit is real and lawful, and it is bounded. A person who milks a sacrificial camel for a season is enjoying something that will stop at a known moment, and the knowledge is part of the point: the animal was never entirely his, and the date on which it stops being usable at all is the date it was always heading for.

**How the Jurists Reasoned From It**

The legal literature derived fine distinctions from this verse, and one of them shows how carefully it was read: riding the sacrificial animal was permitted, but only where necessary, since the permission granted in the verse is for benefits generally while the animal's destination is fixed.

That reasoning captures the verse's balance. Use is permitted; ownership is qualified; the destination is not negotiable. A person who dedicates an animal still milks it and may ride it if he must, and he cannot decide to keep it.

The balance is the same one the sūrah struck in verse 30, when it made cattle lawful while demanding that the invocation be God's, and in verse 32, when it located the rite's value in the heart. Property in this sūrah is never absolute, never worthless, and never free of the purpose it was given for.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:34

> For every community We appointed a rite of sacrifice so that they may pronounce the Name of Allah over the sacrificial animals He has provided for them. For your God is only One God, so submit yourselves to Him ˹alone˺. And give good news ˹O Prophet˺ to the humble:

**Expanded Commentary**

**Every Community Got a Rite**

*Wa-li-kulli ummatin jaʿalnā mansakan* — and for every community We appointed a rite. The word *mansak* refers here to sacrifice specifically, though some commentators took it as religious ritual generally and others as the place at which the ritual is performed.

Taken as sacrifice, the claim is anthropologically striking: there has never been a community without it. The Qur'an is not embarrassed by the universality of the practice it prescribes; it explains it. Sacrifice was given to every people, which means the institution is not a tribal custom the Arabs invented and not a concession to them.

Sūrah al-Māʾidah states the same principle for law and way of life: *“To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you with what He has given ˹each of˺ you”* (5:48). Sūrah al-Naḥl states it for messengers: *“We surely sent a messenger to every community, saying, ‘Worship Allah and shun false gods’”* (16:36), and Sūrah Yūnus for the warning itself: *“And for every community there is a messenger”* (10:47).

**Why the Rite Was Given: to Mention the Name**

*Li-yadhkurū isma Allāhi ʿalā mā razaqahum min bahīmati al-anʿām* — so that they mention God's name over what He provided them of the grazing livestock.

The purpose clause is the verse's heart. The rite exists so that the name is pronounced, and the name is pronounced over something described as provision. Two ideas are fused: the animal came from God, and the act of killing it is to be performed with God named over it.

This is what separates sacrifice from slaughter in the Qur'an's economy. An animal can be killed for food anywhere; what makes an act a rite is the invocation and the dedication. Every community had the institution; what the Qur'an insists on is the name spoken over it, which is why verse 30 placed the prohibition on meat dedicated to other than God at the centre of its dietary ruling.

**"Your God Is One God"**

*Fa-ilāhukum ilāhun wāḥidun* — your God is one God. The sentence is the conclusion the universality of the rite was building toward: every community was given the same institution because every community was addressing the same God.

*Wāḥid* carries both senses that the English "one" carries — single, and the same. The commentators noted both: God is one, and the God of every community is identical. That is why the diversity of rites is not evidence against unity: the plurality was ordained (5:48), and the object is not plural.

The Qur'an states the first half of this claim in its baldest form elsewhere: *“Your God is ˹only˺ One God. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him—the Most Compassionate, Most Merciful”* (2:163).

**"So Submit to Him"**

*Fa-lahu aslimū* — so to Him submit. The imperative follows directly from the oneness, and the inference is tight: if the God is one and the same, the appropriate response is a single posture of surrender rather than the negotiation of rival claims.

The verb is *aslama*, to hand oneself over whole, and it is the root of the word *islam*. The religion's own name is a description of an act rather than of a tribe or a founder, which is why the Qur'an can attribute it to Abraham and to every messenger before him.

**The Good News Is Addressed to the Humble**

*Wa-bashshir al-mukhbitīna* — and give good news to the humble. The word *mukhbit* is unusual and dense: it describes someone who is quiet, lowly, settled — a person whose interior has gone still.

The choice of this word over "the believers" or "the righteous" is pointed. The verse has just said that God is one and that everyone should submit to Him; the people who actually do it are described by a quality of temperament rather than by a credential. Not the learned, not the powerful, not the scrupulous — the ones whose hearts have stopped arguing.

That is also why the good news is given *to* them through the Prophet rather than announced about them. They are the audience that can hear it, and the sūrah will describe them in four particulars.

**The Argument the Verse Settles With the Quraysh**

The claim that every community was given a rite is also an answer to the objection the Qur'an records from its opponents more than once: that they follow what they found their fathers doing.

If inherited practice settled the question, every community would be right by definition and no messenger would ever have been sent against anyone. The Qur'an's response is not to deny the inheritance but to relocate the authority. The rite is inherited; the name pronounced over it is not. What distinguishes a sacrifice from a slaughter is not its antiquity but the God it is addressed to.

That is why the verse moves from the universality of the rite straight to the oneness of God in the same sentence. The plurality of rites is granted and rendered harmless in one move: many rites, one name, and the name is what counts.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Argument**

Verse 34 is a hinge in the passage. It closes the legislation on sacrifice by widening it to all of human history, and by grounding the whole institution in the oneness of God. What follows is no longer about animals. Verses 35 to 37 will describe the people this legislation produces, and the description will begin where this verse ends — with the interior state.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:35

> those whose hearts tremble at the remembrance of Allah, who patiently endure whatever may befall them, and who establish prayer and donate from what We have provided for them.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Marks, and the First Is a Tremor**

The humble of verse 34 are now described, and the description begins with a physical event: *alladhīna idhā dhukira Allāhu wajilat qulūbuhum* — those who, when God is mentioned, their hearts tremble.

The verb *wajila* is the word for fear that shows. It is not calm awe and it is not terror; it is the involuntary response of a person who has been reminded of something enormous and cannot stay unaffected. The Qur'an puts this at the head of the list, which tells the reader that the religion begins in a reaction rather than in a decision.

The same phenomenon is described elsewhere in the same physical vocabulary: *“which causes the skin ˹and hearts˺ of those who fear their Lord to tremble, then their skin and hearts soften at the mention of ˹the mercy of˺ Allah”* (39:23). Note the sequence in that verse — trembling, then softening. The fear is not the destination; it is the first movement, and it is followed by relief.

**The Body Keeps Score**

The second verse often paired with this one is about a different organ: *“When they listen to what has been revealed to the Messenger, you see their eyes overflowing with tears for recognizing the truth”* (5:83).

The pairing is deliberate in the Qur'an's psychology. Faith is not described as an interior state with occasional outward effects; it is described as a state that registers on the body — hearts that tremble, skin that softens, eyes that fill. The commentators who wrote on this verse, including al-Qushayrī, took the trembling as increasing in proportion to a person's awareness of God's self-disclosure in the world, which makes it a function of attention rather than of temperament.

The significance for reading the list is that none of these four marks is purely private. A person cannot verify his own humility, but he can notice whether the mention of God moves him.

**The Second Mark: Endurance Without Commentary**

*Wa-l-ṣābirīna ʿalā mā aṣābahum* — and those who are patient over what befalls them. The preposition is *ʿalā*, which carries the sense of staying on top of something, holding one's place under a load.

This is the quality the sūrah's earlier figures conspicuously lacked. The man on the brink of verse 11 was content when good came to him and flipped over onto his face when a trial did; the man of verse 15 was consumed by rage at the delay of help. Patience is being named here as the specific difference between the humble and those the sūrah has already described.

The phrasing *mā aṣābahum* — whatever strikes them — is deliberately wide. No category of suffering is excluded and no cause is assigned. The verse does not say they are patient over what they deserve, or over what they understand.

**The Third and Fourth Marks: Prayer and Giving**

*Wa-l-muqīmī al-ṣalāti wa-mimmā razaqnāhum yunfiqūna* — and those who establish the prayer, and spend from what We provided them.

The two are always joined in the Qur'an, and the joining is the point. Prayer is what a person does about God; giving is what he does about other people, and the religion insists that neither is the whole of it.

Two details of the phrasing deserve attention. The prayer is *established* — *muqīmī*, set up and kept up — rather than merely performed, which is the language of maintenance rather than of occasional acts. And the giving is *mimmā razaqnāhum*, from what We provided them: the same provision language used of the sacrificial animals in verses 28 and 34. What is given away was never earned outright, and the sūrah says so in the same breath as it commands the giving.

**Why the Humble Are Described Just Before the Fighting Begins**

The placement of this description is one of the sūrah's most deliberate choices, and it is easy to miss. Three verses from here, permission to fight will be granted (22:39), and the community that receives it is the community described here.

The order is a safeguard. A group that has been expelled from its homes and is about to be authorized to take up arms is first told what kind of people it is supposed to be: moved by the mention of God, patient under what befalls them, constant in prayer, open-handed with what they were given. Power is being granted to people whose defining marks are interior.

Read the other way round, the sequence is a warning. The sūrah is describing the people whose good news the Prophet is told to announce, and the next passage will describe what happens when a community is established in the land (22:41). The risk being guarded against is not defeat. It is the loss of the tremor.

**What the List Is For**

Read as a set, the four marks describe a shape rather than a checklist. The humble person is moved by the mention of God, stays in place under what happens to him, keeps the prayer up, and lets go of what he was given.

The order matters. The list begins inward and ends outward: a reaction, an endurance, an act of worship, an act of distribution. A person cannot start at the end of it — a generosity that does not come from the tremor is expenditure, and a prayer maintained without endurance is a habit that lasts as long as comfort does.

That is why the sūrah places this description immediately after the legislation on sacrifice. The sacrifices and the rites were the outward apparatus; these four are the people the apparatus is supposed to produce. The sūrah will say so in its plainest form in the verse after next.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:36

> We have made sacrificial camels ˹and cattle˺ among the symbols of Allah, in which there is ˹much˺ good for you. So pronounce the Name of Allah over them when they are lined up ˹for sacrifice˺. Once they have fallen ˹lifeless˺ on their sides, you may eat from their meat, and feed the needy—those who do not beg, and those who do. In this way We have subjected these ˹animals˺ to you so that you may be grateful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Camels as Emblems**

*Wa-l-budna jaʿalnāhā lakum min shaʿāʾiri Allāhi* — and the sacrificial camels, We have made them for you among the emblems of God. The word *budn* refers to the large sacrificial animals; the jurists differed over whether it is restricted to camels or includes cattle, and both readings are defensible.

The classification is the point. An animal is being called a *shaʿīrah*, an emblem of God — the same word the sūrah used two verses before for the thing whose magnification comes from the reverence of hearts (22:32), and the same word used in Sūrah al-Baqarah for the two hills of Ṣafa and Marwah (2:158).

A camel is therefore in the same category as a hill: an ordinary creature that has been made to point at something. Nothing about the animal changes when it is dedicated. What changes is that it now carries a meaning, and the meaning is not the owner's to redefine.

**"In Them Is Good for You"**

*Lakum fīhā khayrun* — for you in them is good. The statement precedes the command to sacrifice, which is unusual and deliberate.

The animal is not being taken away. Before anything is demanded, the verse says that the arrangement benefits the person making it, and it says so in the widest available word — *khayr*, good, rather than any particular reward. The sūrah will repeat this structure in the verse after next, when it explains what actually reaches God.

**"When They Are Lined Up"**

*Fā-dhkurū isma Allāhi ʿalayhā ṣawāffa* — so mention God's name over them as they stand in rows. Camels are slaughtered standing, with the left foreleg tethered, and the word *ṣawāff* pictures a line of them waiting.

The detail fixes the moment of the invocation: not at the purchase, not at the dedication, and not afterwards, but at the killing. Slaughter is the one act that cannot be performed gently, and the Qur'an surrounds it with the name of the One who gave the animal rather than leaving it as a bare utility.

That is a claim about killing generally. The animal's life is taken at a named moment, for a named purpose, with a name pronounced — the three conditions that separate a sacrifice from a killing, and that the Qur'an enforces in its dietary law.

**"When They Have Fallen on Their Sides"**

*Fa-idhā wajabat junūbuhā* — then when their sides have fallen, eat of them. Camels fall onto their sides when they are slaughtered, and the verse uses that as the marker of the moment after which the meat may be eaten.

The permission to eat was a correction of practice, as the commentators note: in the pre-Islamic period, some groups would not eat the meat of what they had sacrificed. The verse permits it, and the jurists drew the further ruling that eating is desirable rather than obligatory while feeding others is the binding part.

That asymmetry is worth noticing. The sacrificer is allowed the meal and required to give it. The act that looks like a transaction with God turns out to be a provision for other people, with the sacrificer permitted a share.

**"The One Who Asks and the One Who Does Not"**

*Wa-aṭʿimū al-qāniʿa wa-l-muʿtarr* — and feed the contented one and the one who asks. The two words describe two different poor people, and the pairing is standard in the Qur'an.

*Al-qāniʿ* is the one who is content with what he has and does not ask, whose need is therefore invisible; *al-muʿtarr* is the one who presents himself and asks. Sūrah al-Baqarah describes the first kind in a passage that is almost a commentary on this phrase: *“˹Charity is˺ for the needy who are too engaged in the cause of Allah to move about in the land ˹for work˺. Those unfamiliar with their situation will think they are not in need ˹of charity˺ because they do not beg”* (2:273).

The legal and moral consequence is sharp. Charity that waits to be asked for reaches only half the poor. The verse commands the sacrificer to seek out the person whose silence would otherwise cost him his share.

**"Thus We Have Subjected Them to You"**

*Ka-dhālika sakhkharnāhā lakum* — in that way We have made them subservient to you. The verb *sakhkhara* is used throughout the Qur'an for the created order placed at human disposal: *“He has subjected the ships for your service, sailing through the sea by His command”* (14:32), *“And He has subjected for your benefit the day and the night, the sun and the moon”* (16:12), and the same claim for animals, *“so that you may sit firmly on their backs, and remember your Lord’s blessings once you are settled on them”* (43:13).

The word carries an asymmetry: the subjected thing is stronger than the subject. A camel outweighs a man by a factor of six and could kill him; the sea is bigger than any ship. Subjugation here is a gift, not a conquest, and the verse draws the conclusion itself — *so that you may be grateful*.

Gratitude is the response the sūrah names, and it is the right one for a gift that the recipient did not arrange. A person who slaughters an animal that outweighs him, that could not have been subdued by force alone, and that he did not create, is being asked to notice what has been done for him.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:37

> Neither their meat nor blood reaches Allah. Rather, it is your piety that reaches Him. This is how He has subjected them to you so that you may proclaim the greatness of Allah for what He has guided you to, and give good news to the good-doers.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Was Revealed to Stop a Practice**

Al-Qurṭubī preserves the occasion. The idolaters used to smear the blood of their sacrifices on the Kaʿbah, and some of the Muslims wanted to do the same — presumably reasoning that if the gesture was respectable for the other side, it was more so for them. This verse was revealed in response.

The correction is total, and it is aimed at a piece of reasoning rather than at a ritual detail. The assumption behind smearing the blood was that the offering travels: that what a person gives has to get to God, and that the physical stuff is the vehicle. The verse denies the premise. Nothing physical arrives, because the Recipient is not the sort of being that receives parcels.

That is why the sūrah places this verse where it does. It has spent three verses on the sacrificial animals — their benefits, their dedication, the invocation, the distribution of the meat — and it now states what all of that is for, before anyone mistakes the mechanism for the point.

**Two Negations, Then the Positive**

*Lan yanāla Allāha luḥūmuhā wa-lā dimāʾuhā* — neither their meat will reach God, nor their blood.

The two nouns are the two parts of a slaughtered animal: the solid and the liquid, the flesh and what runs out of it. Between them there is nothing left of the carcass. The verse is not saying that a better-quality offering would reach Him; it is saying that the category is wrong. Flesh and blood are the whole material of the sacrifice, and neither arrives.

Then the positive clause, and it is a single word: *wa-lākin yanāluhu al-taqwā minkum* — but reverence from you reaches Him. *Taqwā* is the same word the sūrah used in verse 32, where it was said to come from the hearts. The circle is closed deliberately: the rite's value was located in the heart six verses ago, and now the sūrah says that the heart is the only thing about the rite that arrives.

**The Verb "Reaches" Carries an Assumption**

*Yanāla* means to attain, to get hold of, to arrive at something one was reaching for. It is the verb of effort meeting its object.

The verse uses it twice, and the second use is the surprising one. Ordinarily the word describes a person attaining what he sought — a reward, a goal, a thing he wanted. Here the subject is reverence, and the object is God. What attains God is not what the worshipper shipped but what he *was*.

That is the assumption embedded in the verb and the thing the verse overturns about the practice it corrects. The people smearing blood on the Kaʿbah were operating with a physical theory of transfer: the offering has to arrive, so put it where God is. The verse keeps their verb and replaces the cargo. Something does reach Him; it is not a thing.

**God Is Not Fed**

The theological claim underneath the verse is the one the Qur'an makes with unusual bluntness elsewhere: God has no needs. A sacrifice that feeds the deity belongs to a religion of a different kind, and the Qur'an's insistence on this point is what makes its sacrificial law coherent.

If God were fed, the quality and quantity of the meat would be the variable that mattered, and the poor would be in competition with the altar. Because He is not, the meat has somewhere else to go, which is why the verse immediately before this one commanded that it be eaten and given away, and why the commentators ruled that feeding others is the binding part of the rite while eating is merely permitted.

The sacrifice is therefore not a transfer of goods upward. It is a distribution sideways, with the invocation as the thing directed upward.

**The Address Switches to the Singular**

The last sentence of the verse changes grammatical person. Everything before has been plural — *your* piety, *He has subjected them to you* — and then the closing command, *give good news*, is addressed to a single person: the Prophet ﷺ.

The switch is not a slip and the commentators noted it. The ruling in the first half of the verse is for the community; the announcement in the second half is the Prophet's office. He is the one who gives good news, and the sūrah marks that by changing the grammar rather than by naming him.

The same shift happened in verse 34, which also ended with *give good news*, and the two are a pair: good news to the humble, and good news to the good-doers.

**"That You May Magnify God for Guiding You"**

*Li-tukabbirū Allāha ʿalā mā hadākum* — so that you magnify God for the guidance He gave you. The purpose of the whole arrangement is stated here, and it is not the transfer of anything.

The object of gratitude is *hudā*, guidance. The animal, the subjugation, the slaughter and the meal are all secondary to the fact that these people were shown what to do. A religion that begins with being guided will always owe its central act to the guidance rather than to the offering.

The verb is *kabbirū*, to declare great — the same root as the *takbīr* that opens the prayer and accompanies the slaughter. Magnification is not praise for a benefit received; it is the acknowledgment of the size of the One who gave it.

**Good News to the Good-Doers**

*Wa-bashshir al-muḥsinīna* — and give good news to the good-doers. *Iḥsān* is the word for doing a thing well, for excellence that exceeds the requirement.

The sūrah's last word on the sacrifice is thus addressed to a quality of character rather than to a rite. The people who are promised good news are not the ones who slaughtered, since the sūrah has just said the slaughter itself arrives nowhere. They are the ones for whom the slaughter was an act of *iḥsān* — done properly, for the right reason, with the meat reaching the people it was meant for.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:38

> Indeed, Allah defends those who believe. Surely Allah does not like whoever is deceitful, ungrateful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Defence Is God's Action, Not Theirs**

*Inna Allāha yudāfiʿu ʿan alladhīna āmanū* — God defends those who believe. The verb is from the root shared with the "repelling" of verse 40, and the Arabic is emphatic: God *repels from* the believers, pushes away on their behalf.

The subject of the sentence is worth holding on to. The believers are not the ones doing the defending in this verse, and the sūrah will grant them permission to fight only in the verse after next. Verse 38 is placed first so that the permission, when it comes, cannot be read as the mechanism of their survival.

That ordering is the verse's main work. A community about to be authorized to use force is told, before it is authorized, that its defender is God. The permission does not transfer the defence to them; it enrolls them in a defence that was already under way.

**The Root Shared With Verse 40**

The word used here reappears two verses later in the famous statement that had God not repelled some people by means of others, monasteries and synagogues and mosques would have been destroyed.

The repetition ties the two verses into a single principle. God's defence of the believers is one instance of a general government of history: He restrains, balances and pushes back, and the preservation of anything — a community, a building, a place of worship — is owed to that restraint rather than to the virtue of the people inside it.

Read together, the two verses also remove any suggestion that the believers' fighting is what protects worship. God repels; the believers, in certain defined circumstances, are permitted to take part.

**"God Loves Not Every Treacherous Ingrate"**

*Inna Allāha lā yuḥibbu kulla khawwānin kafūr* — God does not love any treacherous, ungrateful person. Two vices are named in one phrase, and they belong together.

*Khawwān* is the intensive form: a person given to betrayal, for whom treachery is a habit rather than a lapse. *Kafūr* is likewise intensive — not merely ungrateful but a denier of what he has been given. The pairing describes a character rather than an act: the man who betrays trusts and then denies that he was ever trusted with anything.

The Qur'an states the same judgement elsewhere in almost the same words, which confirms that this is a standing principle rather than a remark about one group.

**Why This Verse Precedes the Permission to Fight**

The commentators report an occasion that makes the sequence intelligible. Some believers still in Makkah, suffering persecution, wanted to respond with treacherous methods — assassination, deception — and the revelation answered first with this verse and then, in verse 39, with permission to fight openly.

That sequence is the whole moral structure of the passage, and it is worth stating as clearly as the text does. The permission to use force is granted one verse *after* the prohibition of treachery, which means the permission arrives already bounded. You may fight; you may not betray.

The report also explains why the second vice is ingratitude. A person who has been wronged and concludes that he is therefore free of the rules is denying the good he received — including the good of being forbidden to deceive.

**Treachery Has No Good Ends**

The temptation the verse addresses is the most durable one in political ethics: that a just cause licenses unjust means, and that the urgency of the situation suspends the ordinary prohibitions.

The Qur'an's answer is structural rather than consequentialist. It does not argue that treachery fails, though it often does; it states that God does not love the treacherous, which settles the matter regardless of outcome. A victory obtained by betrayal is not reclassified as righteous by its result.

The tradition recorded a corresponding image for the Day of Resurrection, and the report al-Qurṭubī cites is a well-attested one. ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿUmar narrated that the Prophet ﷺ said: "A flag will be fixed on the Day of Resurrection for every betrayer, and it will be announced, 'This is the betrayal of so-and-so, the son of so-and-so.'" It is recorded in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (6178, and again at 6966).

The image is exact. Betrayal is a hidden act — that is what makes it betrayal — and the punishment announced for it is that it will be made public, with the betrayer's name and his father's name attached to it. The thing he did in secret becomes the thing he is known by in front of everyone.

**Why Ingratitude Is Named Alongside Treachery**

The pairing of the two vices is closer than it looks. *Kafūr* — the habit of denying what one has received — is, on the Qur'an's account, what produces treachery in the first place.

A person who remembers what he was given remembers that he is a trustee, and a trustee who betrays his trust has not merely broken a rule; he has denied the relationship that made the rule. That is why the Qur'an so often couples the two: the man who is ungrateful to God becomes untrustworthy with people, because gratitude is the recognition of having been entrusted.

The application to the occasion of this verse is direct. A believer who had received the faith, the community and the promise of divine defence, and who concluded from his suffering that he was free to deceive, was denying every one of those things at once. The sūrah names the root rather than the branch.

**The Verse's Two Halves Belong Together**

Defence and prohibition are not two unrelated sentences that happen to sit in one verse. They are the same claim from two sides: God is the believer's protector, and God is not on the side of the deceiver.

A community that has been promised divine defence has no need of treachery, and a community that uses it has, by that act, abandoned the protection it was promised. That is the argument of the verse, and the permission that follows in verse 39 is granted to people who have just been told it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:39

> Permission ˹to fight back˺ is ˹hereby˺ granted to those being fought, for they have been wronged. And Allah is truly Most Capable of helping them ˹prevail˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Changed the Community's Posture**

*Udhina li-alladhīna yuqātalūna bi-annahum ẓulimū* — permission is granted to those who are being fought, because they have been wronged.

This is the verse most often identified as the first to authorize the believers to use force. Before it, the community's instructed response to persecution had been patience and withdrawal, and twice that withdrawal had taken the form of emigration: a small group to Abyssinia in 615, under the protection of the Christian Negus, and then the emigration of the Prophet and his companions from Makkah to Madīnah in 622.

The accounts of the occasion differ in detail. Some place its revelation at the point of the Hijrah itself, and al-Qurṭubī records that when Abū Bakr heard it he remarked that he had known all along that combat would eventually be permitted. Al-Ṭabarī connects it instead with a party of believers who set out to leave Makkah, were intercepted and turned back by their persecutors, and were then granted leave to resist.

**The Grammar of Permission**

*Udhina* is a passive verb with no agent named. Nobody is doing the permitting in the sentence, which is the standard Qur'anic way of attributing an authorisation to God without making Him the subject of a finite verb.

The word matters more than it looks. What is granted is permission — *idhn* — not obligation and not enthusiasm. A command would have made fighting a duty of the same order as the prayer; a mere concession would have implied it was tolerated but regrettable. Permission sits precisely between them: it is lawful, it is not mandatory on every individual, and it is not celebrated.

That is the register the whole passage keeps. The sūrah has just forbidden treachery (22:38) and will immediately state the grievance that justifies the fighting (22:40) and the conditions attached to the power it may produce (22:41). Nothing here is a licence.

**"Those Who Are Being Fought"**

The participle is passive: *yuqātalūna*, those who are fought against.

The grammar establishes the sequence, and the sequence is the justification. These people are not aggressors who have been granted a blessing on their aggression; they are the objects of force who have been allowed to stop being only objects. The passive participle carries the whole moral asymmetry of the verse in a single word.

This is why the verse is so often cited in discussions of the Islamic law of war. The permission is not attached to disbelief, to proximity, or to any quality of the opponent. It is attached to the fact of being attacked.

**"Because They Have Been Wronged"**

*Bi-annahum ẓulimū* — because they were wronged. The causal particle *bi-anna* ties the permission directly to a specific injury, and the verb is again passive: wrong was done *to* them.

The clause is the verse's whole case, and it is stated in the most general available term. *Ẓulm* is the word for placing something where it does not belong, for a wrong that everyone recognises as one. It is not defined further here, because the next verse supplies the particulars: expulsion from homes, for no reason except a sentence about God.

The structure matters here: permission, then the reason, and the reason is not a threat assessment or a prophecy. It is a past fact of having been wronged, which can in principle be verified by anyone.

**The Alternative Readings**

The consonantal text admits more than one vocalisation, and the commentators record them. One reading makes the believers the subject of the verb of fighting: leave is given to those who are fighting, since wrong was done to them. Another reading supplies the subject of the granting: it is God who grants leave to those who fight, because they were wronged. Al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī both note the variants.

The readings converge on the same claim. Whoever takes up arms in these circumstances does so as a party that has been wronged, and the permission is a response to that rather than an initiative. The difference between the vocalisations concerns only whether the Arabic presents the fighting as already under way or as being licensed to begin.

**Why the Permission Falls Inside a Pilgrimage Sūrah**

The placement is worth a moment, because it looks like a digression and is not. This is the sūrah of the pilgrimage — the sūrah that was named for the rite its audience had been prevented from performing, and that declared the Sacred Mosque open to resident and visitor alike (22:25).

The people being granted permission to fight are the people who were barred from the House. The passage that follows will state the grievance in a sentence: they were expelled for saying that their Lord is God. What is being authorized is the recovery of a community's ability to worship where it was told to worship, and the authorisation is bounded by that.

That is why the sūrah can move from verses about sacrificial camels to verses about armed defence without any sense of incongruity. Both are about a community that has been prevented from practising its religion, and both are permissions granted to people who had been told to wait.

**"God Is Able to Give Them Victory"**

*Wa-inna Allāha ʿalā naṣrihim la-qadīr* — and God is, for their help, truly capable.

The closing clause reattaches the outcome to God, exactly as verse 38 did when it said that God defends the believers. The permission does not make victory a consequence of the fighting; the victory is God's to give, and the fighting is what the community has been allowed to do while waiting for it.

The emphatic *la-qadīr* is the note the sūrah ends on. A community that has been expelled, persecuted and prevented from emigrating is being told two things at once: it may now resist, and its resistance is not what will save it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:40

> ˹They are˺ those who have been expelled from their homes for no reason other than proclaiming: “Our Lord is Allah.” Had Allah not repelled ˹the aggression of˺ some people by means of others, destruction would have surely claimed monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques in which Allah’s Name is often mentioned. Allah will certainly help those who stand up for Him. Allah is truly All-Powerful, Almighty.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Grievance, Stated in One Sentence**

*Alladhīna ukhrijū min diyārihim bi-ghayri ḥaqqin illā an yaqūlū rabbunā Allāh* — those who were driven out of their homes without any right, except that they say, "Our Lord is God."

The offence for which these people were expelled was a sentence. Not a raid, not a theft, not a sedition: a creed stated aloud. The verse frames it that way because the framing is the argument. Whatever else the Quraysh believed they were doing, the charge against the believers reduces to a claim about who God is.

The exception particle *illā* does the work: they were expelled for nothing *except* saying this. A person can be lawfully expelled for a crime; there is no lawful expulsion for a sentence of worship.

**The Expulsion Was the Grievance the Qur'an Cited Most**

The commentators note that this grievance is among the most frequently cited in the Qur'an against the Quraysh. The same expulsion is named in Sūrah al-Baqarah, where it is the ground of the permission to fight: *“Kill them wherever you come upon them and drive them out of the places from which they have driven you out. For persecution is far worse than killing”* (2:191).

The repetition matters legally as well as rhetorically. In the Qur'anic framework, expulsion from one's home for one's religion is not one injury among many; it is the paradigm injury, and the permission granted in verse 39 is tied to it specifically rather than to hostility in general.

**Four Words for Houses of Worship**

*Lahuddimat ṣawāmiʿu wa-biyaʿun wa-ṣalawātun wa-masājidu* — cloisters, churches, synagogues and mosques would have been pulled down.

The commentators disagreed over the assignment. *Ṣawāmiʿ* is usually taken as the cells and monasteries of Christian monks, though some linked it to the Sabians. *Biyaʿ* is generally Christian churches, possibly Jewish temples. *Ṣalawāt* is usually understood as Jewish synagogues, with some linking it to the Sabians. *Masājid* — literally places of prostration — is taken by most as the mosques of the Muslims, though one reading takes it as a general term covering houses of worship of any kind.

The disagreement is historically interesting, and theologically the point survives it regardless of which community owns which word: what is being protected is worship as such, in all its forms, in buildings belonging to several religions at once.

**A Defence of Other People's Worship**

This is the feature of the verse that is hardest to overstate. The permission to fight is being granted to a community that has been expelled, and the reason given for the permission includes the preservation of buildings belonging to other faiths.

That is not incidental colour. The verse's logic is that unchecked aggression destroys *worship*, and that the destruction is a loss regardless of who was praying in the building. The protection of monasteries and synagogues is cited as a good that the believers are being authorized to secure — the same good that secures their own mosques.

The jurists drew the consistent conclusion: the houses of worship of peoples under treaty are inviolable within and outside Islamic territory, and damaging them is forbidden. The verse's plural vocabulary produces that ruling directly.

**The Content of the Sentence They Were Expelled For**

*An yaqūlū rabbunā Allāh* — that they say: our Lord is God.

The creed is worth parsing, because the sūrah chose these words rather than others. The term used is *rabb*, Lord — the word for master, owner, sustainer and the one to whom a person is responsible. The sentence is not a claim about God's existence; the Quraysh did not deny that a high god existed. It is a claim about who runs what, and about who a person answers to.

That is precisely why it was intolerable. A sentence about existence can be added to an existing arrangement without disturbing it. A sentence about lordship cannot, because lordship is not divisible between God and the patrons, intercessors and tribal authorities that the arrangement depended on. Saying *our Lord is God* removes the middlemen, and the people who were expelled were expelled for saying it in the hearing of the people who profited from them.

**"Had God Not Repelled Some People by Others"**

*Wa-law-lā dafʿu Allāhi al-nāsa baʿḍahum bi-baʿḍin* — and were it not for God's repelling of people, some of them by means of others.
