The principle is a sober one. The world is not preserved by the victory of the good; it is preserved by a balance of restraint in which aggression is met and checked. The agents in the sentence are unnamed and undifferentiated: *some people by means of others*. Nobody is assigned the role of the righteous party.

This is the same root the sūrah used in verse 38 for God's defence of the believers, and the two are one doctrine. God pushes back against aggression through whatever and whoever He wills, and the survival of anything fragile — a community, a building, a rite — is the result.

**"God Will Help Those Who Help Him"**

*Wa-la-yanṣuranna Allāhu man yanṣuruhu* — God will certainly help whoever helps Him.

The phrasing is deliberately paradoxical. God has no need of help, and the commentators resolve it in the obvious way: helping God means helping His religion and His messenger.

The paradox carries a warning, though. "Helping God" can be claimed by anyone with a cause, and the sūrah has just specified what it means in this case: standing up for a religion whose followers were expelled for saying that their Lord is God, and whose defence includes the buildings of other people's worship. Any other claim to be helping Him is not covered by this promise.

The verse closes with two names — *qawiyy*, strong, and *ʿazīz*, mighty — and the point of closing there is that the help promised does not depend on the strength of the people being helped.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:41

> ˹They are˺ those who, if established in the land by Us, would perform prayer, pay alms-tax, encourage what is good, and forbid what is evil. And with Allah rests the outcome of all affairs.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Power Is Described as Something Granted**

*Alladhīna in makkannahum fī al-arḍi* — those who, if We establish them in the land. The verb is *makkannā*, from *makkana*: to give someone a firm place, to put him in a position where he can act.

The subject of that verb is God. These people do not seize power; they are established in it. That is the first thing the sūrah says about the community that has just been authorized to fight, and it is the frame for everything that follows. A community that wins is not a community that has taken something; it is a community that has been placed, and the placing is conditional on what it does there.

**The Sentence Is a Conditional, Not a Prediction**

*In makkannahum* — if We establish them. The particle is a condition, and a condition can go unmet.

The sūrah is being realistic here, and the realism is part of the instruction. Power is described as a possibility that God may grant, not as a promise already made; the community reading this verse in Makkah had no sovereignty and no certainty of obtaining any. What they are told is what would be expected of them if it came.

The conditional also removes any suggestion that the fighting permission of verses 39 and 40 was a grant of territory. It was leave to resist an expulsion. Whether those resisting would ever be established in the land is left open in the grammar, and the conditionality is what keeps the four duties from reading as a programme for conquest.

**Four Acts, in Ascending Difficulty**

The four things they do once established are: they establish the prayer, they give the alms, they command what is right, and they forbid what is wrong.

The order is not random. The first two are what a person does about God and about his wealth — acts that can be performed privately, at least in part. The second two are necessarily public. Commanding right and forbidding wrong means intervening in other people's lives, including the lives of people who do not want it, and it is the act that a community with power can perform and a community without it mostly cannot.

That is why the list is attached to *tamkīn* in the first place. The question the verse answers is what power is for, and the answer is not security or prosperity but the public maintenance of a moral order.

**The Fourth Item Is the Dangerous One**

Forbidding wrong is the item that gets communities into trouble, and the sūrah names it without qualification.

The reason is that the alternative is worse. A community that prays, pays and keeps to itself has abandoned the public character of the religion it was expelled for saying out loud — the very sentence for which these people lost their homes was a public claim about God. The verse is describing a consistency: the people who were driven out for saying something publicly are the people who, once established, will say it publicly again.

The tradition's discussions of *al-amr bi-l-maʿrūf* focus almost entirely on its limits and etiquette — who may do it, when, and by what means — which is the recognition that the fourth item is the one that requires judgement.

**Why the Private Acts Come First**

The list opens with the two acts that can be performed alone and closes with the two that cannot, and the ordering is a safeguard rather than a formality.

A community that commands right and forbids wrong without praying and giving has become a political movement with a moral vocabulary. Its interventions will be about power, because power is all it has. Putting the prayer first is the Qur'an's way of ensuring that the public acts are performed by people who have first been before God about them.

The same ordering appears wherever the Qur'an pairs worship with social obligation. The pattern is always: what you owe God, then what you owe other people, and never the second without the first. The sūrah has been insisting on this since verse 32, when it located the value of the rites in the heart, and it will insist on it again in verse 77, when it commands bowing, prostration, worship and good deeds in a single breath.

**Who Is Being Described**

The commentators proposed several referents. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī took the verse as describing the conditions laid on whoever God establishes in the land, meaning those given sovereignty. Others took it as a description of the Emigrants specifically, or of the Muslims generally.

The first reading has the most to recommend it, because the verse's grammar is a conditional: *if* We establish them. A conditional describes what follows from a condition rather than what a named group happens to do. Whoever holds power is being told what the power was given for.

Read that way, the verse is a standing criterion rather than a historical description, and it applies to the community in every generation in which it has been established — including the generations in which it was an empire.

**"With God Is the End of Affairs"**

*Wa-lillāhi ʿāqibatu al-umūr* — and to God belongs the outcome of all matters.

The closing clause does two things. It states that the results of the four acts are not the measure of them — a community can pray, pay, command and forbid, and still not be given the outcome it hoped for. And it states that the results are not lost either; they go somewhere and are gathered to someone.

Placed immediately after a verse about fighting and immediately before a passage about the nations that denied their messengers, the clause is also a warning against reading history backwards. Victory is not a verdict, and defeat is not one either. The sūrah's next verses will make the point at length: the nations that destroyed their messengers were given power first and were destroyed anyway.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:42

> If they deny you ˹O Prophet˺, so did the people of Noah before them, as well as ˹the tribes of˺ ’Ȃd and Thamûd,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Denial Is Presented as the Pattern, Not the Emergency**

*Wa-in yukadhdhibūka fa-qad kadhdhabat qablahum qawmu Nūḥin* — and if they deny you, the people of Noah denied before them.

The conditional is doing something specific. It does not say "when they deny you" and it does not say "since they have denied you." It says *if*, and the apodosis is not a threat but a precedent. The verse's first move is to reclassify the Prophet's situation: this is not a crisis, it is the norm.

That is the Qur'an's most consistent consolation to its messenger, and it appears in the same form elsewhere: *“If they deny you, so did those before them. Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, divine Books, and enlightening Scriptures”* (35:25). The point is never that rejection is easy. It is that rejection is what happens to messengers, and that a messenger who is being rejected is not thereby failing.

**The List That Follows Is Arabian**

The three names given here — Noah, ʿĀd and Thamūd — are the three the Qur'an most often uses as the opening of this catalogue.

The choice is rhetorical rather than chronological. ʿĀd and Thamūd were the Arabian peoples whose destruction was part of the peninsula's inherited memory; their ruined settlements were places a traveller could point at. By putting them beside Noah, whose flood was known through the scriptural traditions of the region, the verse builds a list that its first audience could not dismiss as foreign folklore.

Sūrah Ibrāhīm uses the same opening for the same purpose: *“Have you not ˹already˺ received the stories of those who were before you: the people of Noah, ’Âd, Thamûd, and those after them? Only Allah knows how many they were”* (14:9).

**A Catalogue With No Verbs of Its Own**

Grammatically, verses 42, 43 and 44 are one sentence. Verse 42 opens it with the denial of the people of Noah and the two Arabian tribes; verse 43 continues the list; verse 44 closes it with Midian and Moses, and only then does the sentence's main verb arrive: *I granted them respite, then I seized them.*

The effect of the suspended verb is suspense. Nine communities are named in three verses with nothing happening to any of them, and then the action arrives once, at the end, for all of them together. A reader hears the list accumulating and waits for what is going to be said about it.

**What Was Denied Was a Messenger**

The verb throughout is *kadhdhaba*, to declare someone a liar — the verb of refusing a person rather than refusing a proposition.

This matters for how the catalogue functions. The parallel is not between the Makkans' theology and the theology of ʿĀd; the parallel is between the Makkans' treatment of their messenger and the treatment of Hūd, Ṣāliḥ and Shuʿayb. Sūrah Hūd records what each of them said, and the message is identical in every case: *“And to the people of ’Âd We sent their brother Hûd. He said, ‘O my people! Worship Allah. You have no god other than Him’”* (11:50).

The offence, in other words, is not rejecting an unfamiliar doctrine. It is calling your brother a liar when he tells you something true.

**Why the Prophet Is Told This Now**

The placement of the catalogue matters. It follows the permission to fight, and that is the moment at which a community might reasonably conclude that it has been vindicated and that the reckoning is about to begin.

Verses 42 to 48 are the correction to that expectation. The pattern in history is not that the truthful are vindicated quickly; it is that the deniers are given time and then seized. The Prophet is being told, before he fights his first battle, that the schedule is not his and that the delay is not a sign of anything.

**Why the Flood Is the Right Place to Start**

Noah's people are the one community on this list that left no ruins to look at. ʿĀd, Thamūd, Midian and Lot's towns are all places whose remains could be pointed at; the flood left a world drowned and a single boat on a mountain.

That makes the first item on the catalogue the one that requires the most from a reader. Nothing in the physical landscape testifies to it. The listener must accept it because the revelation says so, or because the story has been carried by the communities of the region for centuries.

The ordering is therefore an argument about the kind of evidence being offered. The catalogue does not rest on archaeology — it opens with an item that archaeology cannot supply — and it closes with the ruins, which are corroboration rather than foundation. What the sūrah is asking its audience to accept is a pattern, and the pattern does not depend on how many of its instances can still be visited.

**Noah's People First**

There is a reason Noah heads the list. His people were the ones who were given the longest period of warning in the Qur'an's account and the most complete destruction, and his story is the sūrahs' standing example of a messenger who kept calling while nobody listened.

Beginning with him sets the scale of the patience being asked for. The Prophet at Makkah had been preaching for a little over a decade. The catalogue opens with a man who preached for far longer, to a people who were removed from the earth entirely, and who kept preaching while the boat was being built in a place with no water in sight.

It also sets the scale of the outcome. Every community on this list comes to an end; the first one on it comes to the most complete end available in the record, and the sūrah wants its audience to measure the others against that rather than against their own impatience.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:43

> the people of Abraham, the people of Lot,

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Names From One Household**

The verse continues the catalogue of verse 42 with two more communities: the people of Abraham and the people of Lot. The pairing is deliberate, and it is not chronological.

Abraham and Lot were kinsmen — Lot was Abraham's nephew, and he is described in the Qur'an as one of those who emigrated with him and as one of those to whom God gave prophethood. Their two communities are therefore linked in the narrative, and their two destructions are reported in the same passages: the angels who came to announce the birth of Isḥāq went on from Abraham's household to Lot's town.

That is why the sūrah places them together. What is being listed is not a sequence of unrelated tribes but a cluster of stories that the audience already knew as one connected account.

**The Founder of This Sūrah's House Appears Among the Denied**

The inclusion of Abraham's people is the sharpest item on the list, and the sūrah makes the irony available to anyone who has read its first half.

Verses 26 and 27 of this very sūrah commissioned Abraham to purify the House and to call humanity to the pilgrimage. He is the founder of the sanctuary the sūrah is named for, the man whose prayer established the valley, and the one whose religion the Qur'an says the Muslims follow. His own people rejected him.

That is the point the Prophet is being consoled with, and it is a stronger one than the earlier names. If Abraham — the man whose House the Makkans venerated and whose rites they claimed to be performing — was denied by his own community, then denial is not a verdict on the messenger. The Makkans claimed Abraham as their ancestor while rejecting his successor, and the sūrah has already spent several verses showing that the rites they inherited from him had been emptied of what he was sent with.

**Lot's People and the Nature of Their Refusal**

Lot's community is the one item on the list whose specific crime is reported in detail elsewhere in the Qur'an, and it was not idolatry in the ordinary sense.

Their denial took the form of refusing a prohibition, assaulting the guests in their town, and treating the messenger who warned them as an offender. They are described as a people who *transgressed* rather than as a people who worshipped stones, and their destruction was correspondingly specific: the town was turned upside down and rained with stones of baked clay.

Including them widens what the catalogue covers. The list is not a catalogue of polytheists. It is a catalogue of communities that were told something by a messenger and refused it, whatever form the refusal took.

**Two Words in the Arabic, and Their Weight**

The verse is two names long. In the Arabic it is *wa-qawmu Ibrāhīma wa-qawmu Lūṭin* — and the people of Abraham and the people of Lot — no verbs, no predicates, nothing but the continuation of the sentence begun in verse 42.

The brevity is the rhetoric. A catalogue that paused to describe each community would become a set of stories; a catalogue that does nothing but accumulate names becomes an argument from sheer number. Nine communities in three verses, with the verdict reserved for the end, produces the sense of an accumulation too large to argue with.

**What "His People" Means for the Verdict**

The phrase *qawmu Ibrāhīma* — the people of Abraham — is the standard Qur'anic formula for the community a messenger was sent to, and its assumptions repay attention.

The assumption is that a messenger is sent *to* someone. No messenger in the Qur'an preaches to humanity in the abstract; each is sent to a specific people with a specific language. The universal address of verse 49 will come later in this passage, and it is the exception that proves the rule.

The consequence for the catalogue is that the rejection is always local and particular. These communities were not rejecting a distant message; they were rejecting a message delivered to them, in their language, by one of their own, and that is what makes the verdict on them intelligible.

**Every Messenger on This List Was an Insider**

One feature runs through the whole catalogue and is easy to miss because the verses are so compressed. Every messenger named was sent to *his own* people, and the Qur'an is careful to say so.

*“And to the people of Thamûd We sent their brother Ṣâliḥ”* (7:73). *“And to the people of ’Âd We sent their brother Hûd”* (11:50). The word translated "brother" is used repeatedly and with emphasis: Shuʿayb is called the brother of the people of Midian (7:85), and the pattern holds for every community on the list.

The significance for the consolation being offered is considerable. These communities were not rejecting foreigners with unfamiliar customs. They were rejecting a man they had grown up with, whose family they knew, whose honesty they had previously relied on. The denial was not a failure of information about the messenger; it was a refusal to accept a claim from someone whose ordinariness was the problem.

That is precisely the objection the Qur'an records against Muhammad ﷺ — that he was a man like them, that he ate and walked in the market, and that no angel had been sent down with him. The catalogue's answer is that this has always been the arrangement.

**Why the Kaʿbah's Neighbours Should Have Recognised This**

One further consideration. Abraham's people and Lot's people were the communities nearest to the audience's own geography, and their ruins were known. ʿĀd and Thamūd were in the peninsula; Lot's towns were on the trade route to Syria that Makkah's caravans used every year.

The Qur'an's argument here is not addressed to people who needed to be told these stories for the first time. It is addressed to people who passed the remains on their way to work, and who are being asked why the remains did not change their minds.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:44

> and the residents of Midian. And Moses was denied ˹too˺. But I delayed ˹the fate of˺ the disbelievers ˹until their appointed time˺ then seized them. And how severe was My response!

**Expanded Commentary**

**Midian and Its Messenger**

The catalogue closes with the people of Midian, to whom the Qur'an sent Shuʿayb. The account is preserved in Sūrah al-Aʿrāf: *“And to the people of Midian We sent their brother Shu’aib. He said, ‘O my people! Worship Allah—you have no other god except Him. A clear proof has already come to you from your Lord. So give just measure and weight, do not defraud people of their property, nor spread corruption in the land after it has been set in order’”* (7:85).

Midian is the last community on the list whose messenger is named in the Qur'an by name besides the five already given, and it is the one whose offence was economic — fraudulent measures, commercial cheating — which broadens the catalogue again. These are not all idolaters, and they are not all guilty of the same thing. What they share is the refusal.

**Why Moses Is Named Differently**

The construction changes at Moses: *wa-ukhdhiba Mūsā* — and Moses was denied. The verb is passive and the subject is the messenger himself rather than his people.

The shift is meaningful. For every other community on the list, the deniers are named. Here the denied one is named, and the deniers are left as an unnamed subject in the passive voice.

The commentators took this as deliberate. Moses' people — the Children of Israel — were not destroyed in the way the other communities were; they were delivered, and their denial of him was persistent rather than terminal. Naming him rather than them keeps him out of the destruction verdict that follows, which belongs to the communities that were seized.

**"But I Granted the Disbelievers Respite"**

*Thumma akhadhtuhum* is preceded by *fa-amlaytu li-l-kāfirīn* — I gave the disbelievers respite. The verb *amlā* means to give rein, to let run on, to allow more length.

This is the sentence the whole catalogue was building toward, and it is the point the sūrah needed to make at this juncture. Nine communities were named, and the thing said about all of them is that they were given time. The delay is not an oversight or a reprieve; it is the standing procedure.

The sūrah has been preparing this since verse 1. The Hour is *coming* (22:7) rather than arrived; Iblīs was *delayed* in the parallel sūrahs; the man who wanted the punishment hurried is told in the next verse that a day with God is a thousand years. Delay is the sūrah's most consistent claim about how God runs the world, and this verse states it as a matter of history.

**"Then I Seized Them"**

*Thumma akhadhtuhum* — then I took them. The verb is plain and physical, and *thumma* places it after an interval of unspecified length.

The combination is what makes the verse frightening, and it is the same combination the sūrah opposed in verse 40 to the aggression of the Quraysh. Wrongdoing is met, but not on the schedule the wronged party would choose. The delay is long enough for the deniers to conclude that nothing is coming.

**"And How Was My Denial"**

*Fa-kayfa kāna nakīr* — and how was My *nakīr*? The word is the verse's difficulty, and the commentators recorded two readings of it.

*Nakīr* comes from the root *n-k-r*, which gives both "to deny" and "to disapprove, to alter something into a state one dislikes." On the first reading the phrase means "how was My denial of them" — God's rejection answering theirs, the same word used for their refusal. On the second it means "how was My rebuke," or "how terrible was the change I wrought."

Both senses are present in the Arabic, and the ambiguity is a deliberate piece of lexical justice. They denied; the response is described with the same root. The punishment is named as a matching refusal rather than as an unrelated act of force. The same closing question appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in the same form, which confirms that the phrase is a fixed formula: *“Then I seized those who persisted in disbelief. How severe was My response!”* (35:26).

**Two Verbs, Nine Communities**

The whole catalogue of verses 42 to 44 comes down to two verbs at the end: *amlaytu*, I gave rein, and *akhadhtu*, I took.

Everything else in those three verses is a list of names. The sūrah is not interested in the differences between the communities — their different sins, their different messengers, their different centuries. What it extracts from all nine is a single sequence: time given, then time ended.

That economy is the point of the catalogue as a literary form. A historian would want the particulars. The Qur'an wants the rule, and it states the rule in a form its audience can apply to itself. The community reading this verse in Makkah was, in the sūrah's telling, in the first half of the sequence.

**The Catalogue's Verdict, Delivered Once**

Nine communities, three verses, one verdict. The grammatical structure of verses 42 to 44 is what carries the argument: everything is suspended until the final clause, and then respite and seizure are announced for all of them together.

A reader who has been following the list is left with a single principle instead of nine anecdotes. Communities that reject their messengers are not answered on the spot. They are given length, and then they are taken — and the question the verse ends on is not whether it happened but *how*, which is a question the ruins were supposed to answer.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:45

> Many are the societies We have destroyed for persisting in wrongdoing, leaving them in total ruin. ˹Many are˺ also the abandoned wells and lofty palaces!

**Expanded Commentary**

**"How Many a Town"**

*Wa-ka-ayyin min qaryatin ahlaknāhā* — and how many a town did We destroy. The construction *ka-ayyin* is the Arabic idiom for an uncounted large number, and it is always used in the Qur'an to introduce a claim that could be documented but will not be enumerated.

The rhetorical effect depends on the reader supplying the number. A text that said "seven towns" would be making a historical claim that could be checked and disputed. A text that says *how many* is making a claim about the character of history itself, and it invites the audience to count for themselves from what they have seen.

**The Condition Attached to the Destruction**

*Wa-hiya ẓālimatun* — while it was doing wrong. The phrase is a circumstantial clause: the destruction happened in a state of wrongdoing, and the implication is that it happened *because* of it.

The word is *ẓulm*, the same root the sūrah used in verse 39 for the wrong done to the believers. Here it is used for the wrong the destroyed communities did. The symmetry is deliberate: the Qur'an's account of destruction does not run on religious affiliation. What brings a settlement down is injustice, and the communities listed in verses 42 to 44 are described in their own sūrahs in terms of what they did to people — cheating in the market, highway robbery, assault — rather than only in terms of what they believed.

**"Fallen Upon Its Roofs"**

*Fa-hiya khāwiyatun ʿalā ʿurūshihā* — so it lies hollow upon its roofs. The image is an inversion, and al-Rāzī explained the sequence: the roofs collapsed first, and then the walls fell on top of them, leaving the structure lying on what used to be its uppermost part.

The detail is the kind of thing only someone who has walked through a ruined town would say. A building destroyed from the outside leaves walls standing and rubble at the base; a building whose roof goes first ends up inside-out. The verse is describing the specific look of a place that has been abandoned for a long time.

The word *khāwiya* — hollow, empty, collapsed inward — carries the desolation. What is being described is not a ruined building but a town that used to be full and is now a shell lying on itself.

**A Well and a Palace**

*Wa-biʾrin muʿaṭṭalatin wa-qaṣrin mashīd* — and an abandoned well, and a lofty palace.

The two objects are chosen to cover the whole of a civilisation's capital. The well is water: the thing that makes settlement in that country possible, the infrastructure no town can exist without. The palace is power: the monument a ruling house builds to outlast it.

Both are described as they now are. The well is *muʿaṭṭalah*, out of use, idle — not dry but unused, which is worse, because it means there is nobody left to draw from it. The palace is *mashīd*, raised high, plastered and made lofty — the description is of its ambition rather than of its ruin, which lets the reader supply the irony himself.

**The Argument Is Empirical**

The Qur'an's appeal here is to evidence, and it is worth being precise about what kind.

The ruined settlements of the peninsula were not known to the Makkans from books. They were on the trade routes. Caravans passed them, watered at some of the wells, and told stories about them. The verse asks its audience to do the arithmetic: this many towns, all destroyed, all of them in a state of wrong at the time.

Sūrah al-Rūm draws the conclusion the Qur'an draws from this evidence: *“Have they not travelled throughout the land to see what was the end of those ˹destroyed˺ before them? They were far superior in might; they cultivated the land and developed it more than these ˹Meccans˺ ever have. Their messengers came to them with clear proofs. Allah would have never wronged them, but it was they who wronged themselves”* (30:9).

Note the last clause. The evidence from the ruins is not that God is harsh; it is that the destruction was self-inflicted. Nothing in the record suggests that a town was taken while it was behaving well.

**Towns, Not Individuals**

The noun is *qaryah* — a town, a settlement, a collected habitation. The Qur'an's destructions are of settlements rather than of individuals or of nations in the abstract.

That choice carries a claim about how God deals with human beings in history. A *qaryah* is the unit at which a way of life becomes visible: a market with agreed weights, a well shared, a ruling house, a set of tolerated practices. Wrongdoing at that scale is not a private matter, and its correction is likewise public.

The flip side is that the town is also the unit at which repentance would have been visible and effective. The Qur'an's accounts of destruction consistently record that a messenger was sent to the town first (16:36), which means the settlement had a collective chance to change before the collective punishment arrived.

This is why the ruins are usable as evidence. An individual's fate proves nothing; a town reduced to a well and a palace proves that something was done about a shared practice.

**Why the Ruins Did Not Settle the Question**

The next verse will say what follows from all this, and it is the harder claim: the evidence was available and did not produce the conclusion. Travelling through the ruins is not sufficient, because travelling is done with the eyes.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:46

> Have they not travelled throughout the land so their hearts may reason, and their ears may listen? Indeed, it is not the eyes that are blind, but it is the hearts in the chests that grow blind.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Assumes the Answer Is No**

*A-fa-lam yasīrū fī al-arḍi* — have they not travelled in the land? The interrogative expects a negative answer only in the rhetorical sense: of course they have travelled. The Quraysh were merchants; their caravans went to Syria in the summer and to Yemen in the winter, and the routes passed directly through the ruins of the communities named in the previous verses.

So the question is not whether they moved about. It is whether the moving about did what moving about is supposed to do. The sūrah has just described abandoned wells and lofty palaces lying on their roofs; this verse asks why passing them produced nothing.

**What Travel Was Supposed to Produce**

*Fa-takūna lahum qulūbun yaʿqilūna bihā aw ādhānun yasmaʿūna bihā* — so that they would have hearts by which to understand, or ears by which to hear.

Two organs are named, and the pairing is unusual. The heart is given the verb of reasoning — *yaʿqilūna*, to use the intellect — which is not the attribution modern readers expect. In the Qur'an's psychology, the heart is the organ of understanding, and the intellect is a function of it rather than a rival to it.

The ear is given hearing. The choice of the ear over the eye, in a verse about travel and ruins, is pointed. Sight had already done its work: they had seen the wells and the palaces. What was missing was the hearing of the accounts — the reports of what those places were and what happened to them — and the reasoning that would connect what was seen with what was told.

**"It Is Not the Eyes That Go Blind"**

*Fa-innahā lā taʿmā al-abṣāru* — for it is not the eyesights that are blind. The word *abṣār* is the plural of *baṣar*, eyesight or insight, and the verse denies the defect there.

The denial is the verse's centre. The problem with these people is not a failure of observation. They saw the ruins, they saw the wells, and they saw that the towns were empty. Their eyes worked perfectly. What failed was the step from seeing to concluding.

That is a diagnosis of a specific and common condition: the evidence is in front of a person and produces nothing, because the person has no framework that would make it mean something. Adding more evidence would not help. What is missing is the organ that weighs it.

**"But the Hearts Which Are in the Breasts"**

*Wa-lākin taʿmā al-qulūbu allatī fī al-ṣudūr* — but blind are the hearts that are in the breasts.

The phrase *allatī fī al-ṣudūr*, which are in the breasts, looks redundant and is not. It is there to insist that the heart in question is the physical organ in a person's chest and not a poetic abstraction. The Qur'an's anthropology locates understanding in a real thing inside a real body, and it says so to prevent the reader from spiritualising the diagnosis away.

The same insistence appears in a well-known ḥadīth. Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr reported that the Prophet ﷺ said: "In the body there is a piece of flesh; if it is sound, the entire body is sound, and if it is corrupt, the entire body is corrupt — it is the heart." It is recorded in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (52) and *Ṣaḥīḥ Muslim* (1599). The report's logic is the verse's logic: the condition of the whole person follows the condition of a particular organ.

**Blindness Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

The image of blindness for a failure of understanding is one of the Qur'an's most frequent, and it rarely describes a person who lacks information. It describes a person who has it and cannot use it.

The sūrahs use the same vocabulary for the refusal to accept the message, and they pair it with deafness and dumbness — the senses intact, the reception switched off. What the Qur'an calls blindness is a property of the receiving organ, not of the signal.

**What the Verse Requires of a Traveller**

The purpose clause is explicit: travel *so that* hearts reason and ears hear. The Qur'an is not commending travel for its own sake and is certainly not commending curiosity about old stones.

That qualification matters, because travel of exactly this kind was familiar to the audience. The caravans passed the ruins every year and the guides told stories about them, and nothing about the arrangement produced a change. What is being demanded is not more miles but a different kind of attention: the willingness to let what is seen count as evidence about oneself rather than as entertainment.

The sūrahs that repeat this appeal frame it identically, as a demand to see *the end of those before them* rather than merely their remains: *“Say, ‘Travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers’”* (6:11), and *“Similar situations came to pass before you, so travel throughout the land and see the fate of the deniers”* (3:137). The object of the seeing is always a conclusion, never a sight.

**Why This Verse Ends the Ruins Passage**

Verses 45 and 46 form a pair, and the second is the answer to the difficulty the first creates. Verse 45 said that the evidence of destroyed towns is everywhere. The natural question is why the evidence does not settle anything.

The answer is that evidence is not self-interpreting. The ruins require a heart that reasons and an ear that hears, and where those are absent, the traveller comes home with nothing but a story about old buildings. That is why the sūrah's next move is not to produce more evidence but to address the demand for the punishment — the demand of people who have seen everything and concluded that nothing is coming.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:47

> They challenge you ˹O Prophet˺ to hasten the torment. And Allah will never fail in His promise. But a day with your Lord is indeed like a thousand years by your counting.
**Expanded Commentary**

**The Taunt**

*Wa-yastaʿjilūnaka bi-l-ʿadhābi* — and they ask you to hurry the punishment. The verb is the intensive form of "to hasten": they keep asking, and the asking is a form of mockery rather than a request for information.

The demand appears repeatedly in the Qur'an, always in the same key. The opponents do not say that the punishment is unjust; they say it is late, and they treat the lateness as evidence that it is not coming. The challenge is designed to be unanswerable: a messenger who produces the punishment looks vindictive, and a messenger who does not produce it looks mistaken.

The demand is recorded elsewhere in the same words, and in Makkah it was once phrased as a prayer: *"O Allah! If this is indeed the truth from You, then rain down stones upon us from the sky or overcome us with a painful punishment"* (8:32). Sūrah Yūnus supplies the answer the demand invites: *"Imagine if His torment were to overcome you by night or day—do the wicked realize what they are ˹really˺ asking Him to hasten? Will you believe in it only after it has overtaken you? Now? But you always wanted to hasten it!"* (10:50–51). The reply exposes the request as something other than a request for evidence. A punishment that arrives is the end of the period in which evidence could be answered at all, and the taunt is therefore a demand that the testing be brought to a close.

**The Answer's First Half**

*Wa-lan yukhlifa Allāhu waʿdahu* — and God will not fail His promise. The sentence denies the premise rather than the timing.

What the mockers have implied is that a delayed promise is a broken one. The verse says that the promise stands, which relocates the difficulty entirely: the punishment is certain, and the only variable is when. That is why the verse's second half is about time rather than about power — the objection is not that God cannot do it, and the answer is not that He can.

The grammar is emphatic. *Lan* is the strongest available negative, and it negates a verb of failing: God will not, ever, break His appointment.

The same claim about God appears on the other side of the argument, in the prayer of the believers: *"Our Lord! You will certainly gather all humanity for the ˹promised˺ Day—about which there is no doubt. Surely Allah does not break His promise"* (3:9). What the faithful rely on as their hope, the mockers cite as their proof. The verse's point is that the attribute is one and the same in both mouths, and that a promise which can be waited on is not the sort of promise that lapses.

**"A Day With Your Lord Is Like a Thousand Years"**

*Wa-inna yawman ʿinda rabbika ka-alfi sanatin mimmā taʿuddūna* — and a day with your Lord is like a thousand years of what you count.

The commentators proposed several referents. Some took the day as one of the six days of creation mentioned elsewhere in the Qur'an, comparing it with the statement that a day with God measures a thousand years by human reckoning (32:5). Others took it as one of the days of the Hereafter, meaning the Day of Resurrection or the day of reckoning itself. Al-Rāzī recorded a further reading: that a day of punishment will *feel* to the sufferer like a thousand years, so the delay is not the point — the duration, once it begins, is.

The figure has a larger neighbour that the commentators had to reconcile with it: *"˹through which˺ the angels and the ˹holy˺ spirit will ascend to Him on a Day fifty thousand years in length"* (70:4). Their answers differed — that the thousand and the fifty thousand describe different days, or different experiences of one day, or the reckoning as a whole against a single stage of it — but they agreed on what the numbers are doing. Neither is being offered as a measurement. Both are the largest available figures pushed against a duration that has no human equivalent, and the argument of the verse is unaffected by which reading is taken.

**Which Reading Is Stronger**

The second reading fits the context best. The verse is answering a challenge about punishment; the most natural sense of "a day with your Lord" in that context is a day in which the divine reckoning is executed, not a day of creation. The parallel verse supports it: *“He conducts every affair from the heavens to the earth, then it all ascends to Him on a Day whose length is a thousand years by your counting”* (32:5).

But the readings are not exclusive, and holding them together is more faithful to the verse than choosing. What all three share — and what the verse needs — is that the human and divine measures of duration are not the same. A thousand years is not a threat; it is a statement about scale. From the divine side of the comparison, the interval between the warning and the punishment is not long. From the human side, it is the whole of a life.

**The Taunt's Own Logic**

There is a further point the Qur'an makes about demands of this kind, and it is worth stating here because it completes the answer. A person demanding the punishment as a proof would not be satisfied by it if it came, because the demand is not really for evidence.

The sūrah has already said as much about the Hour: once the signs arrive, believing does nothing for a person who did not believe before (6:158). The demand to hurry the punishment is, structurally, a demand that God settle the question in a way that removes the need for faith — and the whole design of the testing period is that the question is *not* settled that way.

The delay the mockers cite against the Prophet is the same delay that keeps them alive to object. *"If Allah were to punish people ˹immediately˺ for their wrongdoing, He would not have left a single living being on earth. But He delays them for an appointed term"* (16:61). The respite is not a gap in the system; it is the system. Every day of it is a day in which the objection can still be withdrawn, and the taunt is an argument against the very arrangement that makes the taunter's existence possible.

**The Number, and the Phrase After It**

The figure is *alf sanah*, a thousand years — the largest round number in ordinary Arabic usage, and chosen for exactly that reason. It is not a measurement; it is the limit of what a listener can picture, offered as a token of something that cannot be pictured.

The words that follow matter more than the number. *Mimmā taʿuddūna* — of what you count. The measure being converted is ours: our days, our arithmetic, our sense of what counts as a long time. The verse does not say that God experiences a day as a thousand years, which would merely relocate the clock. It says that the two measures are incommensurable, and that the human one is the one being set aside.

That is the answer to the taunt. "Late" is a judgement made inside a system of counting, and the verse says the divine promise is not running on that system. A person who concludes from a decade of silence that nothing is coming has applied his own arithmetic to something that was never on his clock.

**What the Verse Does for the Prophet**

The practical effect of the verse is to remove the taunt's sting. A messenger told that a day with God is a thousand years is not being told that his opponents will wait a long time for punishment. He is being told that the delay he is mocked for is not a delay at all from the only vantage point that counts.

What he is given is not a date. It would be a misreading to hear the verse as "wait a little longer," because nothing in it promises that the wait will be short measured in his years — and the sūrah will go on to describe a community that emigrates, fights, and is tested long before it returns to the sanctuary (22:39–41, 22:58). What he receives instead of a date is a scale, and the scale is what makes the wait answerable to him: the silence is not accumulating against the promise, because the promise is not being counted in the units he is being mocked in.

That is the same consolation the catalogue of verses 42 to 44 offered from the other side: the deniers were given length, and then they were taken. Here the length is explained; there it was documented. Between them, the sūrah has answered both halves of the objection — that the punishment is late, and that being late means it is not coming.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:48

> Many are the societies whose end We delayed while they did wrong, then seized them. And to Me is the final return.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Restates Verse 45 With One Word Changed**

*Wa-ka-ayyin min qaryatin amlaytu lahā wa-hiya ẓālimatun thumma akhadhtuhā* — and how many a town did I grant respite while it was doing wrong, then I seized it.

The sentence is almost identical to verse 45, and the difference is one verb. Verse 45 said *ahlaknāhā*, We destroyed it. Verse 48 says *amlaytu lahā*, I gave it rein. The destruction is still there, at the end, but the emphasis has moved from the end to the interval.

This is a deliberate repetition with a purpose, and recognising the purpose is the key to the passage. Verse 45 established that destruction happens. Verse 48 establishes what happens in between: a length of time, granted by God, during which the wrongdoing continued and nothing appeared to be done about it.

**Delay as a Deliberate Act**

The verb *amlā* is significant. It is the word for letting out a rope, for giving an animal more rein, for allowing someone to continue without interruption. It describes a decision rather than an oversight.

That has a consequence for how the interval is read. The centuries during which a wrongdoing community flourished were not a period in which God was unaware or unable. They were granted. The Qur'an has a technical term for this condition and a doctrine built on it — the drawing-on of a person by stages, while he interprets the silence as approval.

Sūrah al-Anʿām states the mechanism: *“When they became oblivious to warnings, We showered them with everything they desired. But just as they became prideful of what they were given, We seized them by surprise, then they instantly fell into despair!”* (6:44). The gifts keep arriving, the warnings stop feeling urgent, and the recipient concludes that the silence of heaven is a verdict.

**"Then I Seized It"**

*Thumma akhadhtuhā* — then I took it. As in verse 44, the verb is plain and the *thumma* marks an interval whose length is not stated.

The pairing of the two clauses is the whole doctrine: a long silence, then a single action. The sūrah is describing the same pattern it has described three times already — in the respite granted to the deniers of verse 44, in the Hour that is coming rather than arrived (22:7), and now here.

For the audience in Makkah, the application was direct and uncomfortable. They were, at that moment, inside the interval. The town they lived in was prosperous, the trade routes were working, and nothing had happened to them. The verse's point is that this is exactly what the interval before the seizure looks like, in every recorded case.

**"To Me Is the Destination"**

*Wa-ilayya al-maṣīr* — and to Me is the place of arrival. The noun is from the root meaning to become, to arrive at a state, to end up somewhere.

The clause is short and it closes the passage, and it does two things at once. It states where the delayed communities went — not merely that they were destroyed but that they arrived somewhere, and that the somewhere is God. And it states the same for the people hearing the verse: the destination is the same for everyone, and the only question is the condition in which a person arrives.

**Why the Repetition Is Not Redundancy**

A reader in a hurry will see verses 45 and 48 as the same verse twice, and will be wrong. Verse 45 was addressed to the evidence: look at what is left of them. Verse 48 is addressed to the objection: they were given time, and you are being given time.

The two are answering different questions. The first answers "does God destroy communities?" The second answers "then why is nothing happening?" — which is precisely the question the previous verse recorded being thrown at the Prophet.

**The Sūrah Returns to Where It Began**

The respite theme is what ties this passage back to the sūrah's opening, and the connection is worth drawing out because the chapter is nearly two-thirds over by now.

Verse 1 announced a quaking that had not happened. Verse 5 addressed people *in doubt* about a resurrection that had not happened. Verse 7 said the Hour is coming. Verses 8 to 13 described people who argued without ground. Verses 42 to 48 have now supplied the historical pattern behind all of it: communities are given length, and then they are taken.

The sūrah will return to the same point one more time, in verse 55, where the disbelievers are said to remain in doubt until the Hour comes upon them suddenly. That verse is the closing bracket on an argument this sūrah has been making since its first line.

Read in that light, the delay is not a subplot of the sūrah; it is the sūrah's central subject, and the pilgrimage passage at its heart is the rite a community performs while it waits.

**The Passage's Conclusion**

Verses 42 to 48 form one movement, and its shape is worth seeing whole. Nine communities, all denied, all given length, all seized. Ruins visible on the trade routes. A demand for the punishment to be hurried. An answer about the measure of time. And then this verse, which restates the entire pattern in a single sentence with the delay put at the centre.

What the sūrah has established by the end of it is that the apparent inactivity of God is a feature of how history runs, and that it has never, in any recorded case, meant what the mockers take it to mean.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:49

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “O humanity! I am only sent to you with a clear warning.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet Is Told What to Say About Himself**

*Qul yā ayyuhā al-nāsu innamā anā lakum nadhīrun mubīn* — Say: O humanity, I am only, for you, a clear warner.

The verse begins with the imperative *qul*, say. The Qur'an uses this formula when it wants the Prophet to announce something in the first person, and what he is told to announce here is a description of his own office rather than a doctrine.

That is itself a claim worth noticing. The message is not "say that God is one" or "say that the Hour is coming." It is "say what you are." The audience has been demanding signs, hurrying the punishment, and disputing about the messenger, and the response is a statement of job description.

The same imperative opens the comparable definitions elsewhere, and the repetition tells the reader that this is a fixed reply to a fixed objection. When the disbelievers ask why no sign comes down, the answer given to the Prophet is: *"You ˹O Prophet˺ are only a warner. And every people had a guide"* (13:7). When he is told to state the limits of his own knowledge, the answer takes the same shape: *"Say, 'I have no power to benefit or protect myself, except by the Will of Allah… I am only a warner and deliverer of good news for those who believe'"* (7:188). The office is defined by the imperative in every case, as though the definition were something the Prophet needed to hear as much as his opponents did.

**"Only" Is the Load-Bearing Word**

*Innamā* is the Arabic particle of restriction: I am *only* a warner, nothing else and nothing more.

The particle does two things at once, and both are needed. It subtracts: the Prophet is not a manager of outcomes, not a dispenser of the punishment, not a ruler over people's consciences. And it adds: what he is, he is completely. A warner without qualification is not a partial messenger.

The subtraction is the answer to the demand recorded in verse 47. The mockers asked him to bring the punishment, which is a request for a function he was never given. The verse says so plainly, and it says it as a definition rather than as an excuse.

**What a Warner Is Not**

The Qur'an is consistent about the limits of the office, and the consistency is the point. A *nadhīr* warns: he tells people what is coming and what the consequences are. He does not compel, he does not settle disputes by force, and he is not responsible for whether anyone listens.

Those limits are not a diminution of the role. They are what make the warning a moral act rather than a threat. A person who is warned retains the ability to respond; a person who is compelled does not. The whole structure of the testing period described in this sūrah — the respite granted to wrongdoing communities, the Hour that is coming rather than arrived — depends on the warning being the only thing that arrives.

The Qur'an states the exemption in so many words: *"We have surely sent you with the truth ˹O Prophet˺ as a deliverer of good news and a warner. And you will not be accountable for the residents of the Hellfire"* (2:119). The accountability that is removed is the accountability the Prophet's opponents were trying to hand him — for their response to what he brought.

**Why the Warning Must Be "Clear"**

*Mubīn* means clear, evident, manifest — the same adjective the sūrah used for the verses sent down in 22:16 and for the scripture the disputant lacked in 22:8.

Clarity is a moral requirement in the Qur'an, not a stylistic preference. A warning that could reasonably be misunderstood leaves the person warned with an excuse, and the entire edifice of judgment requires that no one has one. The messengers were sent with clear proofs and clear warnings for exactly that reason.

This is also why the clarity is attributed to the warning rather than to the warner. It is not that the Prophet is an especially lucid man; it is that what he brought is unambiguous about what is being asked and what is being threatened.

**A Refrain on Many Messengers' Lips**

The formula is not unique to Muhammad ﷺ, and the Qur'an puts it into the mouths of the messengers whose communities were just listed. Noah says it, Hūd says it, Ṣāliḥ says it, Shuʿayb says it, and the Prophet is told to say it in the same words.

The repetition across the sūrahs is a claim about the unity of the message rather than a literary device. Every messenger brought the same thing: a warning, clearly stated, to his own people. The variations were in what each community did about it.

The same unity is stated from God's side in the verse that gives the purpose of sending them at all: *"We have sent messengers only as deliverers of good news and as warners. Whoever believes and does good, there will be no fear for them, nor will they grieve"* (6:48). The two functions are named together there, as they are in 7:188, and the pairing is the reason the office has the shape it has: good news can be refused, and a warning can be heeded, and neither of those is possible unless the recipient is left free.

**The Address Is the Widest One Available**

*Yā ayyuhā al-nāsu* — O humanity. The same formula with which this sūrah opened (22:1), and it is not the formula most of the Medinan passages use.

The choice is deliberate and it matches the claim being made. The Prophet is not being told to announce his office to a tribe, a city or a community of followers. He is being told to announce it to everyone, which is consistent with the sūrah's opening move of addressing the Hour to all humanity rather than to a congregation.

It is also consistent with the verse that appears earlier in this sūrah in which God is described as judging between all the communities by name (22:17). The message goes out to the same breadth as the judgment will cover.

**Where the Verse Sits**

Placed immediately after the taunt and the answer about the measure of time, verse 49 is the conclusion of that exchange. The Prophet has been asked to do something that is not his job, and he is told to say so — to the whole of humanity, in the second person, without qualification.

The address to humanity also settles a question the sūrah raised and left open in verse 47. The taunt was directed at the Prophet personally, and the answer was addressed to him. Here the reply is redirected outward: the statement of his office is not a private reassurance given to a harassed man but a public announcement made to the people who were harassing him. The warning he carries is the only part of the exchange that concerns them.

The next two verses state what follows from the warning for each of the two groups, which is the only remaining thing a warner has to say.

**The Verse's Restriction Is Also Its Dignity**

It is easy to read *innamā anā nadhīrun mubīn* as a demotion — I am only this, I cannot do more — and that reading gets the tone wrong.

What the particle removes is responsibility for outcomes, not significance of office. The Prophet is not accountable for whether Makkah accepts the warning; he is accountable for delivering it clearly, and the clarity is in his mandate rather than in his talents. Every messenger before him had the same brief and the same limits, and the sūrah has just spent three verses listing them.

The dignity lies in the second half of the formula. A *nadhīr* is someone sent *for you* — *lakum*, for your benefit — with a message that matters enough to be delivered in person. Being only a warner is not a reduction of the job. It is the job, and the job has never been anything else.

The scope of the audience is what finally settles the question of tone. A man sent with a warning to his own city is a local figure; a man told to stand before humanity and say "I am only sent to you with a clear warning" is being given the widest commission in the sūrah, in the same breath in which his powers are being narrowed. The restriction and the universality belong together: because he cannot compel anyone, he can be sent to everyone.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:50

> So those who believe and do good will have forgiveness and an honourable provision.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Forgiveness Comes First**

*Fa-alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti lahum maghfiratun* — so those who believe and do sound deeds, for them is forgiveness.

The order is fixed and it is the whole structure of the promise. Forgiveness is mentioned before provision, and the Arabic gives it the front position in the sentence.

The reason is not rhetorical balance. Forgiveness deals with what a person has already done; provision deals with what he will be given. The first is a settlement of the past and the second is an arrangement for the future, and a religion that offered the second without the first would be offering payment without pardon. Every person who comes to this verse arrives with a record.

The sūrah said as much in verse 10: what is at the judgment is what your hands sent ahead, and the record is complete. Forgiveness is the removal of that, and it is placed first because it is the thing that has to happen before anything else could matter.

**"A Noble Provision"**

*Wa-rizqun karīm* — and a noble provision. The adjective is *karīm*, a word with a wide field: generous, noble, honourable, plentiful, fine.

The range matters, because the promise is not that the provision will be large. *Karīm* describes the manner of the giving at least as much as the quantity given. A provision that is *karīm* is given generously, by a giver who is not counting, in a way that honours the recipient rather than merely sustaining him.

The same pairing appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in the same formula, which confirms that this is a standing description of the reward rather than a phrase coined for this verse. What is promised to the believers is not a wage computed against their works; it is provision given in the manner of a generous host.

**The Third Statement of This Promise**

This is the sūrah's third formulation of the believers' reward, and the repetition is worth tracking rather than skipping. Verse 14 gave the Gardens with rivers beneath them. Verse 23 added the bracelets of gold and the silk. Verse 50 gives forgiveness and a noble provision.

The three are not competing accounts. They describe the same thing from three directions: location (the Garden), honour (the adornment), and relationship (forgiveness, then provision). Together they cover what a person could want from a reward — somewhere to be, dignity, and a settled account.

**The Two Halves Match the Sūrah's Two Failures**

Read against the sūrah's earlier figures, the pairing is exact. The man on the brink of verse 11 wanted the provision without the forgiveness: he was content with what he was given and wanted no part of the accounting. The disputants of verses 3 and 8 wanted to argue about the Recipient and had no interest in either half.

Forgiveness and provision, in that order, are the answer to both. The man who wants only the second is the man who lost both worlds; the man who accepts the first receives the second as something other than a wage.

**Provision, Not Wages**

The word is *rizq*, provision — the same word used for the sacrificial animals in verses 28 and 34, and for what the believers spend from in verse 35. It is not a word for payment.

That distinction carries weight in a sūrah that has been insistent about grace. Verse 14 said God *admits* the believers to the Gardens; verse 23 said they are *adorned*, passively; and this verse offers provision rather than remuneration. Nothing in the sūrah's vocabulary of reward suggests a transaction that God is settling.

The practical consequence is that the reward is not proportioned to the works, and the verse does not invite anyone to compute it. Karīm provision is what a generous giver gives, and what he gives is his to determine.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The verse also declines two things that readers regularly add to it.

It does not say the provision is in the Hereafter only. The word is unqualified, and the Qur'an uses the same phrase for what God gives in this life as well as the next; the promise is not dated.

And it does not say that the believers will be spared suffering in the interval. The sūrah has just described a community authorized to fight after being expelled from its homes, and it will describe martyrs in verse 58. What is promised is forgiveness and a noble provision, and neither is a promise that the interval will be comfortable.

That omission is itself part of the sūrah's honesty. A promise of ease would have been contradicted within a generation, and the Qur'an does not make promises that experience can falsify. What it promises is that the account is settled and the provision will come — neither of which is the same as comfort.

**The Verse Answers the Demand of Verse 47**

There is a direct line from the taunt to this promise. The opponents demanded that the punishment be brought quickly, and the sūrah's answer has been about the measure of time. Verse 50 says what is actually on offer in the interval: not the punishment of the mockers but the forgiveness of those who believe.

That is the asymmetry the sūrah keeps returning to. The warning is real and the punishment is certain, and what the Prophet was sent to announce in the meantime is a promise. A community that hurries the punishment is asking for something that would close the door this verse opens — and the punishment, when it comes, arrives only after the door has been open for as long as anyone wanted it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:51

> But those who strive to discredit Our revelations, they will be the residents of the Hellfire.”
**Expanded Commentary**

**Not Disbelief but Campaigning**

*Wa-alladhīna saʿaw fī āyātinā muʿājizīna* — and those who strove against Our signs, seeking to frustrate.

The verb *saʿā* means to strive, to exert oneself, to work at something with effort. It is the same verb used for a person running to the prayer or working for a livelihood. What is described here is not a state of unbelief but an expenditure of energy.

That is the verse's target, and it is narrower and more serious than simple denial. A person can disbelieve passively, and the Qur'an treats that as one thing. This verse describes people who work at it: who organize, argue, mock, and spend their days trying to make the signs ineffective.

The verb's other use in the Qur'an shows what is being graded. The same imperative is addressed to the believers about the Friday prayer: *"O believers! When the call to prayer is made on Friday, then proceed ˹diligently˺ to the remembrance of Allah and leave off ˹your˺ business"* (62:9). Striving is not the offence; it is the raw material of every human life. What distinguishes the two groups is not that one expends itself and the other does not, but where the expenditure is pointed. Both verses use the same verb for the same human capacity, and the sūrah is interested in the direction of the effort rather than in its quantity.

**What "Muʿājizīn" Means**

The participle carries two readings, and the commentators recorded both.

On the first, it means *trying to escape* — striving to outrun the judgment and put themselves beyond God's reach. On the second, it means *trying to frustrate* — working to render the signs powerless, to defeat them, to nullify what they were sent to do. The root contains the idea of making someone unable to act.

Both readings fit the people the sūrah has been describing since verse 3: those who dispute about God without knowledge, who turn their shoulders to lead others astray, who call the revelation ancient fables, and who demand the punishment as a way of showing that it will not come. What all of it has in common is effort directed at making the message not work.

The word appears elsewhere in exactly this construction, and the parallel confirms the second reading: *"As for those who strive to discredit Our revelations, it is they who will suffer the ˹worst˺ torment of agonizing pain"* (34:5). There the striving is met with the one thing it was trying to escape, and the punishment is described as a place from which there is no exit — the frustration the strivers wanted to inflict on the signs is the frustration they arrive at.

**What the Campaign Looked Like**

The Qur'an records the specific tactics, and they are worth listing because they are not what modern readers expect. The opponents did not typically argue that God does not exist. They disputed *about the signs* (40:35), they plotted against them (10:21), and they deviated with regard to them (41:40).

The most common tactic was reclassification: calling the revelation sorcery, poetry, or tales of the ancients. Each of those moves takes the message out of the category where it demands a response and puts it in a category where it can be dismissed. That is a genuinely clever strategy, and the Qur'an treats it as the serious offence it is.

Two further tactics are recorded from Makkah, and both are attempts to change the terms of the exchange rather than to answer it. One was to ask for the message itself to be revised: *"Bring us a different Quran or make some changes in it"* (10:15) — a request framed as a negotiation, to which the Prophet was told to answer that he does not alter what is revealed to him. Another was to claim the ability to match it and then decline to bother: *"We have already heard ˹the recitation˺. If we wanted, we could have easily produced something similar. This ˹Quran˺ is nothing but ancient fables!"* (8:31). The claim of ability is unfalsifiable by design, and the demand for a spring to gush from the earth (17:90–93) belongs to the same family: conditions set so that the sign, if given, would be reclassified as well.

**"The Companions of the Fire"**

*Ulāʾika aṣḥābu al-jaḥīmi* — those are the companions of the Fire.

The word *aṣḥāb* means companions, associates, those who belong together. It is the same word used for the companions of the Garden, and the symmetry is deliberate: each group ends up with what it kept company with.

The noun also implies permanence in a way that "inhabitants" does not. A person can pass through a place; a companion belongs to it. The verse is not describing people who visit the Fire but people whose association with it is the settled fact about them — which is what the campaign of the first half of the verse has been building, act by act.

The Qur'an uses the same noun for both destinations in a single comparison, and the inequality it draws is between the two companies rather than between two locations: *"The residents of the Fire cannot be equal to the residents of Paradise. ˹Only˺ the residents of Paradise will be successful"* (59:20). Elsewhere the companionship is stated with the adverb that settles the question: *"But those who disbelieve and deny Our signs will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever"* (2:39). Belonging is permanent on both sides, and the sūrah's point is that the permanence is what the striving was building all along.

**Why Effort Is the Aggravating Factor**

The sūrah has already graded offences by whether they stay private, and it does so again here. Verse 9 described people who disputed, and then described people who disputed *in order to lead others astray* — the purpose clause was what doubled the sentence.

Verse 51 works the same way. Disbelief is one thing; striving against the signs is another, and the difference is the energy. A person who simply does not believe has declined something. A person who works to make the signs ineffective has joined a campaign against them, and his sentence is described in terms of what he has joined.

The principle behind the grading is stated in a single line of Sūrah al-Najm, using the same root as this verse: *"and that each person will only have what they endeavoured towards"* (53:39). What a person gets is what he worked for, and the work is counted as his own. That is why the verse can describe the punishment as the appropriate outcome rather than an arbitrary one: the strivers spent their effort making the signs ineffective, and what they receive is the one condition in which the signs are no longer available to be acted on.

**"Our Signs" — What Is Being Attacked**

*Āyātunā*, Our signs. The word *āyah* covers the verses of the revelation and the phenomena of the natural world, and the Qur'an uses both senses deliberately in the same word.

That breadth matters for reading the offence. The campaign described here is not only against a text. It is against the evidence as well — against reading the created world as evidence, which is precisely what verses 5, 18 and 46 of this sūrah demanded. Striving against the signs is the attempt to make the world unreadable.

It is worth being concrete about what that looks like. The sūrah's own signs are the embryo in the womb, the field that greens under rain, the ships held on the sea, the submission of everything in creation. Each of these is an ordinary occurrence with an interpretation attached. The campaign of this verse is the effort to detach the occurrence from the interpretation — to leave the world in place and empty it of meaning.

That is a more ambitious project than atheism, and the Qur'an treats it as the more serious offence. A person who denies God outright has taken a position that can be argued with. A person who works to make the world illegible has removed the ground on which any argument could stand.

**The Verse's Pairing With Verse 50**

The two verses are one sentence in argument if not in grammar: believers get forgiveness and a noble provision, and the strivers against the signs get the Fire.

The sūrah has used this pairing repeatedly — verses 19 and 23, verse 14 against verses 11 to 13, and now here — and each time the two halves are described in matching terms. The believers receive; the strivers belong. Neither is described as a surprise to the person it happens to.

What is new in this pairing is the emphasis on effort. Verse 50's people believed and did sound deeds, which is also an expenditure. Verse 51's people strove, and the striving is what the verse names them by. Two groups, both working, arriving at two destinations — and the difference is not the energy but its direction.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:52

> Whenever We sent a messenger or a prophet before you ˹O Prophet˺ and he recited ˹Our revelations˺, Satan would influence ˹people’s understanding of˺ his recitation. But ˹eventually˺ Allah would eliminate Satan’s influence. Then Allah would ˹firmly˺ establish His revelations. And Allah is AllKnowing, All-Wise.
**Expanded Commentary**

**What the Verse Actually Says**

*Wa-mā arsalnā min qablika min rasūlin wa-lā nabiyyin illā idhā tamannā alqā al-shayṭānu fī umniyyatihi* — We sent no messenger or prophet before you but that, when he *tamannā*, Satan cast something into his *umniyyah*.

The two words govern every reading of the verse. *Tamannā* means to long for, to wish, to desire. *Umiyyah*, from the same root, means a longing, a desire, or, in several Qur'anic occurrences, a false hope — as in the hearsay of 2:78, the wishes of 2:111, the desires of 4:119, and the false hopes of 57:14. A secondary sense of "recitation" exists and is the one many commentators adopted, but it is rare and it cuts against the Qur'an's own usage of the term.

The translation used here takes *umniyyah* as the recitation the messenger delivered, and reads the casting as interference with how that recitation lands rather than with what was revealed. That reading has strong support, and the rest of this commentary is built on it.

**"Messenger or Prophet"**

The verse names both categories: no *rasūl* and no *nabī* was sent before you without this happening. The pairing is not redundant for its own sake; it is the Arabic way of closing off every exception.

The two words have been distinguished in various ways in the tradition — a messenger as one sent with a new scripture, a prophet as one who calls to an existing one — but the point of naming both here is totality. Whoever is sent, in whatever capacity, meets interference. The verse is not describing an occasional hazard of one office; it is describing a standing feature of the job.

**Interference With Reception, Not With Revelation**

On this reading the verse makes a claim that the rest of the Qur'an makes repeatedly: a messenger delivers, and the delivery is met with interference. Satan casts into the reception — into what people hear, into the interpretation that circulates, into the rumour that follows the message around.

This is not a small claim, and it is not consolation. It says that the corruption of a message after it leaves the messenger's mouth is a predictable feature of the world rather than a sign that the message failed. Every messenger experienced it.

Al-Rāzī recorded a related reading with approval: that the verse establishes the humanity of the prophets. They are not angels; they feel, long for and suffer rejection, and they are not impervious to prompting. What protects them is not insensibility but God. The Qur'an describes the Prophet as grief-stricken at his people's refusal — *“Perhaps you ˹O Prophet˺ will grieve yourself to death over their denial”* (18:6) — which is the condition this verse speaks to.

**The Story of the Cranes**

No verse in this sūrah comes trailing a longer and more contested appendix. A number of early commentators connected this verse — along with Sūrah 53's verses on the goddesses and the passage at 17:73–75 — to an account known as the "story of the cranes," and in Western literature as the "Satanic Verses."

The account, in its various versions, runs as follows. The Prophet was reciting Sūrah al-Najm near the Kaʿbah. After the verses naming three of the goddesses, Satan cast words onto his tongue during a lapse, and he was heard to utter a sentence praising them as exalted intercessors whose mediation could be hoped for. The Quraysh, pleased that their goddesses had been honoured, joined the prostration at the sūrah's close. Gabriel afterwards corrected him, and this verse was revealed to console him.

**Why the Account Fails**

The grounds for rejecting it are cumulative, and the classical commentators assembled them methodically.

*Transmission.* The versions contradict one another on who spoke the words, whether they were spoken at all, and what happened next. Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both noted that early exegetes and historians applied inconsistent filters to what they recorded, and that the story is poorly sourced.

*Psychology.* A man known for trustworthiness, who had endured years of persecution for condemning those very idols and had refused offers of wealth and power to abandon his mission, would have destroyed his own credibility by making the concession. Al-Rāzī observed that the struggle against idols was what defined the mission. Al-Māturīdī put the sharper question: if the Prophet could not tell one caller from the other the first time, on what basis would he have recognised the second one as the correction?

*Chronology.* Most versions date the incident to year five of the calling. Sūrah 17 is placed five or six years after that, and this sūrah later still. A verse sent down to console the Prophet for an episode eight years in the past would be anomalous, and nothing accounts for the alleged verses circulating in the interval.

*Theology.* The prophets are held to be protected from error of this kind. Al-Qurṭubī rejects the premise outright, citing Satan's own testimony: *“I did not have any authority over you. I only called you, and you responded to me”* (14:22). If Satan could seize a person's speech, no one could withstand it.

*Literary fit.* The verse's two halves are not joined in the manner the account requires. God first wipes out what Satan cast, and afterwards establishes His signs — so the establishing follows the erasure rather than being produced by it, and it attaches most naturally to verse 51: they labour to frustrate the signs, and God makes them firm. The alleged interpolation would also contradict the sūrah it supposedly entered, which argues that intercession belongs to God alone (53:26).

**What Remains**

Set the story aside and the verse is clearer, not thinner. What it says is that every messenger's delivery was interfered with, that God removes the interference, and that God then establishes His signs — in that order, and as three separate acts.

The closing names are the verse's own emphasis: *All-Knowing*, so the interference was not hidden from Him, and *All-Wise*, so the interval in which it operated was not a lapse. What looks like a message being corrupted is, on the sūrah's account, a message being delivered, interfered with, and then established more firmly than before.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:53

> All that so He may make Satan’s influence a trial for those ˹hypocrites˺ whose hearts are sick and those ˹disbelievers˺ whose hearts are hardened. Surely the wrongdoers are totally engrossed in opposition.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Purpose Clause Explains the Interference**

*Li-yajʿala mā yulqī al-shayṭānu fitnatan* — so that He makes what Satan casts a trial.

This is the answer to the difficulty the previous verse created. If Satan is permitted to interfere with the delivery of revelation, the obvious question is why. The verse answers that the interference is not permitted as a defeat; it is made into a *fitnah*, a test.

That is a theodicy, and it is worth stating in its strongest form. God does not author the interference — the verse attributes the casting to Satan and the testing to God. What God authors is the use to which the interference is put: it becomes a sorting mechanism.

Theologians disputed how the *li-* here should be read. One reading takes it as the *lām* of purpose: God permits the casting in order to test.
