Another takes it as the *lām* of consequence — the *lām al-ʿāqibah* of Arabic grammar, in which an outcome is described as though it were an aim, because what God allows stands in the relation of an aim to His wisdom. The dispute was about how to keep the permission from becoming an authorship: on either reading, the casting belongs to Satan and the trial to God, and the verse's own word order is what keeps the two apart.

**A Fitnah Is Not a Temptation Only**

The word *fitnah* covers trial, testing, temptation and sedition, and the Qur'an uses it for all of them. The root sense is of putting something to the test by fire — assaying metal to see what it is.

Applied to the interference of verse 52, the sense is that the same event reveals what was already in a person. Nothing is created in him by the test; what is created is the visibility. That is why the sūrah can describe the interference as a trial without making God the author of anybody's unbelief.

The word's other occurrences confirm that the fire in the root is the point. Persecution is called *fitnah* and declared worse than killing (2:191); war is to be waged *until there is no more fitnah* (8:39). In both, the trial is external pressure applied to a community to see what it is made of. Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt applies the same word to the believers themselves: *"Do people think once they say, 'We believe,' that they will be left without being put to the test?"* (29:2). A *fitnah* is therefore not a trap set for the weak but the ordinary condition of a claim that has been announced; what differs here is that the pressure is an ambiguity in the revelation rather than a persecution outside it.

**"Those in Whose Hearts Is Sickness"**

*Alladhīna fī qulūbihim maraḍ* — those in whose hearts is sickness. The noun *maraḍ* is disease, and the Qur'an uses it for the heart repeatedly and consistently.

The commentators understood it as covering hypocrisy, doubt, ignorance and the absence of settled faith. The usage supports them: *“There is sickness in their hearts, and Allah ˹only˺ lets their sickness increase”* (2:10), with the same phrase applied elsewhere to the waverers (9:125; 33:12) and to those who say one thing and believe another (47:20).

What is striking is the progression the Qur'an describes. The sickness *increases*. It is not a static condition that a person has; it is a condition that develops by being indulged, and the verses that mention it almost always add the increase.

The sickness also has a visible symptom, and the Qur'an names it: calculation. *"You see those with sickness in their hearts racing for their guardianship, saying ˹in justification˺, 'We fear a turn of fortune will strike us.' But perhaps Allah will bring about ˹your˺ victory or another favour by His command, and they will regret what they have hidden in their hearts"* (5:52). What is diseased is the faculty that weighs claims, so the person with the sickness divides his allegiances by expected outcome rather than by truth — which is precisely the disposition an ambiguous event will expose.

**"Those Whose Hearts Are Hardened"**

*Wa-l-qāsiyati qulūbuhum* — and those whose hearts are hard. A second group, and the pairing with the first matters.

The sick heart is unsettled; the hard heart has settled, in the wrong direction. One wavers and the other has stopped moving. The Qur'an's vocabulary for hardness is precise: *“Can ˹the misguided be like˺ those whose hearts Allah has opened to Islam, so they are enlightened by their Lord? So woe to those whose hearts are hardened at the remembrance of Allah!”* (39:22). Hardness is defined by what it does not respond to.

Sūrah al-Ḥadīd gives the mechanism and the cure in one verse: *“Has the time not yet come for believers’ hearts to be humbled at the remembrance of Allah and what has been revealed of the truth, and not be like those given the Scripture before—˹those˺ who were spoiled for so long that their hearts became hardened?”* (57:16).

**Why the Heart Is the Site**

The sūrah has been consistent about where the problem lies, and the consistency is a claim about human nature rather than a turn of phrase.

Verse 32 located the value of the rites in the reverence of hearts. Verse 35 described the humble as those whose hearts tremble at the mention of God. Verse 46 diagnosed the failure as blindness of the hearts in the breasts. Verse 54 will describe knowledge ending in hearts that humble themselves.

The heart, in this usage, is neither the organ of emotion opposed to reason nor a metaphor for the self. It is the faculty that receives, weighs and settles. When the Qur'an says it is sick or hard, it means that the apparatus by which a person evaluates a claim has been damaged — which is why more evidence does not help, and why the damage is described in the language of disease and hardness rather than of ignorance.

**"The Wrongdoers Are in Far Schism"**

*Wa-inna al-ẓālimīna la-fī shiqāqin baʿīd* — and the wrongdoers are in a schism far away.

*Shiqāq* is from a root meaning to split or to be at the side of: it is the state of standing apart in opposition, a fracture rather than a disagreement. The same word is used for the fracture between a community and the truth it was given.

The word *baʿīd*, far, completes the description. These people are not near the truth and slightly off. The sūrah has used this language before — the far straying of verse 12 — and it uses distance consistently to describe the gap between a person and what he was made for.

The grammar turns the opposition into a location. They are *fī shiqāqin*, in a schism, not merely holding one: the fracture is the element they now occupy, and the preposition is the same one the verse used for the sickness in their hearts. The same construction elsewhere measures belief by its opposite: *"So if they believe in what you believe, then they will indeed be ˹rightly˺ guided. But if they turn away, they are simply opposed ˹to the truth˺"* (2:137), where the Arabic has the same *fī shiqāqin*. Opposition in this usage is not an argument a person makes; it is a position he stands in, and the distance is measured from the truth rather than from the community he is arguing with.

**Why a Test Is Not a Trap**

The obvious objection to verse 53 is that a God who makes Satan's interference into a trial is setting people up. If the interference produces the unbelief it punishes, the test is not a test but a mechanism.

The classical answer has two parts, and both are in the text. First, the casting is attributed to Satan and the testing to God — two different agents, two different acts. What God authors is not the interference but its function. Second, the two groups in this verse are described as already having the condition that the test reveals: the sickness and the hardness are mentioned as existing before the trial operates on them.

That second point is the one that matters. A trial in the Qur'an's usage never creates the trait it exposes. It is the difference between a test and a seduction, and the sūrah is careful to keep the two apart: the verb of making trial belongs to God, and the verb of casting belongs to Satan.

The practical corollary is uncomfortable for the reader. The question the verse poses to anyone facing a confusing piece of evidence is not "is this clear?" but "what does my heart do with it?" — and both answers are treated as disclosures rather than as verdicts.

**The Two Groups and the Same Event**

The verse's structure is the point it is making. One event — the interference with the delivered message — produces opposite effects in two different kinds of people.

In those whose hearts are sick, it confirms the doubt they were already nursing. In those whose hearts are hard, it confirms the refusal they had already settled on. Neither group was made a disbeliever by the event. Both were shown by it.

That is the mechanism by which a test works, and the sūrah states it here so that the next verse can describe the opposite effect in the opposite kind of person.

The mechanism is not unique to this episode, and the Qur'an describes it for revelation as a whole: *"Whoever Allah wills to guide, He opens their heart to Islam. But whoever He wills to leave astray, He makes their chest tight and constricted as if they were climbing up into the sky"* (6:125). One revelation, two chests, and the variable is the receiver. That is what makes a trial a trial: the same message arrives, and the difference in what it produces belongs to the heart it arrives at.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:54

> ˹This is˺ also so that those gifted with knowledge would know that this ˹revelation˺ is the truth from your Lord, so they have faith in it, and so their hearts would submit humbly to it. And Allah surely guides the believers to the Straight Path.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Purpose of the Same Event**

*Wa-li-yaʿlama alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma annahu al-ḥaqqu min rabbika* — and so that those who have been given knowledge may know that it is the truth from your Lord.

The verse is a second purpose clause attached to the same event as verse 53. What Satan casts is a trial for one group and, simultaneously, a confirmation for another. The identical occurrence moves in two directions at once.

That is the sūrah's theodicy completed. The interference is not merely permitted; it is used. The person with a sick heart finds his doubt confirmed by it, and the person with knowledge finds the truth confirmed by it — and what separates them is not information, since both saw the same thing.

**Who "Those Given Knowledge" Are**

The Qur'an uses this phrase with a consistent profile, and it is worth assembling.

They are the ones who recognise the message when they hear it: *“Indeed, when it is recited to those who were gifted with knowledge before it ˹was revealed˺, they fall upon their faces in prostration”* (17:107). They are contrasted with people who prefer this life: *“But those gifted with knowledge said, ‘Shame on you! Allah’s reward is far better for those who believe and do good’”* (28:80). The revelation is described as residing in them: *“But this ˹Quran˺ is ˹a set of˺ clear revelations ˹preserved˺ in the hearts of those gifted with knowledge. And none denies Our revelations except the ˹stubborn˺ wrongdoers”* (29:49), and *“Those gifted with knowledge ˹clearly˺ see that what has been revealed to you from your Lord ˹O Prophet˺ is the truth”* (34:6).

The knowledge in question is therefore not a credential. It is the capacity to recognise the truth when it arrives, and the Qur'an links it directly to belief in the same breath.

**Knowledge Produces Humility, Not Certainty of a Different Kind**

*Fa-yuʾminū bihi fa-tukhbita lahu qulūbuhum* — and so they believe in it, and their hearts humble themselves to it.

The verb is from the same root as *al-mukhbitīn*, the humble ones described in verse 34, whose hearts tremble at the mention of God. The sūrah is deliberately closing a circle: the people promised good news in verse 34 are the people described here, and the mark of both descriptions is not intellectual assent but a heart that has gone quiet.

The sequence is worth holding on to. Knowledge comes first, then faith, then humility — and the order rules out a familiar error. Knowledge that does not end in humility has not, on this verse's account, arrived at its object. A person who knows the truth and remains bristling about it has stopped at the second step.

**Why the Same Event Produces Opposite Results**

The sūrah gives no mechanism, and the silence is instructive. It does not say that the knowledgeable are smarter, or that they were given more evidence, or that they were protected. It says they were given *ʿilm*, and that what they did with it was believe.

The implication is that knowledge functions as a capacity rather than as a content. The same occurrence that confirms a doubter confirms a knower, because each brings to it the thing he has been cultivating. A heart that has practised doubt finds confirmation for doubt; a heart that has attended to evidence finds confirmation for the truth.

That is also why the sūrah can hold the two verses together without tension. Nothing in verse 54 rescinds verse 53. Both are describing what a test does.

**Knowledge Is Described as Something Given**

The phrasing is passive: *alladhīna ūtū al-ʿilma*, those who *were given* knowledge. The Qur'an never uses this expression as a compliment to the person's own achievement.

That is worth holding on to, because it changes what the verse is saying about how people differ. If knowledge were an attainment, the knowledgeable would simply be the diligent and the sick-hearted the lazy, and the difference between verse 53 and verse 54 would be a difference of effort. The grammar rules that out. Both groups received; what they did with what they received is what the two verses record.

The sūrah has insisted on this since verse 16: the signs are clear, and guidance is God's to give. Verse 54 is that principle applied to the specific case of a message that has been interfered with. The knowledgeable recognise the truth not because they worked harder at it but because they were given the capacity and did not refuse it.

**The Verse Answers a Question the Sūrah Has Been Asked Twice**

Twice in this sūrah the Prophet's opponents have demanded something that would settle matters by force: the punishment, hurried up (22:47), and now the interference that they hoped would discredit the message.

Both demands rest on the same assumption — that the truth of the revelation should be demonstrable on the spot, in a form nobody can dispute. The sūrah's answer in both places is to refuse the terms. The message is not established by the removal of every objection; it is established by God, after the objections have had their run.

That is the practical content of this verse. The interference does not prevent the knowledgeable from knowing. It is precisely the circumstance in which their knowledge is exercised, and the recognition that follows from it is worth more than a recognition extracted by a display.

**"God Guides the Believers to a Straight Path"**

*Wa-inna Allāha la-hādī alladhīna āmanū ilā ṣirāṭin mustaqīm* — and God is the guide of those who believe, to a straight path.

The closing clause returns to the theme the sūrah stated in verse 16: guidance is God's, and it is given to those who believe. The order in that sentence is the point — they believe, and then they are guided, and the guidance is described as continuing rather than as a single act of direction.

A *ṣirāṭ mustaqīm* is a road without bends in it, and the promise is not that the believer will find it easy but that he will find it straight. For a community that has just been told that its message will be interfered with, that is the relevant assurance.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:55

> Yet the disbelievers will persist in doubt about this ˹revelation˺ until the Hour takes them by surprise, or the torment of a terminating Day comes to them.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Doubt as a Permanent Posture**

*Wa-lā yazālu alladhīna kafarū fī miryatin minhu* — and those who disbelieve will not cease to be in doubt concerning it.

The verb is *zāla* with the negative, the construction for an unbroken state: they will not stop, they will not leave off. What is described is not a hesitation that will eventually resolve. It is a posture that will be maintained until something external ends it.

*Mirya* is the word for doubt of a particular kind — the doubt that disputes and argues rather than the doubt that simply does not know. It is used elsewhere for the scepticism that demands proofs and then disputes them. The sūrah's doubters have never been short of evidence; they have been short of the willingness to be settled by it.

**The Sūrah's Third Statement of the Doubt**

This is the third time the sūrah has named this condition, and the three statements trace its development.

In verse 5 the doubt was addressed and answered: *if you are in doubt about the Resurrection*, look at the embryo and the field. In verse 7 the doubt was denied an object: there is no doubt in it. Here in verse 55 the doubt is described as permanent, and the thing that will end it is not an argument.

The progression is the sūrah's diagnosis of unbelief in miniature. Doubt begins as a question that can be argued with, becomes a claim that can be refuted, and settles into a condition that nothing except the event itself will interrupt. The sūrah has moved from reasoning with doubters to describing them, and that shift is itself the verdict.

**"Until the Hour Comes Upon Them Suddenly"**

*Ḥattā taʾtiyahum al-sāʿatu baghtatan* — until the Hour comes to them suddenly.

*Baghtatan* is suddenness without warning, the adverb the Qur'an reserves for the arrival of the Hour and of punishment. The sūrah used the same idea in verse 1's quaking and verse 7's Hour that is coming; here the coming is described as overtaking rather than approaching.

The point of the adverb is that the doubters will not be granted a transition. A person who has spent his life in doubt about the Hour will not be given a period of adjustment when it arrives. The dispute that he maintained to the end will be ended by the arrival of the thing he disputed — and the manner of its arrival is precisely what makes the doubt fatal rather than merely mistaken.

**"Or the Punishment of a Barren Day"**

*Aw yaʾtiyahum ʿadhābu yawmin ʿaqīm* — or the punishment of a barren day comes to them.

*ʿAqīm* means barren, sterile, unable to produce. It is used in the Qur'an for a woman who cannot bear and for a wind that brings nothing. Applied to a day, it describes a day after which nothing follows: no tomorrow that repairs it, no morning that undoes what it did.

The adjective is doing the work that "final" or "last" would do less well. A final day is merely the last in a series; a barren day is one that generates nothing, and the terror is in the sterility rather than in the position. Every day a person has lived has produced a next day to which he could carry his plans. This one does not.

**Two Arrivals, Not Two Alternatives**

The verse gives two endings: the Hour, or the punishment of a barren day. The commentators took these as the general arrival and the particular one — the Hour for everyone, and for some an even earlier and more specific punishment.

Either way, the structure of the sentence is the same as the one the sūrah opened with. The end is coming; it is not scheduled by the doubters; and when it arrives it will not be argued with.

**The "Barren Day" and the Question of Badr**

Some commentators took the barren day as a reference to the Day of Resurrection — a day after which there is no other — and the mention of judgment in verse 56 supports that reading. Al-Rāzī recorded a further view: that it refers to the Battle of Badr, a day on which mothers lost sons and which held no blessing for the disbelievers.

Both readings are defensible, and they are not mutually exclusive, since the Qur'an frequently works with a near event and an ultimate one in the same phrase. What matters for reading the verse is the shared sense: a day on which the doubters' calculation stops working, whether it arrives in this life or at the end of it.

The specific application to Badr also explains the placement. The sūrah has just granted permission to fight (22:39) and described the people who were expelled; a warning that a barren day is coming is not, in that setting, an abstraction about the end of time.

**The Doubters' Own Account of Themselves**

The most uncomfortable feature of this verse is that it describes the doubters as continuing rather than as being deceived. They are not, on the sūrah's account, waiting for evidence that never arrives. They have the evidence and have chosen the posture.

That is consistent with the diagnosis offered at verse 8, where the disputant was said to lack knowledge, guidance and an illuminating scripture — three things that were available and that he had none of. And it is consistent with verse 46, where the failure was located in the receiving organ rather than in the signal.

The sūrah is therefore not offering these people a longer period in which to be persuaded. It is describing what they will do with the period: continue. The verb's persistence is the diagnosis, and the only thing the verse names as an end to it is the arrival of the event itself.

**Where the Argument Has Arrived**

Verses 52 to 55 form the sūrah's most compressed statement of why a message can be clear and still rejected. The delivery is interfered with; the interference tests some and confirms others; and the doubters continue in doubt until the event they doubted arrives.

The sūrah is not offering a solution to that problem, because on its own terms there isn't one. What it offers instead is an account of the interval: the message is established, the test is running, and the closing of the argument belongs to God on a day that will not be barren for anyone except those who doubted it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:56

> All authority on that Day is for Allah ˹alone˺. He will judge between them. So those who believe and do good will be in the Gardens of Bliss.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sovereignty on That Day**

*Al-mulku yawmaʾidhin lillāh* — the dominion on that Day belongs to God. The sentence is a noun sentence with a fronted predicate, which in Arabic gives it the force of exclusivity: the authority is God's, and on that Day it is visibly, undeniably His.

The *that Day* is the barren day of the previous verse — the day after which nothing follows. The connection is the point. Sovereignty is not being asserted for a day that will be followed by another arrangement; it is being asserted for the day that ends the series.

There is an implied contrast with every other day. Authority in this life is distributed, contested and mostly invisible; kings, judges and assemblies hold pieces of it and the pieces are frequently in the wrong hands. The Qur'an's claim is not that God is absent from that arrangement but that on this day the distribution stops.

**The Same Claim Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

The statement is one of the Qur'an's fixed formulae for the Resurrection, and the wording is nearly identical wherever it appears: *“the Day all will appear ˹before Allah˺. Nothing about them will be hidden from Him. ˹He will ask,˺ ‘Who does all authority belong to this Day? To Allah—the One, the Supreme!’”* (40:16).

Sūrah al-Infiṭār states it with an added consequence: *“˹It is˺ the Day no soul will be of ˹any˺ benefit to another whatsoever, for all authority on that Day belongs to Allah ˹entirely˺”* (82:19). The two halves belong together. Human authority in this life includes the ability to intercede, to protect, to pull strings. On that day there is none of it, and the reason given is that the authority is not divided.

Sūrah al-Anʿām pairs the same claim with the act of creation itself: *“All authority is His ˹alone˺ on the Day the Trumpet will be blown. He is the Knower of all—seen or unseen”* (6:73).

**"He Will Judge Between Them"**

*Yaḥkumu baynahum* — He judges between them. The preposition *bayna* is the same one the sūrah used in verse 17, when it said that God would judge between the believers, the Jews, the Sabians, the Christians, the Magians and the associators.

The repetition is deliberate: the deferred adjudication of verse 17 is the adjudication being executed here. Everything the sūrah said about religious disputes being settled by God rather than by human verdicts comes due in this clause.

That clause is also the answer to a natural objection. If authority on that day is God's alone, what does He do with it? The verse says: He judges. Sovereignty is not described as raw power exercised for its own sake but as the capacity to settle cases justly, which is what the sūrah argued in verse 10 when it insisted that God is never unjust to His servants.

**The Gardens of Bliss**

*Fa-alladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti fī jannāti al-naʿīm* — so those who believe and do sound deeds are in Gardens of bliss.

The word *naʿīm* is not the ordinary word for pleasure. It denotes ease, softness of life, the condition of a person for whom nothing is sharp or scarce. A garden of *naʿīm* is therefore the opposite of the Fire in a specific register: not heat against cool, but hardship against ease.

The reward is stated in the present tense — they *are* in it — even though the day being described is still future. The Qur'an does this regularly in its judgment scenes, and the effect is to make the outcome as good as settled.

**"On That Day" Implies Other Days**

The phrase *yawmaʾidhin* — on that day — is a demonstrative of time, and the Qur'an uses it almost exclusively for the Resurrection and its scenes.

The implication is that the distribution of authority on other days is different, or at least appears so. In this life, authority is exercised by people who frequently have no right to it and who are rarely held to account for it. The Qur'an's language does not deny that they hold it; it denies that it is ultimately theirs, and it says that the day is coming when the appearance and the reality coincide.

That is why the claim is consoling rather than merely threatening. A person wronged by a judge, a ruler or an occupying power is being told that the arrangement is temporary and that the day on which it ends is a real date in the calendar.

**What "Gardens" Implies About the Body**

The reward is a garden, and the sūrah has been consistent about what that means: a place, with water, shade and food, entered by people who have bodies.

This matters in a sūrah that opened by arguing for bodily resurrection from embryos and dry fields (22:5–7). If the resurrection were of souls only, a garden would be a strange promise, and the sūrah's whole first argument would have been beside the point. The reward and the argument stand together.

**The Verse's Structure**

Three clauses, in order: whose the authority is, what is done with it, and where one group ends up. The sūrah has been building toward this formulation since verse 17, and it will restate it in the next verse for the other group.

What is notable is how little description the reward receives. The gardens of verse 23 had bracelets and silk; this one has only *naʿīm*. The emphasis in this verse falls entirely on the sovereignty clause, and the reward is named as a consequence rather than described as a place.

That economy is deliberate. The sūrah has already given the full description twice, at verse 14 and again at verse 23. By this point in the chapter, the reward does not need to be pictured; it needs to be located. Where it is located is inside a sovereignty that has no partners, and the verse's work is to say who holds the authority rather than what the gardens contain.

There is also a rhetorical effect worth noticing. The verse moves from the largest possible subject — who rules the universe on the final day — to a single clause about a group of people standing in a garden. The contrast is the point: the same sovereignty that settles the fate of every community is the one that attends to these particular believers.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:57

> But those who disbelieve and deny Our revelations, it is they who will suffer a humiliating punishment.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Judgment**

Verse 56 gave the authority, the judgment and the reward. Verse 57 gives the other outcome, and the two verses are one sentence in argument.

The symmetry is exact and it is the point. The believers are *in Gardens of bliss*; the disbelievers *have a humiliating punishment*. Neither outcome is described at length here, because the sūrah has already described both at length — the Gardens in verse 23 and the Fire in verses 19 to 22. What these two verses add is the authority under which the distribution is made.

**Two Conditions Joined**

*Wa-alladhīna kafarū wa-kadhdhabū bi-āyātinā* — and those who disbelieved and denied Our signs.

Two verbs describe one group. The first, *kafarū*, is the state: they covered over, they refused. The second, *kadhdhabū*, is the act: they declared the signs false.

The combination is more specific than either alone. A person can fail to believe through neglect; a person who actively declares the signs false has taken a position on them, and the sūrah has been describing that position since verse 3. The offence here is the same one verse 51 named as striving against the signs — an expenditure of effort rather than a mere absence.

**"A Humiliating Punishment"**

*Fa-ulāʾika lahum ʿadhābun muhīn* — those, for them is a humiliating punishment.

The adjective is *muhīn*, from the root meaning to make small, to abase, to render contemptible. It is the same word the sūrah used for the disgrace promised in verse 9, and the repetition ties the two passages together: what was announced as coming in this world and the next is here described as delivered.

The choice of adjective is precise. The punishment is not described primarily as painful in this verse — the sūrah has used *alīm*, painful, elsewhere and will use it again. Here the emphasis is on the abasement, and the abasement fits the offence. The people described spent their effort trying to make the signs of God ineffective, and what they receive is the experience of being made small.

**Humiliation Is the Natural Answer to Arrogance**

The sūrah has diagnosed its opponents' condition as one of pride more than once. Verse 9 described a man turning his shoulder away in contempt. Verse 15 described a man in a rage at God's help going to someone else. Verse 51 described people striving to frustrate the signs.

What all three share is a refusal to be small. The punishment named here is the imposition of smallness, and the sūrah's logic is not vindictive but reparative: the humiliation is what happens when a claim to greatness is measured against the actual size of the One it was asserted against.

Sūrah al-Kahf makes the same point about the destination rather than the punishment: those who strive against the signs find the worst of places to be, and what makes it worst is not the temperature.

**"Those" — the Emphatic Pronoun**

*Fa-ulāʾika lahum* — so *those*, for them. The Arabic uses the demonstrative pronoun for distance as the subject of the sentence before the predicate arrives.

The effect is to point. The verse is not describing a class in the abstract; it is identifying the specific people who did the denying, and the pointing pronoun is the grammar's way of singling them out for what follows. The sūrah used the same construction in verse 51 for the same group, and it will use it again at verse 72.

There is a secondary effect as well. The distance implied by *ulāʾika* — those, over there — quietly separates the group from the speaker and from the reader. They are named as a party that has been identified, and the pronoun is part of the verdict rather than incidental to it.

**Why the Punishment Is "Humiliating" Rather Than Merely Painful Elsewhere**

The sūrah has a vocabulary for punishment and it uses it selectively. Verse 9 promised *the torment of burning*; verse 19 described garments of fire and scalding water; verse 25 promised a *painful* punishment. Here the adjective is *muhīn*, humiliating.

The variation is not random. Verse 25's punishment was announced for people who had prevented others from the Sacred Mosque — a public crime against a public institution — and it was described in the language of pain. This verse's group is described by what they denied, and the punishment is described by what it does to their standing.

The match is exact. The offence was the attempt to make the signs of God ineffective, which is an attempt to make God's word small. What is imposed in return is smallness.

**The Verse's Pairing With 56**

Read the two verses together and the sūrah's eschatology is stated in its barest form. Sovereignty belongs to God; He judges; one group is in ease and the other in abasement.

The pairing also answers the objection the sūrah raised at verse 17 and deferred. Every religious community claimed that it had the truth and that the others were wrong, and this sūrah refused to adjudicate between them in this world. Verses 56 and 57 are the adjudication, and the criteria stated here are not communal membership: belief and sound deeds on one side, disbelief and active denial of the signs on the other.

**Where the Sūrah Goes From Here**

The next two verses will address the specific case of the emigrants, and the passage will then turn to a series of short statements about God's power and truth. Verse 57 is the last full statement of judgment in the sūrah; everything after it is either particular application or the evidence on which the judgment rests.

It is also, in a sense, the last statement of the sūrah's main argument. The chapter opened with the Hour, argued for the Resurrection, legislated the pilgrimage, authorised defence, and traced the pattern of destroyed communities. Verses 56 and 57 are where all of that arrives: a judgment, executed by the sole authority, dividing people by what they did with what they were given.

What remains after this is not new argument but application. The emigrants, the retaliators, the signs in the natural world, the parable of the fly, and the closing command to strive are all addressed to a reader who already knows how the matter ends.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:58

> As for those who emigrate in the cause of Allah and then are martyred or die, Allah will indeed grant them a good provision. Surely Allah is the Best Provider.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse Addresses a Question Nobody Wanted to Ask**

The immediate context explains the wording. Some of the Emigrants had died in Madīnah of illness rather than in battle, and people were saying — within the community — that dying while fighting for God was the better death.

The verse's answer is in its grammar. *Thumma qutilū aw mātū* — then are killed *or die*. The two outcomes are joined by a conjunction that gives no preference, and both are followed by the same promise with the same verb. Whatever hierarchy existed in the community's talk, the revelation declines to ratify it.

That is a substantive ruling rather than a consolation. The emigrant's act was the leaving; whether the leaving ended in a battle or in a bed is not presented as the variable that matters.

The verbs are also worth attending to. *Qutilū* is passive: they *were killed*. It is not *qātalū*, they fought. The verse names what was done to these people rather than what they did, and it places the passive verb alongside *mātū*, they died — which is likewise something that happens to a person rather than something he does. Both branches of the sentence describe a fate, and the act for which the reward is promised sits outside the pair, in the emigration itself.

**What Emigration Was**

*Hājarū fī sabīli Allāh* — those who emigrated in the way of God. The verb shares a root with *hijrah*, the emigration, and the word carries the sense of cutting a tie and going.

The sūrah has already named this act as a crime committed against the believers: they were expelled from their homes (22:40). Emigration is the other side of that expulsion — the same departure, described from the point of view of the person who chose it rather than the people who imposed it.

Sūrah al-Nisāʾ states the promise attached to it in unambiguous terms: *“Whoever leaves their home, emigrating ˹in the cause of˺ Allah and His Messenger, and then dies, their reward is guaranteed by Allah”* (4:100). The guarantee is tied to the departure and the intention, and death overtaking the emigrant does not interrupt it.

The intention is what makes the act what it is, and the Prophet ﷺ said so in the report that opens al-Bukhārī's collection: actions are reckoned by their intentions, and a person has what he intended — so that whoever emigrates for God and His Messenger has emigrated for God and His Messenger, while whoever emigrates for some worldly gain or for a woman he wishes to marry has emigrated for that (Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907). That is why the verse's phrase *fī sabīli Allāh* is not decorative. Emigration is not defined by the distance travelled but by what the traveller left for.

**"God Will Provide Them a Good Provision"**

*La-yarzuqannahum Allāhu rizqan ḥasanan* — God will provide them a good provision. The verb is emphatic, with the doubled *n* of the strongest form of the future.

The provision is described as *ḥasan*, good or beautiful — a different adjective from the *karīm* provision of verse 50, and the difference deserves attention. *Karīm* described the manner of the giving; *ḥasan* describes the quality of the thing given. Both are promised here, in effect: God gives well, and what He gives is good.

The commentators differed about when this provision arrives. Many took it as the provision of the Garden, owed to the emigrant in the Hereafter. Others took it as a present provision — that the martyr is sustained now, in the interval between death and resurrection, rather than only after it. The second reading has a strong parallel in Sūrah Āl ʿImrān, which uses the same root: *"Never think of those martyred in the cause of Allah as dead. In fact, they are alive with their Lord, well provided for"* (3:169). On that reading the promise of this verse is not deferred compensation but a description of the present state of the dead, and the dead are described as being provided for rather than as waiting.

**"He Is the Best of Providers"**

*Wa-inna Allāha la-huwa khayru al-rāziqīn* — and God is the best of providers.

The comparative is pointed, because human beings provide too. Families feed their members, patrons maintain their clients, states distribute. The verse does not deny that; it ranks God above every provider and thereby relativises the others.

For an emigrant that ranking was the whole issue. Leaving Makkah meant losing the economic protection of one's clan, which was the only social security a seventh-century Arab had. The people this verse addresses had given up the only provider they had ever known, and they are told that the exchange was not a loss.

The argument is generalised elsewhere, and the generalisation is directed at exactly this fear. *"How many are the creatures that cannot secure their provisions! ˹It is˺ Allah ˹Who˺ provides for them and you ˹as well˺"* (29:60). Provision is not distributed according to who holds the means of producing it; if it were, the argument runs, the animals would starve. The same point is turned into a prohibition: *"Do not kill your children for fear of poverty. We provide for them and for you"* (17:31). The emigrant who has walked out of his clan's protection has not walked out of the arrangement described in those two verses.

**Why the Emigrants Are Named Here**

The sūrah has just stated the two destinations at the judgment (22:56–57), and it now applies the reward to the people who had done the hardest thing the community had been asked to do so far.

The application is also an argument. The permission to fight was granted in verse 39 on the ground that these people had been expelled; verses 40 and 41 described what they would do if established. Verse 58 now says what becomes of those who left and did not come back — which is, in a sūrah that has been insisting that God's help is not a matter of visible outcomes, the assurance the community needed most.

The form of the answer is as important as its content. A community that had begun ranking its dead is answered in the vocabulary of sustenance, not of rank: *rizq*, provision, rather than *darajah*, degree. The verse does not say the two deaths are equal in merit. It says both are provided for, which is the answer a grieving community can actually use, and it removes the comparison rather than settling it.

**A Report Behind the Verse, and Its Limits**

Al-Qurṭubī preserves the occasion: two of the Emigrants died in Madīnah, and the talk in the community was that dying in battle was better than dying at home. The verse is reported to have come down in response to that conversation. [UNVERIFIED: I have not been able to check the chain or the grading of this occasion-of-revelation report in this session; it is given here as commentary rather than as established report.]

Whether or not that specific occasion is accepted, the verse's wording settles the question it raises. Al-Rāzī extended the meaning to everyone who leaves home to serve the religion, and the general reading is the safer one, since the wording names the act rather than the circumstance of the death.

**Death Is Not the Variable**

The last thing to draw out is the verse's treatment of the two deaths. It does not say that dying in bed is as meritorious as dying in battle; it does not rank them at all. What it does is place both under the same promise, which removes the question from the community's jurisdiction.

That is consistent with the sūrah's method throughout. Where human beings are inclined to grade one another, the revelation either supplies a different criterion or declines to grade. Here it does the second, and in doing so it protects the living from the conclusion that those who died quietly died less well.

The protection runs in both directions. The person who died in bed is not diminished, and the person who died fighting is not inflated — and the second half matters as much as the first, because a community that glorifies one kind of death makes the other kind unbearable for the people facing it.

The sūrah has been given to correcting exactly this instinct. It refused to grade the poor who ask against the poor who do not (22:36), it refused to grade the emigrant's manner of dying, and it will close by commanding a striving that is defined by its object rather than by its form (22:78).

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:59

> He will certainly admit them into a place they will be pleased with. For Allah is truly All-Knowing, Most Forbearing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**"He Will Admit Them" — the Verb of Grace Returns**

*La-yudkhilannahum mudkhalan yarḍawnahu* — He will surely admit them an entrance they are pleased with.

The verb is the same one the sūrah used in verses 14 and 23 for the believers' entry into the Gardens, and the emphatic form is the strongest available. Entry is caused by God, and the sūrah has never described it any other way. The emigrants' leaving was their act; their arriving is not.

That asymmetry runs through the whole passage. Verse 58 promised provision from the best of providers; verse 59 promises entry from the One who admits. Both halves of the emigrant's future are described as done to him rather than achieved by him, which is consistent with a sūrah that has insisted since verse 16 that guidance belongs to God.

**"An Entrance They Are Pleased With"**

*Mudkhalan yarḍawnahu* — an entering-place that they are content with.

The object of the verb is not a place but an *entering*, a verbal noun. What is described is the manner and the experience of arriving rather than the coordinates of a destination. The sūrah does not say where they go; it says that the going-in will be such that they are pleased with it.

The relative clause is the substance of the promise. Contentment is internal and cannot be conferred by the quality of a place alone — a person can be in a garden and be wretched. What is promised here is the removal of that possibility.

**Why Contentment Is the Right Promise for Emigrants**

The people addressed had left their homes, their property, their clan and, in many cases, their families. What an emigrant loses is not primarily comfort; it is the sense of being where one belongs.

That is why the promise is phrased as it is. A description of palaces and rivers would not have answered the specific deprivation. What is promised is that the arriving will feel right — that the person who spent his life being out of place will be put somewhere he does not want to leave.

Sūrah al-Fajr states the same promise in the direct address to the soul at peace: *“Return to your Lord, well pleased ˹with Him˺ and well pleasing ˹to Him˺. So join My servants, and enter My Paradise”* (89:27–30). The contentment and the entry are the same event.

**"God Is Knowing and Forbearing"**

*Wa-inna Allāha la-ʿalīmun ḥalīm* — and God is Knowing, Forbearing.

The two names are worth taking together, because their combination is what makes the verse work.

*ʿAlīm*, Knowing: He knows what each of these people left, what it cost them, and what they would have done had they stayed. The emigrants' inner histories are not interchangeable, and none of them is lost. That is the attribute that makes a promise of *contentment* credible — a generic reward could be given by a distributor, but a reward each person is pleased with requires knowledge of each person.

*Ḥalīm*, Forbearing: the word describes restraint in the face of provocation, the quality of not hastening. It is the attribute the sūrah has been illustrating since verse 44: communities are given rein and then seized. Forbearance here means that the delay did not cost these people anything. The emigrants who died before the victory are not disadvantaged by having died early.

**"Them" — Who Is Covered**

The pronoun refers back to verse 58's emigrants, and the two verses are a single promise. But the wording of verse 58 — *those who emigrate in the way of God and then are killed or die* — is general enough to cover more than the first generation.

That generality was noticed by the commentators, who extended the promise to anyone who leaves home for the sake of the religion. The extension is sound, because the verse names an act and a consequence rather than a group of named people.

Notice what the verse does *not* require. It does not require that the emigration succeed, that the person arrive anywhere in particular, or that he live to see any result. The promise is attached to the leaving and to the death or killing that follows it, whatever form either takes.

**Forbearance Is the Sūrah's Own Attribute**

*Ḥalīm* deserves one more word, because it is the attribute this sūrah has been illustrating rather than merely naming.

The chapter's entire middle section is an argument about delay: communities given rein before being seized (22:44, 22:48), a punishment whose arrival is measured on a different clock (22:47), a warning delivered by a messenger who is not responsible for the outcome (22:49). Forbearance is the divine name for all of that.

Naming it here, at the close of the promise to the emigrants, is the sūrah's way of saying that the delay cost them nothing. Those who died before the victory are described as entering a place they are pleased with, and the One who admits them is the One who was never in a hurry.

**The Pairing in the Sūrah's Closing Movement**

Verses 58 and 59 form one promise, and the two names at the end of verse 59 are the seal on it. What is promised to people who gave up everything is that the One they gave it up for knows what it was and was never in a hurry about it.

For a community that had waited years for permission to fight, and that had lost people while waiting, that is the relevant assurance. The forbearance is not a delay in the reward; it is the character of the One who gives it.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:60

> That is so. And whoever retaliates in equivalence to the injury they have received, and then are wronged ˹again˺, Allah will certainly help them. Surely Allah is Ever-Pardoning, All-Forgiving.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Seal**

*Dhālika* — that is how it is. The demonstrative appears here for the fourth time in the sūrah, closing the passage on the emigrants just as it closed the passages on the pilgrimage (22:30) and the rites (22:32).

Each time the word does the same work: it declares a matter settled and then turns to what follows from it. Here what has been settled is that God provides for those who emigrate and admits them to a place they are pleased with, and what follows is a ruling about retaliation.

The sūrah uses the pointer in two distinct ways, and the difference is instructive. At 22:30 and 22:32 it stands alone, sealing a block of instruction: what has been said is to be taken as stated. At 22:60 and 22:62 it is followed by *bi-anna*, because: a ground is about to be supplied. The retaliator's permission and the promise of help are therefore not asserted twice in a row. The first is asserted and sealed, and the four verses that follow give the reasons.

**"Whoever Retaliates With the Like of What He Suffered"**

*Wa-man ʿāqaba bi-mithli mā ʿūqiba bihi* — and whoever retaliates with the equivalent of what he was punished with.

The structure of the phrase is a built-in limit. The retaliation is measured against the original injury, and the measurement is the point: the verse authorises a response and fixes its size in the same clause.

Sūrah al-Naḥl states the rule and then the preference in one sentence: *“If you retaliate, then let it be equivalent to what you have suffered. But if you patiently endure, it is certainly best for those who are patient”* (16:126). Sūrah al-Shūrā does the same: *“The reward of an evil deed is its equivalent. But whoever pardons and seeks reconciliation, then their reward is with Allah”* (42:40).

The question of the sacred month — which is where the reported occasion of this verse bites — is settled on the same principle of measure rather than on the calendar. *"˹There will be retaliation in˺ a sacred month for ˹an offence in˺ a sacred month, and all violations will bring about retaliation"* (2:194). The sanctity of the month is real, and so is the right of reply within it; what is prohibited is the excess, not the response.

**Equivalence Is What Separates a Response From Revenge**

The restriction to equivalence is what separates retaliation from revenge, and the distinction is not a matter of degree.

Revenge has no natural stopping point; it escalates, and the escalation is the point, since the gratification is in the excess. Equivalence has a stopping point built into it, and the stopping point is the original injury. A response that matches the injury can end; a response that exceeds it cannot, because the excess becomes a new injury requiring its own answer.

The Qur'an's law of retaliation in cases of homicide rests on exactly this logic, and it is stated with an explicit preference for waiving it: *“But if the offender is pardoned by the victim’s guardian, then bloodmoney should be decided fairly and payment should be made courteously. This is a concession and a mercy from your Lord”* (2:178).

**The Reported Occasion, and How Much Weight It Bears**

Several commentators relate this verse to a specific incident: a party of Muslims met idolaters during Muḥarram, one of the sacred months in which the Arabs themselves held fighting to be forbidden. The idolaters pressed for a fight despite the taboo; the Muslims responded in kind; and afterwards they were troubled by what they had done. [UNVERIFIED: this occasion is reported by al-Qurṭubī, al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī in the material available to me without a chain or grading I could check in this session.]

If the report is sound, the verse's ruling is sharper than it first appears. The Muslims' scruple was about fighting in a sacred month, and the revelation's response was not to rebuke them but to define the limit: responding in proportion is permitted even under those conditions, provided the response does not exceed the injury.

If the report is not sound, the general reading still holds. Al-Ṭabarī took the verse as a general description of the believers who had been persecuted in Makkah and driven to Madīnah, and that reading fits the sūrah's argument seamlessly, since the expulsion has been the sūrah's stated grievance since verse 40.

**"Then Was Oppressed Again"**

*Thumma bughiya ʿalayhi* — then, after that, he was wronged again.

The sequence matters. The person described has already responded proportionately and has then been subjected to further wrong. He has not escalated, and he has not been left alone either.

This is the case the verse addresses, and it is the hard case. A person who has been wronged once and responded in kind has done what the law permits. A person who has then been wronged *again* is in a different position, and the promise of help is attached to him specifically.

The implication is that the help is not promised because the retaliation was clever but because the injury was repeated. The second wrong establishes where the aggression actually lies.

The verb is passive, as it was in verse 58: he *was* transgressed against. Nothing in the clause credits him with an action, and the passive is what makes the promise coherent. He is the object of the second wrong, not its author, and what the verse promises him is help rather than permission — the help is God's to give, and it is not described as a second round of retaliation to be taken by his hand.

**"God Will Help Him"**

*La-yanṣurannahu Allāhu* — God will certainly help him. The emphatic form, with the doubled *n*, is the strongest available promise in Arabic.

The object of the promise is the person who stayed within the limit. That is the connection the verse is making, and it is the same connection the sūrah made in verse 39: the permission to fight was granted to people who had been wronged, and the help was declared to be God's to give rather than a consequence of taking up arms.

A community that has just been authorised to use force is now told that its help comes from God and that the condition attached to it is proportion.

**The Closing Names Are the Difficulty**

*Inna Allāha la-ʿafuwwun ghafūr* — God is certainly Pardoning, Forgiving.

The two names at the end of a verse about retaliation are the verse's most demanding element, and they are not decorative.

*ʿAfuww* is the intensive form of pardoning — the one who effaces, who wipes the offence out as though it had not been written. *Ghafūr* is the one who covers. Both describe what God does with offences against Himself, and the verse places them immediately after authorising a response to offences against a person.

The juxtaposition is the instruction. Retaliation is permitted within a limit, and the character of the One who permits it is pardon. A person who takes the permission and forgets the character has read half the verse.

The character is not left as a description of God alone; it is prescribed to the believers in the same terms. *"Let them pardon and forgive. Do you not love to be forgiven by Allah?"* (24:22). The question is a piece of reasoning rather than a piece of piety: the pardon a person wants for himself is the pardon he is being asked to extend, and the verse on retaliation closes by naming the attribute that makes the two ends meet.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Ethics of Force**

Read together, verses 39, 40, 41 and 60 form the sūrah's complete position on the use of force, and it is worth assembling because the parts are scattered.

Fighting is permitted, to those who were fought, because they were wronged (22:39). The grievance is specified: expulsion for a creed (22:40). Power, if granted, carries four duties, two of them public (22:41). Treachery is forbidden (22:38). Retaliation is permitted in exact measure, never in excess (22:60). And the God who authorises all of it is described as pardoning and forgiving.

Nothing in that set is a licence. Every element is a boundary, and the last one is a character description.

The set also has an outer boundary that the sūrah assumes rather than states, and it is worth stating because the objection is predictable. Force is directed at those who wage it: *"Fight in the cause of Allah ˹only˺ against those who wage war against you, but do not exceed the limits. Surely Allah does not like transgressors"* (2:190). Read with verse 60, the shape of the ethic is a target, a measure and a prohibition on excess — a permission that is hedged on every side, addressed to a community that had been expelled from its homes and is being told to count before it strikes.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:61

> That is because Allah causes the night to merge into the day, and the day into the night. Indeed, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**What "That" Refers To**

*Dhālika bi-anna Allāha yūliju al-layla fī al-nahāri* — that is because God makes the night enter into the day.

The demonstrative points back to the promise of verse 60: God will help the person who has been wronged twice. The ground given for that promise is not military, political or even moral. It is the daily alternation of light and darkness.

The logic is worth stating plainly. A God who can reverse the condition of the world twice a day, without interruption and without effort, is not going to find the reversal of a community's fortunes difficult. The promise is being grounded in a power that is demonstrated on schedule, in front of everyone.

The particle doing the work is *bi-anna*, because that — a causal construction, not an assertion. The sūrah is not adding a second promise to the first; it is giving a reason. That distinguishes this verse from the *dhālika* that sealed the earlier passages, and it means the four verses from here to verse 64 are to be read as one argument with four grounds rather than as four separate declarations.

**The Structure of the Four Causal Verses**

Verses 61 to 64 each begin with the same particle, *anna*, and each supplies a ground for the promise that precedes them. The sequence is worth seeing whole before taking the verses one at a time.

The first ground is the alternation of night and day (v. 61). The second is that God is the Truth and everything invoked besides Him is void (v. 62). The third is the rain that greens the earth (v. 63). The fourth is God's ownership of everything and His freedom from need (v. 64).

Two of the four are about power and two are about what God is in Himself. The pairing is deliberate: a promise grounded only in power would leave a reader wondering about the will to use it, and a promise grounded only in God's nature would leave him wondering about the capacity. The sūrah supplies both, in alternation, the way the night enters the day.

**The Verb: Entering, Not Replacing**

*Yūliju* means to insert, to cause to enter, to make one thing pass into another. It is not the verb for replacing or for following.

The choice is precise, and the parallel verses use the same word: *“Do you not see that Allah causes the night to merge into the day and the day into the night, and has subjected the sun and the moon, each orbiting for an appointed term”* (31:29), and in the fuller statement, *“He wraps the night around the day, and wraps the day around the night”* (39:5).

What is described is a continuous interpenetration rather than a switch. The night does not end and the day begin; each is always entering the other, and the boundary between them is a gradient that is constantly moving. The same verb appears in the verse that describes the provision of life from death, which is the larger point: the God who blends night into day is the God who brings the living out of the dead.

Sūrah Āl ʿImrān makes the connection explicit: *“You cause the night to pass into the day and the day into the night. You bring forth the living from the dead and the dead from the living. And You provide for whoever You will without limit”* (3:27).

**Why This Sign Above All Others**

The Qur'an returns to the alternation of night and day more than to any other natural phenomenon, and the reason is not that it is the most spectacular sign available.

It is the most *available*. Nobody is excluded from it by geography, wealth, literacy or health. A person who has never seen the sea nonetheless experiences this twice daily. The sign cannot be dismissed as local, obscure or reserved for specialists, and it cannot be forgotten, since it structures every human life.

Sūrah Yūnus states the argumentative function in a single line: *“Surely in the alternation of the day and the night, and in all that Allah has created in the heavens and the earth, there are truly signs for those mindful ˹of Him˺”* (10:6). Sūrah al-Furqān adds the purpose: *“And He is the One Who causes the day and the night to alternate, ˹as a sign˺ for whoever desires to be mindful or to be grateful”* (25:62).

**What the Sign Establishes**

The transition from night to day is not a proof of the Resurrection in the way that the reviving field of verse 5 is. It establishes something prior: that the conditions of the world are not fixed.

A person being oppressed is apt to experience his situation as permanent — as though the present arrangement were the nature of things rather than an arrangement. The daily reversal is the standing refutation of that. The world's most reliable structure is one in which darkness is always being replaced by light, and it happens whether or not anyone deserves it.

That is the specific comfort the verse offers. The promise of help in verse 60 is attached to a demonstration that conditions reverse.

The two signs are complementary rather than interchangeable, and the sūrah needs both. Verse 5's field proves that dead matter can be brought back to life, which answers the objection that resurrection is impossible. This verse proves that a settled condition can be inverted without ceasing to be ordered, which answers the different objection that a present situation is unchangeable. An oppressed community is not usually worried about whether the dead can rise; it is worried about whether anything ever turns around.

**"Hearing, Seeing"**

*Wa-anna Allāha samīʿun baṣīr* — and God is Hearing, Seeing.

The two attributes are the ones the Qur'an invokes when it is talking about justice, and their presence here is not generic. The verse's subject is a person who has been wronged, retaliated proportionately, and been wronged again.

Such a person's first need is not power but witness. He needs the injury to have been registered, and he needs it registered accurately — the full extent, the sequence, who started it. *Samīʿ* covers what was said and *baṣīr* covers what was done, and between them nothing in the case is missing from the record.

The pairing is used the same way at the opening of Sūrah al-Mujādilah, in a case that is likewise about a complaint brought by someone with no power to enforce it: *"Indeed, Allah has heard the argument of the woman who pleaded with you ˹O Prophet˺ concerning her husband, and appealed to Allah. Allah has heard your exchange. Surely Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing"* (58:1). The hearing is announced before the ruling is given, because the first thing an unheard party needs is to have been heard. That is what the two names do at the end of this verse: they tell the wronged party that the case is already complete.

**The Verse in the Chain**

Verses 61 to 64 are a chain of four verses each beginning with a causal particle: because God merges night into day (v. 61), because God is the Truth (v. 62), because God sends rain (v. 63), because to Him belongs everything (v. 64).

Each supplies a ground for what was promised before it, and the four together rest the sūrah's argument on the divine attributes rather than on circumstances. The help promised to the wronged is not a forecast about the balance of power. It is a deduction from what God is.

The four grounds also answer four different objections, and that is why there are four of them rather than one. The alternation of night and day answers "the situation cannot change." God's being the Truth answers "the cause is not worth changing it for." The rain that revives dead ground answers "the cause is finished." Ownership of everything answers "there is nothing left to give." None of the four is redundant, and none of them is an argument about the believers' numbers or equipment.

What the chain as a whole does is convert a promise into a conclusion. The help of verse 60 was stated with the strongest emphatic form available; the four verses that follow are the premises from which it is drawn. A reader who accepts them has accepted the conclusion, and a reader who does not is not being asked to trust a prediction — he is being asked to disagree with an argument about the alternation of day and night.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:62

> That is because Allah ˹alone˺ is the Truth and what they invoke besides Him is falsehood, and Allah ˹alone˺ is truly the Most High, All-Great.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Ground, and What It Adds**

*Dhālika bi-anna Allāha huwa al-ḥaqqu* — that is because God, He is the Truth.

The sūrah has said this before, at verse 6, in almost the same words. What is added here is the second half: *wa-anna mā yadʿūna min dūnihi huwa al-bāṭil* — and what they call upon apart from Him is falsehood.

The addition changes the sentence's function. Verse 6 was establishing a premise for the Resurrection: God is real, He gives life, He is capable. Verse 62 is drawing a conclusion about worship, and it does so by putting the true and the false in the same sentence with the same predicate structure. God *is* the Truth; what is invoked beside Him *is* the falsehood. Both are declared with the emphatic pronoun, and the parallelism is the argument.

**What "Bāṭil" Means**

The word translated as falsehood is *bāṭil*, from a root meaning to be void, to come to nothing, to fail.

The sense is not primarily that the idols do not exist — a carved stone exists — but that they are weightless in the way that matters. A thing is *bāṭil* when it cannot deliver what is claimed for it, when it dissolves under examination, when it produces nothing. The Qur'an's charge against the objects of pagan worship has never been that they are hallucinations; it is that they are null.

That is why the sūrah's argument has been functional throughout. Verse 12's test was whether the thing can harm or benefit. Verse 73, near the end of the sūrah, will press the same point with a fly. The criterion never changes, and it never concerns whether the object can be seen.

**The Objection This Answers**

The verse is addressing a specific and reasonable objection: that the idols are real forces with real effects, or, more modestly, that they are conduits to a power that is real.

The response is not to deny the conduit's existence but to deny its validity. What is invoked apart from God is *bāṭil* even when the invocation is sincere, even when the worshipper feels something, even when the cult has been practised for centuries. Sincerity does not confer on an object the capacity it lacks.

Sūrah Luqmān states the entire verse almost identically, which confirms that this is a fixed formula rather than a remark tailored to one audience: *“That is because Allah ˹alone˺ is the Truth and what they invoke besides Him is falsehood, and ˹because˺ Allah ˹alone˺ is the Most High, All-Great”* (31:30).

**Two Predicates, One Shape**

The Arabic sets the two claims in exactly parallel form: *anna Allāha huwa al-ḥaqqu wa-anna mā yadʿūna min dūnihi huwa al-bāṭil*. Each clause has the particle, the subject, the emphatic pronoun, and the predicate. Neither is subordinate to the other.

The parallelism is the argument's whole structure, and it is worth slowing down over why it matters. A sentence that said "God is true, so your idols are false" would be making an inference that a listener could dispute at the level of the inference — why should one follow from the other? A sentence that simply states both, with the same grammar and the same emphasis, is not inferring. It is describing two things at once and letting the reader see that they cannot both be held.

The emphatic pronoun *huwa* in each clause does the rest. God *is* the Truth: not a truth among truths, but that which truly is. What is called upon besides Him *is* the false: not weaker, not lesser, but the thing that comes to nothing.

**"The Most High, the Great"**

*Wa-anna Allāha huwa al-ʿaliyyu al-kabīr* — and God is the Most High, the Great.

The two names are chosen for their fit with what precedes. If what is worshipped besides God is null, the reason is not that it is weak in a competition among powers. It is that the distance between it and God is not a distance at all: God is *al-ʿaliyy*, the High, above comparison, and *al-kabīr*, the Great, beside whom greatness is not a measurable property.

That is why the sūrah does not argue that God is greater than the idols. Greatness of that comparative kind would concede that the idols have some. The names are absolute: high without reference to what is beneath, great without reference to what is smaller.

**Why the Pairing Recurs**

The combination of *al-ʿaliyy* and *al-kabīr* appears together at the close of the Āyat al-Kursī in the same emphatic form, and the recurrence tells us what the pairing is for: it is the standard Qur'anic way of ending an argument about God's uniqueness.

Having established that everything worshipped besides Him is void, the verse closes by naming the two attributes that make the voidness necessary rather than accidental. The idols are null because the One they are set beside is high and great in a way that leaves no room.

**The Verse's Place in the Chain**

This is the second of the four causal verses. The first grounded the promise of help in God's management of night and day; this one grounds it in what God is and what everything else is not.

The movement is instructive. The sūrah is not arguing that the believers will win because their cause is just or their number sufficient. It is arguing that help is available because the power being invoked against them is void and the power being invoked for them is absolute. A community facing an enemy's gods is being told what those gods are.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:63

> Do you not see that Allah sends down rain from the sky, then the earth becomes green? Surely Allah is Most Subtle, All-Aware.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Sign, and a Return to the Opening**

*A-lam tara anna Allāha anzala mina al-samāʾi māʾan fa-tuṣbiḥu al-arḍu mukhḍarratan* — do you not see that God sends down water from the sky, and the earth becomes green?

The sūrah has used this sign before. Verse 5 described the lifeless earth stirring and swelling under rain as the final step in its proof of the Resurrection, and the commentaries noted there that the analogy is the Qur'an's most repeated one.

Its reappearance here, near the end of the sūrah, is a return rather than a repetition. The chapter opened with the argument from the embryo and the field; it closes with the argument from the field again, and the effect is to bracket everything in between. What has happened in the interval — the pilgrimage legislation, the permission to fight, the catalogue of destroyed towns — has all been delivered inside a world whose most ordinary event is a resurrection.

**The Verb: "Becomes"**

*Fa-tuṣbiḥu* — and it *becomes*, in the morning. The verb carries the sense of entering a state, and in this form it is used for what happens at daybreak.

The temporal nuance is exact. Rain falls, and by morning the ground is green. The change is not described as a slow process but as something a person wakes up to. The same verb appears throughout the Qur'an for this: a person goes to sleep in one condition and rises in another.

That is the piece of the analogy the sūrah wants. The Resurrection is described elsewhere as a waking, and the field is the daily rehearsal of it: the earth is dead at night and alive in the morning, and nobody who has seen the second has any difficulty believing it.

**"Green"**

*Mukhḍarratan* — green, verdant. The word is from the same root as *khaḍir*, and it denotes the specific green of new growth rather than the colour of anything else.

The detail is doing theological work. What is being described is not the wetting of soil but the production of life, and the colour is the evidence of it. The argument of verse 5 was that God brings the living out of the dead, and the green is what that looks like from a distance.

**A Fourth "Do You Not See"**

*A-lam tara* — do you not see? The question appears four times in this sūrah's closing movement, and the repetition is part of the teaching method.

The sūrah is not asking whether its audience has functioning eyesight. It has already answered that question in verse 46, where it said that the eyes are not blind but the hearts are. What these four questions ask is whether the seeing has produced anything — whether the rain, the ships, the sky held overhead and the whole created order have been registered as what they are.

The questions are also, in form, rhetorical challenges that expect agreement. Nobody denies that the rain greens the earth. The sūrah takes a fact that no one disputes and asks why the one conclusion that follows from it is not drawn.

**Laṭīf: Subtle, and Reaching Into the Fine Places**

*Inna Allāha laṭīfun khabīr* — God is Subtle, Aware.

*Laṭīf* is one of the hardest divine names to render. It covers subtlety, fineness, gentleness and the quality of reaching where nothing else reaches. Applied to God it means that His action operates at a scale smaller than anything a person can observe, and that His kindness works through channels the recipient does not perceive.

The name is placed here because of what rain does. Water reaches the root of a plant through soil, and the mechanism by which a seed turns water into tissue is not something any observer of the seventh century — or indeed of any century — could see. The green appears; the process that produced it was hidden. That is exactly what *laṭīf* describes: an action whose effects are visible and whose working is not.

**Khabīr: Aware**

The second name is *khabīr*, from the root meaning to be acquainted with the inside of a thing, to know it thoroughly.

Paired with *laṭīf*, it completes the claim. God's action reaches the finest detail, and it reaches it knowingly rather than by mechanism. The rain that finds the root is not a system running on its own; it is an act of a Being who knows where the root is.

For the reader of this sūrah, the consequence is the one the verse is building toward. A God who is *laṭīf* and *khabīr* about a seed in the ground is not going to lose track of a person who was expelled from his home, or of an emigrant who died in a strange city.

**The Sign Is Directed at a Specific Doubt**

The rain sign answers the specific objection the sūrah recorded at verse 5: *if you are in doubt about the Resurrection*. Doubt about the Resurrection is, at bottom, doubt about whether something dead can become alive again.

The field is the standing refutation, and it is a refutation rather than a metaphor. The Qur'an does not say that the Resurrection is *like* the revival of the earth; it says that the same Agent who does the second will do the first, and it uses the identical verb for both. Where the field is concerned, nobody disputes that the dead thing became alive.

That is why the sign is repeated so often across the Qur'an. It is not the most beautiful of the divine signs and it is not the most obviously miraculous. It is the one that answers the hardest question with an event that everyone has seen.

**Why the Rain Sign Carries More Weight Than Any Other**

The Qur'an's preference for this sign over every other is worth accounting for, since the choice is not arbitrary.

The alternatives do not work as well. The stars are distant and their governance is abstract. The mountains are impressive but static. The sea is not available to everyone. Rain is local, repeated, visible in its effects, and tied to the difference between a living year and a famine — which means that every person in the audience has a material stake in it.

A sign that everyone depends on and nobody can produce is the ideal argument. It cannot be dismissed as irrelevant and it cannot be credited to anyone else.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:64

> To Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth. Allah ˹alone˺ is truly the Self-Sufficient, Praiseworthy.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Claim of Ownership**

*Lahu mā fī al-samāwāti wa-mā fī al-arḍ* — to Him belongs whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth.

The sentence has no verb. It is a nominal construction with a fronted predicate, and in Arabic that form gives both exclusivity and permanence: the belonging is His, and it is not a temporary arrangement.

The comprehensiveness of the phrase is the point. *Mā*, whatever, covers everything without exception — not the good things, not the useful things, not the things people are willing to credit to a creator, but everything. The verse is placed after three verses of signs and before two more, and it is the conclusion they were building: the One who does all of this owns all of it.

**What Ownership Excludes**

If everything belongs to God, several things follow that the sūrah has been insisting on since its first verse.

Nothing belongs to the idols, which is the argument of verse 62 restated as a property claim. Nothing belongs to the worshipper in the absolute sense, which is why the sūrah keeps describing wealth as *what We provided you* (22:28, 22:35) and why the sacrificial animal may be used but not sold. And nothing that a person surrenders is lost, since it was never his to begin with — which is the argument behind the promise to the emigrants in verses 58 and 59.

The claim is therefore not a piece of abstract theology. It is the premise of every transaction this sūrah legislates: sacrifice, charity, expenditure, emigration, and the willingness to fight and die for something.

**Al-Ghanī: the Self-Sufficient**

*Wa-inna Allāha la-huwa al-ghaniyyu al-ḥamīd* — and God, He is the Self-Sufficient, the Praised.

*Ghanī* means rich, free of need, self-sufficient. The word is used for a person of independent means, and applied to God it means that nothing outside Him supplies anything to Him.

The consequence for worship is the one the Qur'an draws repeatedly and without softening: God does not benefit from being worshipped and is not diminished by being denied. The sūrah has already said this in the plainest available language at verse 37, where it stated that neither the meat nor the blood of the sacrifice reaches God. Sūrah al-Zumar puts the corollary in a divine saying: were every human being to become a disbeliever, nothing would be lost to Him.

That is why the verse's argument can be made at all. A God who needed worship could be negotiated with, pressured, or bribed. A God who needs nothing is not subject to those levers, and the worship He asks for is not a payment.

**Al-Ḥamīd: the Praised**

*Ḥamīd* is the passive form: the one who is praised, and, more strongly, the one to whom praise is due whether or not anyone offers it.

The pairing with *ghanī* is what makes the name substantive. A being who needed nothing and deserved nothing would be merely remote. A being who needs nothing and is worthy of praise is one toward whom praise is the fitting response — not because it is required of you, not because it adds anything to Him, but because it is the accurate description of the situation.

This is the second of the sūrah's two uses of the root.
