The *path of the Praised* appeared at verse 24 as the destination of the people of the Garden, and the sūrah's closing verse will command the believers to hold fast to God as their Guardian. What is named here as an attribute will be named there as a relationship.

**The Verse Answers the Sūrah's Opening Question**

The sūrah opened with a command to fear God because the Hour is a dreadful thing, and it spent its first seven verses arguing that the Hour will come. What it has not yet explicitly said is why any of that should concern the reader personally.

Verse 64 supplies the missing premise. If everything in the heavens and the earth belongs to God, then every human being is a tenant rather than an owner, and a tenant can be called to account. The Hour is not an intrusion into a life that was the person's own; it is the audit of something he was holding in trust.

That is why the ownership claim appears here, in the closing movement, rather than at the beginning. It is the last of the premises, and it is the one that turns the sūrah's argument from a demonstration into a summons.

**The Verse's Place in the Chain**

This is the fourth and last of the causal verses that ground the promise of help made in verse 60. The sequence has moved from the alternation of night and day, through the truth of God and the nullity of the idols, through the rain, to this: ownership and independence.

The order is ascending, and it ends where a theodicy has to end. The reason help is available to the wronged is not that the universe is arranged to reward the just; it is that the universe belongs to Someone who lacks nothing, who therefore cannot be corrupted, and who is therefore free to act without being moved by anything outside Himself.

**What the Verse Asks of the Reader**

A verse like this is easily assented to and rarely believed. Everyone concedes that God owns everything; the sūrah's own opponents did not deny that a high god existed.

What the verse requires is the specific application: that what you have is not yours, that what you lose was never withheld unjustly, and that what you give was never going to be lost. The sūrah has been moving toward that application since the emigrants of verse 58, and it will close by asking the reader to hold fast to the Owner rather than to what is owned.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:65

> Do you not see that Allah has subjected to you whatever is in the earth as well as the ships ˹that˺ sail through the sea by His command? He keeps the sky from falling down on the earth except by His permission. Surely Allah is Ever Gracious and Most Merciful to humanity.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fifth "Do You Not See"**

*A-lam tara anna Allāha sakhkhara lakum mā fī al-arḍi* — do you not see that God has subjected to you whatever is on the earth?

The question is the same one the sūrah has been asking, and the pattern of these questions is worth tracking. They come at the end of the sūrah rather than the beginning, after the argument from the embryo and the field has already been made and accepted or refused. The closing movement is a rapid accumulation of signs, and the effect is of a case being summarised rather than opened.

**What "Subjected" Means**

*Sakhkhara* means to make something subservient, to put it at someone's disposal. The sūrah has used the word before, in verse 36, for the sacrificial camels, and the meaning is the same: a thing stronger than the person is made usable by him.

The word's asymmetry is what makes it a sign rather than a fact about human cleverness. The earth and everything in it is not weaker than a human being; the animals are stronger, the soils and waters are indifferent to him, and the whole arrangement could have been otherwise. That it is arranged for him is the claim, and the sūrah repeats it elsewhere in the same vocabulary: *“Have you not seen that Allah has subjected for you whatever is in the heavens and whatever is on the earth, and has lavished His favours upon you, both seen and unseen?”* (31:20).

**The Ships**

*Wa-l-fulka tajrī fī al-baḥri bi-amrih* — and the ships run upon the sea by His command.

Ships are the Qur'an's second most frequent sign after rain, and they are named here alongside the subjection of the earth because they are the exception that proves the rule. The land was subjected by being made habitable; the sea was not. It remains uninhabitable, and human beings cross it only by an arrangement they did not establish.

The phrase *bi-amrih*, by His command, is the operative one. A ship floats by physics and sails by wind, and the Qur'an attributes the whole operation to a command — which is not a denial of the mechanism but a claim about whose mechanism it is.

Sūrah al-Jāthiyah states it as a purpose: *“Allah is the One Who has subjected the sea for you so that ships may sail upon it by His command, and that you may seek His bounty”* (45:12). Sūrah Ibrāhīm pairs it with the rivers: *“He has subjected the ships for your service, sailing through the sea by His command, and has subjected the rivers for you”* (14:32).

**"He Holds the Sky From Falling"**

*Wa-yumsiku al-samāʾa an taqaʿa ʿalā al-arḍi illā bi-idhnih* — and He holds the sky back from falling on the earth except by His permission.

This is the most remarkable clause in the sequence, and it is remarkable because of what it describes: nothing happening.

Every other sign in the closing movement is an event — rain falling, a ship moving, the earth greening. This one is a restraint. The sky is held, and the holding is the thing being pointed at. Al-Rāzī took it as the divine maintenance of the natural order that makes seafaring possible at all; others understood *samāʾ* as a metonym for what comes down from it, the rain.

Both readings converge on the same claim: the stability of the world is an act rather than a given. A person standing on the ground is being kept from a catastrophe that would occur by default if it were not prevented — which is precisely the structure of the sūrah's whole argument about delay. Communities are held, the Hour is held, and the sky is held, and in every case the holding is an act of God rather than a property of matter.

**The Exception**

*Illā bi-idhnih* — except by His permission. The clause keeps the catastrophe available.

That is what stops the sign from being sentimental. The sky does not fail to fall because falling is impossible; it fails to fall because it is permitted not to. The permission is renewed, and the sūrah's audience is being asked to consider what the arrangement would look like if it were withdrawn.

The same structure appears in the sūrah's account of the destroyed towns: the normal state of a settlement is that it stands, and its standing was not a property of the town.

**The Subjection Is Not a Licence**

A reader should be careful here, because *sakhkhara* — made subservient — is a word that has been used to justify a great deal.

The sūrah's own use of the verb confines it. Verse 36 used it for the sacrificial animals and immediately followed it with the command to give the meat to the poor and the destitute. Verse 37 followed it by saying that none of the animal reaches God. The subjection in this sūrah is always accompanied by an obligation toward the people who have less.

The same structure is visible here. The earth is subjected, the ships are made to run, the sky is held up — and the verse closes by describing God as kind and merciful to humanity. The subjection is an act of kindness rather than a grant of dominion, and a person who uses it as the latter has missed the attribute the verse ends on.

**"Kind and Merciful to Humanity"**

*Inna Allāha bi-l-nāsi la-raʾūfun raḥīm* — God is, to humanity, Kind and Merciful.

The object of the mercy is specified: *bi-l-nāsi*, to people as such, not to the believers and not to a particular community. That specification matters in a sūrah that has spent much of its length describing what happens to those who reject.

The two names are close but not identical. *Raḥīm* is the one who acts with mercy; *raʾūf* is the intensive form, the one whose kindness is directed at the specific condition of the person receiving it. Placed at the end of a verse about ships, rain and a held-up sky, the pair describes a God whose general provision is also personal.

The verse is therefore not only an argument from nature. It is the answer to the question that the held sky raises: what is holding it up, and for whom.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:66

> And He is the One Who gave you life, then will cause you to die, and then will bring you back to life. ˹But˺ surely humankind is ever ungrateful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Verbs, and the Sūrah's Whole Argument in One Line**

*Wa-huwa alladhī aḥyākum thumma yumītukum thumma yuḥyīkum* — and He is the One who gave you life, then will cause you to die, then will give you life again.

Three verbs, in sequence, with the same subject. The sūrah opened with the Resurrection as something to be argued for and ended with it as something to be asserted; here, near the close, it is stated as a bare sequence of acts by a single Agent.

The compression is the point. The argument of verses 5 to 7 took three verses to establish what this verse states in eleven Arabic words. A reader who has followed the sūrah from its first line is now being given the conclusion without the scaffolding, which is how a conclusion is supposed to arrive.

**The Sequence Matches the Sūrah's Own Structure**

The three stages — life, death, life — are the sūrah's architecture as much as its doctrine.

The sūrah began by addressing people who are alive and in doubt about what follows (22:5). It described the death of the earth and its revival under rain. It described communities that were given life, then taken. It will close by commanding a striving from people who will be brought back. Every movement of the chapter has been inside this sequence.

That is why the verse can say *thumma* twice without explanation. The intervals are the whole subject of the sūrah; the three verbs are the shape of the timeline.

**"Then He Will Give You Life" Is the Difficult One**

The first two verbs are uncontested. Everyone grants that he was given life and that he will die.

The third is the one the sūrah has spent its length on, and what the verse does with it deserves attention: it states it in exactly the same form as the other two, with no additional emphasis, no oath and no qualifier. The Resurrection is presented as an event of the same order as birth and death — which is precisely the claim. A person who accepts the first two and denies the third is not being asked to accept a stranger event; he is being asked to accept the same Agent doing the same kind of thing a third time.

**"Man Is Ungrateful"**

*Inna al-insāna la-kafūr* — truly the human being is ungrateful.

The word is *kafūr*, the intensive form of *kafara*: to cover over, to deny, to be ungrateful. It is the same root that gives *kāfir*, the disbeliever, and the Qur'an uses the ambiguity deliberately. Covering over a benefit and covering over the truth are the same act in different registers.

The subject is *al-insān*, the human being as such, not a particular community. The verse is not naming the Quraysh; it is naming the species, and the sūrah has already given the evidence — the man on the brink who was content with the gift and forgot the giver (22:11), the man who wanted the punishment hurried (22:47), and the people who saw the ruins and drew no conclusion (22:46).

**Why the Charge Follows the Sequence**

The placement is exact. The verse has just listed three acts, two of which are universally acknowledged and one of which is disputed, and then it says that the human being denies.

What is denied is not only the third act. The charge is that a person can receive life — which he did not produce, did not request and cannot sustain for a moment by himself — and treat it as though it were his own. Gratitude in this verse is the recognition of having been given; its absence is the assumption of ownership.

That is why the word *kafūr* is the right one. The ungrateful person and the disbelieving person are doing the same thing: treating a gift as a possession and the giver as unnecessary.

**The Verse Is Addressed to Believers**

One more feature of the placement deserves attention. This verse is not addressed to the sūrah's opponents. It sits in the closing movement, in a passage of instruction and signs, and its subject is *al-insān* — the human being.

The believers are therefore included in the charge. They were given life, they will die, they will be given life again, and they are members of a species that forgets its giver by default.

That inclusion is what makes the sūrah's closing practical rather than merely declarative. If forgetfulness were the failing of the opponents only, the remedy would be argument. Because it is the failing of the species, the remedy has to be repeated: prayer, remembrance, a pilgrimage performed every year, a scripture recited daily. The apparatus of the religion is the answer to the condition this verse names.

**The Verse's Honesty About the Species**

There is no exception stated here, and the sūrah does not soften the claim. *Al-insān* is *kafūr*.

The sūrah has been merciless about this all the way through — the man on the brink, the disputants, the idolaters, the doubters who will not stop doubting. And yet the verse sits in a passage addressed to believers, in a sūrah that will close by telling them to hold fast to God.

The purpose of the statement is diagnostic rather than condemnatory. A species that forgets its giver by default needs reminders, which is what a scripture is, and needsrites, which is what the pilgrimage is, and needs a warning, which is what the Hour is. The whole apparatus of this sūrah is the answer to the condition named in this verse.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:67

> For every community We appointed a code of life to follow. So do not let them dispute with you ˹O Prophet˺ in this matter. And invite ˹all˺ to your Lord, for you are truly on the Right Guidance.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Statement Is the Same One the Sūrah Made in Verse 34**

*Li-kulli ummatin jaʿalnā mansakan hum nāsikūhu* — for every community We appointed a rite which they are to perform.

The sūrah has said this before, at verse 34, in almost the same words. There the point was that sacrifice is universal and that the name pronounced over it is what distinguishes a rite from a slaughter. Here the point is procedural: because the rites differ by community, arguing about them is not the task.

The word *mansak* carries the same range it carried there — a ritual act, a body of ritual, or the place where the ritual is performed. Whichever sense is taken, the claim is that these were appointed, and appointed by God rather than chosen by the communities.

The repetition is a frame rather than a slip. Verse 34 stands at the head of the sūrah's longest legislative passage, and everything from there to verse 66 — the sacrificial animals, the pilgrimage, the permission to fight, the ruined towns, the emigrants — sits inside it. Verse 67 takes the same sentence up again at the far side of that material, and this time draws the consequence that was left implicit the first time: since the rites were appointed and not chosen, they are not settled by disputing about them.

**The Inference Drawn From the Plurality**

The verse's logic runs in a direction that readers often miss. The plurality of rites is not presented as a problem to be resolved. It is presented as a fact that settles a question: if God appointed a different rite for each community, then uniformity was not the goal, and disputing about which rite is correct is a category error.

That is the meaning of *fa-lā yunāziʿunnaka fī al-amr* — so let them not dispute with you concerning the matter. The prohibition is not a refusal to give reasons; it is a refusal to accept the terms of the argument. What is being disputed was appointed, and an appointed difference is not a contradiction to be adjudicated by debate.

Sūrah al-Māʾidah states the same principle about law and way of life: *“To each of you We have ordained a code of law and a way of life. If Allah had willed, He would have made you one community, but His Will is to test you”* (5:48). The difference is described there as the test rather than as the problem.

**What the Prophet Is Told to Do Instead**

*Wa-dʿu ilā rabbika* — and call to your Lord.

The instruction replaces the dispute with the invitation, and the difference between the two is the whole of the verse.

A dispute takes place on the ground the disputant has chosen: which rite, which community, which practice is correct. An invitation takes place on ground the caller chooses: the Lord. The sūrah's instruction is to decline the first and keep to the second, which is also the strategy of verse 49, where the Prophet was told to announce his office rather than to produce the punishment.

This is a recurring pattern in the Qur'an's guidance to its messenger. Where the opponents want a debate about particulars, the messenger is told to return to the single claim — and to trust that the particulars follow from it if the claim is accepted.

The standing formulation of the same instruction makes clear that the verse is not banning argument as such. *"Invite ˹all˺ to the Way of your Lord with wisdom and kind advice, and only debate with them in the best manner"* (16:125). Debate conducted well is part of the invitation. What verse 67 withdraws from is a debate conducted on the opponent's terms, about a matter that was appointed rather than argued.

**"You Are Indeed on Straight Guidance"**

*Innaka la-ʿalā hudan mustaqīm* — you are certainly upon a guidance that is straight.

The assurance is absolute, and it is given before the arguments have landed. That is its function: the Prophet is told where he stands so that the disputants' refusal does not become a measure of his position.

The phrase *hudā mustaqīm* is the same pairing the sūrah used at verse 54 for the path to which God guides the believers. The echo is deliberate — the messenger's guidance and the believers' guidance are the same one, and the route is described as straight rather than as easy.

The same assurance is given to him elsewhere in nearly the same words, and the parallel shows what is being protected: *"You ˹O Prophet˺ are truly one of the messengers, upon the Straight Path"* (36:3–4). In both places the statement precedes the outcome. The Prophet is not told that the disputants will be convinced; he is told that his position does not depend on whether they are.

**The Dispute the Sūrah Has in View**

The commentators noted two readings of who is being told not to dispute. One takes it as addressed to the Prophet: do not let them draw you into argument. The other takes it as addressed through him to the opponents: they should stop disputing and submit.

The first is the more natural reading of the grammar, since the verb is a prohibition addressed in the second person with *them* as the object. It is also the reading that fits the sūrah, which has repeatedly told the Prophet what his job is and what it is not (22:49).

The second reading is not to be discarded, however, because it preserves the sense that the opponents are being given a reason as well as an instruction. On that reading the verse tells them what the first reading tells the Prophet: the rite was appointed, so the argument is not available.

Either way, the verse is not a general prohibition of discussion. The Qur'an prescribes argument elsewhere and prescribes its manner: *"Do not argue with the People of the Book unless gracefully, except with those of them who act wrongfully"* (29:46). What is withdrawn in this verse is narrower — a dispute about an appointed difference, with people the sūrah has already described as disputing without knowledge.

**What "Appointed" Excludes**

The verb is *jaʿalnā*, We appointed — the same verb used at verse 34, and the same one used at verse 36 for making the sacrificial camels into emblems.

The subject of the verb is God, and that is the part the argument rests on. If the rites had been appointed by the communities, they would be open to comparison, criticism and revision by anyone with an argument. Appointed by God, they are not comparable in the way the disputants want them to be.

This is the same principle the sūrah applied at verse 34, when it moved straight from the plurality of rites to the oneness of God. The diversity is real and it is ordained; what is not ordained is ranking them by debate.

The verse therefore closes the door that the sūrah opened at verse 17, when it listed six communities and declined to adjudicate between them. Nothing has changed about that deferral. What has been added is the instruction about how to live inside it.

**The Verse's Place in the Sūrah's Closing**

Verses 67 to 69 form a small unit on how to conduct disagreement, and they come at a point in the sūrah where the argument has been made, the evidence given and the judgment announced. What remains is instruction for the interval.

That is the right place for it. A community that has been told it is right, and told that the adjudication is deferred to God (22:17, 22:56), needs to know what to do with people who disagree — and the answer the sūrah gives is not argument.

The three verses also close the question that the sūrah has held open since its middle. Verse 69 will state that God will judge between them on the Day of Resurrection concerning what they differed about, which is the same deferral that verse 17 made for the six communities and verse 56 made for the judgment generally. Between the deferral and the day, the instruction is to invite and not to dispute — a rule for the interval rather than a solution to the disagreement.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:68

> But if they argue with you, then say, “Allah knows best what you do.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Concession the Verse Builds In**

*Wa-in jādilūka* — and if they dispute with you.

The conditional is doing something the previous verse did not. Verse 67 instructed the Prophet not to let them draw him into argument over the rites. Verse 68 assumes they will do it anyway.

That is not an inconsistency; it is the Qur'an's usual way of legislating for the world as it is. The instruction in verse 67 was about where the Prophet's effort belongs. The instruction here is about what to do when the instruction is ignored, and the sūrah supplies it in advance because the outcome is not in doubt.

The verb *jādala* is the same one used in verses 3 and 8 for the disputant who argues without knowledge, and the echo is intentional. The people described in verse 68 are the same type the sūrah described at its opening, and by this point in the chapter the reader has been given a full account of them.

**What to Say, and Why That**

*Fa-qul Allāhu aʿlamu bi-mā taʿmalūn* — then say: God knows best what you do.

The sentence is a formula of disengagement, and it is worth taking apart, because a careless reading hears it as a shrug.

It is not a shrug, and it does three things at once. It declines the argument, since it offers no counter-claim for the opponent to attack. It relocates the question to a knowing that the opponent cannot dispute, since nobody claims that God is ignorant of what people do. And it warns, because the knowledge being invoked is the knowledge that will be acted on.

The last of those three is what makes the sentence effective rather than evasive. "God knows best what you are doing" is not a way of saying the matter is settled in the speaker's favour. It is a way of saying the matter is recorded.

**The Knowledge Invoked Is the Sūrah's Own Theme**

The sūrah has been insistent that nothing escapes the record. Verse 10 described the book a person will be handed and told to read. Verse 70, two verses from here, will say that everything in the heavens and the earth is in a clear record. Verse 76 will say that God knows what is before them and what is behind them.

Verse 68 is the first of those statements turned into a reply. What makes the formula work is that it is not rhetorical: on the sūrah's own account, there is a record, it is complete, and it will be produced.

**Disengagement Is Not Defeat**

The Qur'an's instructions for handling disputes of this kind are consistent, and they are worth assembling because they are frequently misread as weakness.

The pattern is always the same: state the claim, give whatever evidence the audience can check, and then, if the argument continues, decline it and refer the matter to God. The Prophet is told at verse 49 to announce his office rather than to produce the punishment; at verse 67 to invite rather than to dispute; and here to say one sentence and stop.

What this protects is not the messenger's dignity but the message's time. A dispute of this kind has no natural end — the sūrah has already described people who dispute without knowledge and who will not stop (22:55) — and every hour spent in it is an hour not spent inviting.

**Restraint Where a Threat Would Be Easy**

One further feature deserves notice: the reply contains no threat, no insult and no prediction.

Compare it with what the sūrah says elsewhere about these same people — garments of fire, boiling water, iron rods. In a face-to-face dispute, the Prophet is given none of that to say. He is given a sentence about knowledge and left there.

The restraint is the instruction's most imitable part. What the sūrah permits itself in describing the outcome is not what it puts into the messenger's mouth during the argument.

**The Verse's View of Argument Itself**

There is an assumption embedded in this instruction that deserves to be made explicit, because it shapes everything the sūrah says about disagreement.

The assumption is that some arguments cannot be won, and that this is a fact about the other party rather than about the case. The sūrah has described people who dispute without knowledge (22:8), whose hearts are sick (22:53), whose hearts are hard (22:53), and who will not cease to be in doubt until the Hour arrives (22:55). Against that description, more argument is not merely futile; it is a category error.

What can be done instead is exactly what the verse prescribes: state the position, decline the exchange, and name the fact that the matter is recorded. That is not a retreat from the disagreement but a recognition of what kind of thing it is.

**The Formula's Second Life**

The sentence the Prophet is given — *God knows best what you do* — is not unique to this verse, and its other occurrences tell us how the Qur'an intends it to be heard.

It appears in the sūrahs both as a reply to opponents and as a statement about the interior of a community: a reminder that motives are not visible to the people who judge them and are entirely visible to the One who will. In both uses the function is the same — to close a line of investigation that cannot produce a result here and to place it where it can.

That is what makes the sentence more than a conversational exit. It transfers the case, and in doing so it removes the speaker's need to win.

**Where This Leaves the Believer**

Read with verses 67 and 69, this verse completes a three-part rule for disagreement: invite, do not dispute, and if disputed, say one sentence and refer the matter upward.

The rule is demanding in an unobvious way. It requires a person to forgo the satisfaction of winning, to accept being thought defeated, and to leave the verdict with someone else. The sūrah offers no guarantee that the argument will be won in this life; it guarantees only that it will be settled in the next.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:69

> Allah will judge between you ˹all˺ on Judgment Day regarding your differences.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Third Deferral in This Sūrah**

*Allāhu yaḥkumu baynakum yawma al-qiyāmati fī mā kuntum fīhi takhtalifūn* — God will judge between you on the Day of Resurrection concerning that in which you used to differ.

This is the third time the sūrah has deferred an adjudication to the Day of Resurrection, and the recurrence is the sūrah's method rather than its habit.

Verse 17 deferred the question of which religious community is right. Verses 56 and 57 executed that judgment in the eschatological scene, where the criteria turned out not to be communal membership. Verse 69 brings the deferral back down into the present and hands it to the Prophet as a rule of conduct.

**"Concerning That in Which You Differ"**

The scope of the phrase is wider than the context might suggest. The immediate disagreement is about the rites, since verse 67 has just said that every community was given its own.

But *mā kuntum fīhi takhtalifūn* — that in which you used to differ — is a relative clause with no restriction, and the sūrahs that use the same formula apply it to religious disputes generally: *“Say, ‘Allah knows best what you do.’ Allah will judge between you ˹all˺ on Judgment Day regarding your differences”* is the shape of the instruction, and it is used for disagreements between communities rather than only within them.

Sūrah Āl ʿImrān states the same deferral for the disputes of the People of the Book: *“Then to Me you will ˹all˺ return, and I will settle all your disputes”* (3:55). Sūrah al-Māʾidah does the same for the plurality of laws (5:48).

**The Deferral Has a Date**

*Yawma al-qiyāmati* — on the Day of Resurrection. The deferral is not open-ended, and the specificity of the date is what distinguishes it from indifference.

A dispute referred to an indefinite future has been shelved. A dispute referred to a named day that the sūrah has spent its length establishing — the Hour that is coming (22:7), the day on which sovereignty is God's alone (22:56) — has been scheduled.

That difference is everything. The sūrah is not telling the Prophet to stop caring which community is right; the sūrah's own verses are entirely clear about what belief and sound action lead to. It is telling him that the adjudication belongs to a specific day and a specific Judge, and that living in the interval meansinviting rather than litigating.

**Why Deferral Is Not Indifference**

The obvious objection to a verse like this is that it sounds like a way of not answering. The reply is that the answer is given, and it is given in the form of a date.

A deferred judgment is not a declined one. What is being said is that the adjudication will happen, that it will be executed by the only party qualified to execute it, and that the interval is not a period of uncertainty about the outcome but a period in which the outcome is simply not yet delivered.

That distinction matters practically. A person who believes the dispute will never be settled behaves in one way; a person who believes it will be settled on a known kind of day, by a known kind of Judge, behaves in another.

**What the Deferral Does for the Community**

The social consequence is the one the sūrah is after, and it is larger than it looks.

A community that has been told it is right, and told that the adjudication belongs to God, is released from the obligation to win every argument in the meantime. That release is what makes it possible to live alongside people who disagree without either silencing them or conceding to them. The alternative — a community required to settle every dispute here — has only two instruments available, and both are force.

The sūrah has just legislated the limits of force (22:39–41, 22:60). This verse supplies the other half of that legislation: the disputes that cannot be settled by force are not abandoned, they are reserved.

**The Verse's Connection to Verse 17**

The verb is the same one used at verse 17: *yaḥkumu bayna*, He judges between. There the parties were named — believers, Jews, Sabians, Christians, Magians and associators — and the sūrah declined to rank them.

Verse 69 does not name the parties; it says *between you*. The shift from the third person to the second is what turns a doctrine into an instruction. The Prophet is being told that he is a party to the disagreement and that his standing in it is not the matter.

That is the same thing verse 67 told him when it instructed him to invite rather than to dispute, and verse 68 told him when it gave him one sentence to say. The three verses are a single posture: state the claim, decline the argument, reserve the verdict.

**The Sūrah's Closing Posture**

Placed where it is — after the signs, after the judgment scene, near the end of the chapter — this verse is the sūrah's final word on how to disagree.

Notice what it does not say. It does not say that the disagreements are unreal, or that all parties are equally correct, or that the differences do not matter. It says that the differences will be settled by a Judge who knows what each party did with what it was given — which is the sūrah's argument from its first verse to its last.


It is, finally, a verse about scale. The disagreements that consume a community — which rite, which community, which practice — are placed beside a day on which sovereignty belongs to God alone, and the comparison is left to do its work. What looked like the whole argument turns out to be one item on a list that a single Judge will settle.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:70

> Do you not know that Allah ˹fully˺ knows whatever is in the heavens and the earth? Surely it is all ˹written˺ in a Record. That is certainly easy for Allah.
**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question Changes From Seeing to Knowing**

*A-lam taʿlam anna Allāha yaʿlamu mā fī al-samāʾi wa-mā fī al-arḍ* — do you not know that God knows whatever is in the heaven and whatever is on the earth?

The sūrah has asked *a-lam tara*, do you not see, four times in the preceding verses. Here the verb changes to *a-lam taʿlam*, do you not know.

The shift is deliberate, and it marks a movement in the argument. Seeing was the appropriate faculty for the signs — rain, ships, the held sky. Knowing is the appropriate response to a claim that cannot be observed: that everything is in a record. Nobody sees the record. The verse asks for assent to something that has to be taken on the strength of the One who says it.

**The Singular "Heaven"**

The Arabic here has *al-samāʾ* in the singular rather than the plural *al-samāwāt* used in verse 64. This is the only place in the Qur'an where the singular form appears in a statement of this kind, and the variation is worth noting precisely because it is unusual.

The simplest explanation is the one the commentators give: *samāʾ* in the singular can denote the sky above as a single thing rather than the layered heavens. In a verse about a record that omits nothing, the singular is apt — the whole expanse over a person's head, taken as one, is included.

The plural is what the Qur'an uses when it is counting or when it is describing structure: *"So He formed the heaven into seven heavens in two Days, assigning to each its mandate"* (41:12). What is at issue in this verse is not structure but coverage. A hearer who is told that the seven heavens are in the record might wonder about what lies between them; a hearer who is told that the heaven is in the record, taken as one undivided expanse, is given no gap to insert anything into. The singular is the more exhaustive word in a sentence about exhaustive knowledge.

**"Indeed That Is in a Book"**

*Inna dhālika fī kitāb* — that is in a Book. The demonstrative points back to *whatever is in the heavens and the earth*, which is to say: all of it, without exception, is written down.

The claim is the one the sūrah has been building toward since verse 10, where the record was described as something a person will be handed and told to read. It will be restated two verses from the end, where God is said to know what is before people and what is behind them.

The Qur'an's insistence on the record is a claim about the kind of knowledge God has. It is not a general awareness or an intuition; it is an entry. Sūrah Luqmān makes the scale explicit: *“˹Even˺ if a deed were the weight of a mustard seed—hidden in a rock or in the heavens or the earth—Allah will bring it forth”* (31:16). The same claim is made elsewhere in the same vocabulary: *“He knows whatever is in the heavens and the earth”* (3:29) and *“He knows whatever is in the heavens and on the earth”* (5:97).

**Why a Record Rather Than a Memory**

The metaphor carries a specific assertion: the record is external, fixed and legible.

That matters for the judgment the sūrah has been describing. A verdict based on recollection could in principle be disputed on the ground of error. A verdict based on a written entry that the accused is invited to read cannot — which is why the Qur'an's judgment scenes show the record being produced rather than the charges being announced (17:14; 18:49; 69:19).

For the reader of this sūrah, the consequence is the one verse 68 already put to use: "God knows best what you do" is not a rhetorical flourish. There is a document.

Sūrah al-Kahf describes what happens when the document is opened, and the reaction is not a dispute about facts: *"And the record ˹of deeds˺ will be laid ˹open˺, and you will see the wicked in fear of what is ˹written˺ in it. They will cry, 'Woe to us! What kind of record is this that does not leave any sin, small or large, unlisted?'"* (18:49). Sūrah al-Qamar gives the same property in two lines: *"Everything they have done is ˹listed˺ in ˹their˺ records. Every matter, small and large, is written ˹precisely˺"* (54:52–53). Completeness is the attribute that makes a record a record rather than a summary, and it is the attribute this verse is claiming.

**"That Is Easy for God"**

*Inna dhālika ʿalā Allāhi yasīr* — that is easy for God.

The sentence is standard in the Qur'an after a statement of comprehensive knowledge or power, and it is easily skimmed. It should not be. The claim is not that the record is a small task; it is that the category of difficulty does not apply.

The difference matters. If recording everything were difficult for God, it would be impressive; if it is easy, it is simply what He is. The verse is making a statement about the divine nature rather than about the size of the archive.

The same formula is attached elsewhere to the act that readers find hardest to accept, and the comparison is what gives the word its force: *"And He is the One Who originates the creation then will resurrect it—which is even easier for Him"* (30:27). The comparative *even easier* is not measuring two labours against each other; it is pointing out that the second act follows from the first by the same power. Ease here is the absence of strain in the agent, not the absence of magnitude in the act.

**The Record and the Sūrah's Own Argument**

The claim of a comprehensive record is not an isolated doctrine here; it is the mechanism that makes the sūrah's whole eschatology work.

Verse 10 put a sentence in the mouths of the keepers: *this is for what your hands sent ahead*. For that sentence to mean anything, the shipments must have been logged. Verse 56's judgment requires a procedure, and verse 57's distinction between the two groups requires that each person's case be complete.

Verse 70 is the guarantee that the procedure exists and that nothing is missing from it. Without it, the sūrah's threats would be rhetoric and its promises would be hopes. With it, both are verdicts waiting for a date.

The record is also what makes the sūrah's repeated deferrals coherent. God is said to judge between the communities on the Day of Resurrection (22:17), sovereignty on that day is His (22:56), and the Prophet is told that God will judge the disputed matter (22:69). Every one of those is a postponement, and a postponement is only just if nothing is lost in the interval. The Book is the answer to that worry: the deferral costs the wronged party nothing, because the case does not decay while it waits.

**What the Record Does Not Imply**

The verse is also, in the way the Qur'an handles this doctrine generally, not deterministic in the sense readers often assume.

A record of what people do is not a script of what they will do. The Qur'an holds both without strain: everything is written, and people are responsible for what they do — and the sūrah's own verse 11, which describes a man who chooses to flip over onto his face, is written in the language of choice throughout. The record is the consequence of the act being knowable, not the cause of the act being done.

That distinction matters for how the doctrine is received. A person who believes his entry is already fixed has no reason to act; a person who believes his acts are being written has every reason to.

**What the Verse Requires**

Three things are asserted in one line: comprehensive knowledge, complete inscription, and the ease of both. Together they establish the premise the sūrah has been operating on since its opening — that a judgment is possible at all.

A court needs a record. Without one, the sūrah's eschatology would be a threat without a procedure, and the promise of verse 56 would be a hope rather than a verdict. Verse 70 is the guarantee that the procedure exists.

The Qur'an also describes how the entries get made, and the description turns the doctrine into something a person lives inside rather than something he waits for: *"while you are certainly observed by vigilant, honourable angels, recording ˹everything˺. They know whatever you do"* (82:10–12). The writing is contemporary with the act, not compiled at the end of it.

That is what the verse finally asks of its reader. It does not ask him to imagine an enormous archive at the end of time; it asks him to accept that his day is being written as he lives it, which is a claim about the present rather than about the Hereafter. The sūrah will close in eight verses with a command to strive, and this is the premise that gives the striving weight: what is done is not lost, because it is being kept.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:71

> Yet they worship besides Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and of which they have no knowledge. The wrongdoers will have no helper.

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Grounds, and Neither Is Present**

*Wa-yaʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi mā lam yunazzil bihi sulṭānan wa-mā laysa lahum bihi ʿilm* — and they worship, apart from God, that for which He has sent down no authority and of which they have no knowledge.

The verse names two things the worshippers lack, and the two are exactly the pair the sūrah named at verse 8 for the disputant. There the three absences were knowledge, guidance and an illuminating scripture. Here the two are authority and knowledge. The argument has not changed; it has been tightened.

The object of worship is described as *mā*, "that which" — the pronoun used for what is not a person. The grammar itself is part of the case: they are worshipping a *thing*, and the things in question have had no warrant sent down for them.

The two absences are also cumulative rather than alternative. The first is external: nothing has come down from God authorising this. The second is internal: the worshippers do not know what they are dealing with. A person might admit the first and still claim the second, which is why the verse names both — inherited practice produces confidence without warrant, and confidence can feel like knowledge. The relative clause is indefinite in both halves, so the description is not a list of particular idols but a rule: whatever lacks the warrant and the knowledge is what they are worshipping.

**What "Sulṭān" Means Here**

*Sulṭān* is the word for authority, warrant, proof, and the power by which one thing prevails over another. Its range is wide and all of it is in play.

In one sense it means a revelation: God has sent down nothing authorising this worship. In another it means intellectual demonstration — the argument by which a claim is established — and al-Rāzī took it in that sense here, linking it to the second half of the verse. In a third it means sheer power, the sense in which Satan says at the judgment: *“I did not have any authority over you. I only called you, and you responded to me”* (14:22).

The three senses combine into one requirement. For a claim about God to be acted on, something must have come down from God establishing it, or something must be demonstrable by reason, or something must actually have power. The verse says the idols have none of the three.

**The Burden of Proof Is Placed on the Worshipper**

This is the structural move of the verse, and it is the one that matters most.

The verse does not argue that the idols do not exist, or that they are powerless in some absolute sense. It says that no warrant has come down for worshipping them and that the worshippers have no knowledge of them. The claim being made is not about the object; it is about the justification.

That relocates the argument. Instead of the messenger having to disprove the idols, the worshipper has to produce the authorisation — and the sūrah's whole case is that he cannot, because no such authorisation exists and because the worship of these things was inherited rather than established.

The Qur'an makes the inheritance explicit when it wants to, and the wording it uses closes the remaining exit. Of the idols it says that the worshippers have *"mere names that you and your forefathers have made up—a practice Allah has never authorized"* (53:23), and of the people it says that *"most of them follow nothing but ˹inherited˺ assumptions. ˹And˺ surely assumptions can in no way replace the truth"* (10:36). The verse is not demanding that the worshippers produce a demonstration of metaphysics. It is asking what they are relying on, and naming the answer before they give it.

**The Claim Is Falsifiable, and That Is the Point**

One of the more striking features of this verse is that it invites a test. If a warrant has been sent down, produce it. If the worshipper has knowledge of what he worships, state it.

The Qur'an makes this same challenge in the same form elsewhere, and the challenge is what makes the argument an argument rather than a denunciation: *“Call upon those you claim ˹to be divine˺ besides Allah. They do not possess ˹even˺ an atom’s weight either in the heavens or the earth, nor do they have any share in ˹governing˺ them”* (34:22).

The test is never met in the Qur'an's narratives. What happens instead is what happened at verse 72's recitations and what happened at verse 15's rope: rage, evasion, or an appeal to inheritance. The absence of a warrant is the sūrah's case, and it is a case that could in principle be answered.

**"The Wrongdoers Will Have No Helper"**

*Wa-mā li-l-ẓālimīna min naṣīr* — and the wrongdoers have no helper.

The word *naṣīr* is the one used for an ally who comes when called, the party to a pact of mutual defence. In the tribal vocabulary of the sūrah's audience, it was the word for the people who show up when there is fighting.

The claim is therefore concrete. On the day these people need their allies, none will come — and the irony is that the objects they worshipped were precisely the allies they had been counting on. Verse 12 established that those objects can neither harm nor benefit; verse 13 said they are worse than nothing; and this verse says that at the moment of need, there is no one.

The same sentence is put into Abraham's mouth in Sūrah al-ʿAnkabūt, addressed to exactly this arrangement, and it adds what the absence will feel like from the inside: *"You have taken idols ˹for worship˺ instead of Allah, only to keep ˹the bond of˺ harmony among yourselves in this worldly life. But on the Day of Judgment you will disown and curse one another. Your home will be the Fire, and you will have no helper!"* (29:25). The alliance dissolves in both directions: the gods do not come, and the worshippers turn on each other.

**Why "Wrongdoers" Rather Than "Disbelievers"**

The closing noun is *ẓālimīn*, those who do wrong — not *kāfirīn*, those who disbelieve.

The choice fits the argument the verse has just made. The charge was never that these people hold a false opinion about metaphysics. The charge is that they transferred to something else what belongs to God, which is an act of misplacement — the definition of *ẓulm*.

The sūrah used the same word at verse 25 for those who bar people from the Sacred Mosque, and it will use it at verse 72 for the rage on the disbelievers' faces. In every case the wrong is something done rather than something merely believed.

The Qur'an names the same act as the worst instance of the same category, and the naming is what settles the question of why *ẓālimīn* and not something milder: *"Never associate ˹anything˺ with Allah ˹in worship˺, for associating ˹others with Him˺ is truly the worst of all wrongs"* (31:13). The verse is not softening the charge by calling it wrongdoing. It is using the word the Qur'an reserves for the gravest misplacement there is.

**The Verse in the Sūrah's Cumulative Case**

By this point in the sūrah the case against idolatry has been made in four different registers: the functional test of verse 12, the escalating harm of verse 13, the parable of the falling man in verse 31, and now the demand for a warrant.

The four are not repetitions. Each addresses a different excuse — that the object delivers benefits, that the worship is harmless, that the practice is inherited, and now that the worshipper has grounds. Verse 71 closes the last of them.

What follows confirms the reading. Having established that no warrant exists, the sūrah stops arguing about what the idols can do and starts describing what they are: the fly of verse 73, which cannot recover what it loses, and the mismeasurement of God in verse 74. Those are not further arguments; they are illustrations of a conclusion already reached. The warrant was never produced, and the two verses that follow are written for a reader who has accepted that it never will be.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:72

> Whenever Our clear revelations are recited to them, you ˹O Prophet˺ recognize rage on the faces of the disbelievers, as if they are going to snap at those who recite Our revelations to them. Say, “Shall I inform you of something far more enraging than that? ˹It is˺ the Fire with which Allah has threatened those who disbelieve. What an evil destination!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Face Betrays What the Argument Conceals**

*Wa-idhā tutlā ʿalayhim āyātunā bayyinātin taʿrifu fī wujūhi alladhīna kafarū al-munkara* — and when Our signs are recited to them as clear proofs, you recognise the denial in the faces of those who disbelieve.

The sūrah has been interested in faces before. Verse 11 described a man turned over onto his; verse 9 described a shoulder turned away. Here the face is where the state of the heart becomes visible, and the verb *taʿrifu*, you recognise, implies something that can be read without being told.

That is the verse's first claim: the reaction is involuntary. Whatever these people say in argument, their faces have already answered, and the answer is not doubt or boredom. It is rejection that shows physically.

The Qur'an treats the face as a readable surface elsewhere, and always at moments of exposure. Sūrah Āl ʿImrān makes it the index of the verdict: *"On that Day some faces will be bright while others gloomy. To the gloomy-faced it will be said, 'Did you disbelieve after having believed?'"* (3:106). The same contrast opens Sūrah al-Qiyāmah and Sūrah ʿAbasa (75:22; 80:38–40). What happens here is the same disclosure in miniature: an interior state becoming legible on a face, in public, before anyone has been told anything.

**"They Almost Pounce"**

*Yakādūna yasṭūna bi-alladhīna yatlūna ʿalayhim āyātinā* — they are on the point of assaulting those who recite Our signs to them.

The verb *saṭā* means to seize violently, to spring on something and grip it. The commentators glossed it in exactly that sense. What is described is not debate and not even interruption; it is the edge of physical violence against the reciters.

The detail is worth holding on to, because it tells us what was actually happening. The recitation of the Qur'an in Makkah was not being met with counter-argument. It was being met with something closer to an attack, and the verse names the impulse precisely.

The verb's prefix matters just as much as the verb. *Yakādūna* — they are on the point of, they *almost* — records an impulse that stopped short of the act. The reciters in Makkah were unarmed and unclan-protected, and the sūrah is describing a hostility that could have been carried out and was not. Read as history, that is a statement about the community's exposure; read as revelation, it is a statement about the restraint that kept the recitation going.

**Why Recitation Produces Rage Rather Than Indifference**

The question the verse invites is why. If the message were merely false, the natural responses would be dismissal or boredom. Rage requires that something has been touched.

The sūrah has given the answer in its own vocabulary. The signs are *bayyināt*, clear proofs; the audience's position was described at verse 71 as resting on nothing sent down and nothing known. A person whose practice has no warrant, and who is told so plainly, does not experience the telling as information. He experiences it as an assault on the arrangement his life is built on.

That is also why the rage is directed at the reciters rather than at the argument. The reciter is the one who keeps bringing the thing into the room.

The noun for what shows on their faces carries the same charge. *Al-munkar* is the Qur'an's word for what is to be forbidden — the same word the believers are commanded to prohibit: *"enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong"* (3:104). What the Prophet recognises on these faces is therefore not a neutral expression of displeasure. It is the thing the community is commanded to stand against, appearing on the face of the person who objects to being told.

**"Shall I Tell You of Something Worse Than That?"**

*Qul a-fa-unabbiʾukum bi-sharrin min dhālikum* — Say: shall I inform you of something worse than that?

The rhetorical move is worth noting. The Prophet is not told to answer the rage with rage, or to answer it with a warning delivered in anger. He is told to ask a question, and the question is calibrated to the emotion in front of him.

Worse than what? The commentators took the comparison as worse than the anger itself — worse than the feeling that is contorting their faces. The question therefore meets these people where they actually are, in the middle of the emotion, rather than where they claim to be, in an argument about proofs.

The Qur'an uses the same interrogative frame when it wants to expose a ranking the audience has got backwards: *"Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ 'Shall I inform you of those who deserve a worse punishment from Allah ˹than the rebellious˺?'"* (5:60). The device is a revaluation rather than a threat: the hearer is invited to agree that something is bad, and then shown that he has been worried about the wrong thing.

**"The Fire Which God Has Promised the Disbelievers"**

*Al-nāru waʿadahā Allāhu alladhīna kafarū* — the Fire, which God has promised to those who disbelieve.

The answer reframes the comparison. They are enraged by the recitation; there is something more enraging than the recitation, and it is the destination the recitation was warning them about.

The phrasing is precise. The Fire is described as *promised* to them, which is the language of covenant and appointment rather than of threat. And it is described as what God promised, which means the promise will be kept — the sūrah said at verse 47 that God does not fail His promise.

The symmetry is the part that deserves attention, because the same verb is used for what the believers are given: *"Allah has promised the believers, both men and women, Gardens under which rivers flow, to stay there forever"* (9:72). One verb, two destinations, and the certainty is identical on both sides. The verse is not saying that God has issued a threat which may or may not be carried out. It is saying that these people have an appointment, in the same sense and from the same source as the believers have theirs.

**"What an Evil Destination"**

*Wa-biʾsa al-maṣīr* — and what an evil place of arrival.

The closing formula is the same one the Qur'an uses for the worst outcomes, and the noun *maṣīr* is the word for where a journey ends. The sūrah used the same root at verse 48: *to Me is the destination*.

The echo is deliberate. What these people are heading toward has already been named in this sūrah, and the verse's closing phrase is a summary rather than a new threat.

The formula is not peculiar to this context; Sūrah al-Taghābun closes the fate of the rejecters with it word for word: *"As for those who disbelieve and reject Our revelations, they will be the residents of the Fire, staying there forever. What an evil destination!"* (64:10). Identical wording, identical position after a description of rejection — which tells the reader how to hear it. It is the settled term for an arrival that cannot be reversed, and the sūrah is using a fixed formula rather than inventing an insult.

**The Reaction Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

The scene in this verse is not unique, and the repetitions are worth collecting because together they describe a single recognisable pattern.

Sometimes the response is mockery: *“When Our clear revelations are recited to them, the disbelievers ask the believers ˹mockingly˺, ‘Which of the two of us is better in status and superior in assembly?’”* (19:73). Sometimes it is a charge of sorcery: *“Whenever Our clear revelations are recited to them, the disbelievers say of the truth when it has come to them, ‘This is pure magic’”* (46:7). Sometimes it is dismissal: *“whenever Our revelations are recited to him, he says, ‘Ancient fables!’”* (68:15).

What none of them is, is a counter-argument. Every recorded response is a reclassification or a taunt, and this verse adds the one response that is not even verbal: the faces, and the impulse to strike.

That is why the Prophet is told to answer with a question rather than a proof. There is no argument in front of him to answer.

**What the Messenger Is Given to Say**

One last observation about the verse's construction. Everything up to *Say* is addressed to the Prophet as diagnosis — you will see this, you will recognise it, they will nearly attack. Then the Prophet is given exactly one sentence to say in response.

That sentence contains no insult, no threat of his own and no prediction about them. It asks a question and names a destination. The sūrah has been consistent about this since verse 49: the messenger's office is the warning, and the warning is not his to enforce.

What he is conspicuously not told is as important as what he is told. He is not told to stop reciting, to recite privately, to soften the passages that provoke this, or to answer the rage with a display of strength. The diagnosis is given to him in detail — the faces, the near-assault — and the instruction that follows it is to keep reciting and to say one sentence. The sūrah's confidence is that the recitation is doing something even where it produces nothing but fury, and that the fury itself is part of the evidence.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:73

> O humanity! A lesson is set forth, so listen to it ˹carefully˺: those ˹idols˺ you invoke besides Allah can never create ˹so much as˺ a fly, even if they ˹all˺ were to come together for that. And if a fly were to snatch anything away from them, they cannot ˹even˺ retrieve it from the fly. How powerless are those who invoke and those invoked!

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Parable Announced Before It Is Told**

*Yā ayyuhā al-nāsu ḍuriba mathalun fa-stamiʿū lahu* — O humanity, a parable is struck, so listen to it.

The verse opens by demanding attention before delivering anything, and the address is once again to all humanity rather than to the believers. The sūrah has used this address at its first verse and at verse 49, and both times what followed was something everyone is asked to weigh.

The word *mathal* is the term for a parable, a similitude, a comparison used to make a point land. The verb *ḍuriba* — struck — is the one used for striking a coin or a seal: the parable is being impressed rather than merely offered.

**The Test Is Set at the Lowest Possible Bar**

*Inna alladhīna tadʿūna min dūni Allāhi lan yakhlūqū dhubāban wa-law ijtamaʿū lahu* — those you call upon apart from God will never create a fly, even if they gathered together for it.

The challenge is not to create a universe, a mountain, or even a living thing of any size. It is to create a fly.

The choice of creature is the argument. A fly is small, ubiquitous, unregarded and, in the ancient world as now, faintly contemptible. If the test had been to create a camel or a human being, a worshipper could have replied that his god works in larger matters. There is no such reply available here. The bar has been set where no one can pretend it is too high.

The Qur'an uses the same logic elsewhere in the same vocabulary: *“Do they associate ˹with Allah˺ those ˹idols˺ which cannot create anything, but are in fact created”* (7:191), and the challenge issued directly: *“Those ˹idols˺ you invoke besides Allah are created beings like yourselves. So call upon them and see if they will answer you, if your claims are true!”* (7:194).

**The Second Half Is Worse Than the First**

*Wa-in yaslubhumu al-dhubābu shayʾan lā yastanqidhūhu minhu* — and if the fly snatches anything from them, they cannot recover it from it.

The escalation is deliberate and it is the verse's sharpest move. The first clause said the idols cannot *make* a fly; the second says that a fly can rob them and they cannot get it back. A thing that cannot recover what a fly has taken is not merely uncreative. It is weaker than the smallest creature in the room.

The scenario also echoes Abraham's confrontation with his people, where he broke the idols and left the largest one intact, and his people were forced to concede the point themselves: *“You already know that those ˹idols˺ cannot talk”* (21:65). In both passages the reduction is total, and in both it is achieved by the idolaters' own admission of what an idol can do.

**"Feeble Are the Seeker and the Sought"**

*Ḍaʿufa al-ṭālibu wa-l-maṭlūb* — weak is the seeker, and the sought.

The closing verdict names both parties, and the symmetry is the point. The worshipper is weak and the worshipped is weak, and the verse declines to grade them. A person who has spent his life venerating something weaker than an insect has been measured by what he venerates.

The two nouns are from the same root: *ṭālib* is the one who seeks, *maṭlūb* the one sought. Between them they describe the entire transaction of prayer, and the verdict covers both ends of it.

**The Fly in the Prophetic Teaching**

The choice of a fly has a further resonance in the tradition that is worth noting, because it runs in the same direction as the verse.

Abū Hurayrah narrated that the Prophet ﷺ said: *“If a housefly falls into the drink of any of you, let him dip it all in and then throw it away, for on one of its wings is a disease and on the other is a cure”* — recorded in *Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī* (3320). Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī's version adds that the fly protects itself with the diseased wing, and the report also appears in *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (3844, graded ṣaḥīḥ by al-Albānī) and *Sunan Ibn Mājah* (3505).

The relevance is the creature's agency. The fly in that report acts, chooses a wing, protects itself, and carries both a disease and its cure. It is, on the Qur'an's and the Prophet's account, a creature with capacities — which is precisely why it can be set against an idol that has none.

**The Parable Is Not an Insult but an Argument**

It is worth being clear about what kind of speech this is, because the tone is easily misread as mockery.

A parable in the Qur'an is a *mathal*: a comparison constructed to make a point that a direct statement would not make as well. The fly is not chosen to humiliate the idolaters' gods — the gods are not the audience and cannot be humiliated. It is chosen because the smallest available unit is the clearest test of a capacity claim.

If the claim were about dignity, the comparison would be unfair. The claim is about power, and a claim about power can be tested at any scale. That is why the parable works, and why the sūrah follows it immediately with the charge that these people have not measured God correctly: the fly was the measuring instrument.

**Why This Parable Rather Than Another**

The sūrah is near its end, and it has deployed several arguments against idolatry already. This is the last, and it is the most reductive.

The reduction is the method. Earlier arguments established that the idols cannot help (22:12), that they harm (22:13), that no warrant was sent down for them (22:71), and that God is the only truth (22:62). The parable of the fly takes all of that and makes it small enough to hold in one image: the thing you are praying to cannot swat an insect.

Nothing more needs to be said after it, and the sūrah does not say it. The next verse draws the conclusion in one line.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:74

> They have not shown Allah the reverence He deserves. Surely Allah is All-Powerful, Almighty.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Idiom of Measuring**

*Mā qadarū Allāha ḥaqqa qadrih* — they did not measure God with the measure due to Him.

The verb *qadara* and the noun *qadr* belong to the vocabulary of measuring, estimating, and assigning a thing its proper worth. The phrase is an idiom for failing to size someone up correctly — for treating a great thing as small.

That is the precise charge, and it is worth distinguishing from the ones around it. The verse does not say that these people denied God, or that they hated Him, or that they were ignorant of Him in the ordinary sense. It says that they got His size wrong.

The idiom appears three times in the Qur'an, and the comparisons are what fix its meaning. In Sūrah al-Anʿām the same sentence is directed at people who refused the possibility of revelation itself: *"And they have not shown Allah His proper reverence when they said, 'Allah has revealed nothing to any human being'"* (6:91). In Sūrah al-Zumar it is attached to the largest image of divine scale in the scripture, the earth held in a grip and the heavens rolled up in a hand (39:67). One case is a denial that God speaks to men, one is a denial that He is as large as He is, and the third is the one in front of us. All three are failures of estimation, and in none of them is the existence of God the point at issue.

**How a Person Measures God**

The failure is diagnosable, and the sūrah has spent its whole length supplying the correct measurement.

God is measured by what He does. He creates from dust, brings the dead field to life, holds the sky from falling, sends the rain, raises those in the graves, and will judge between every community on a day that belongs to Him alone. A person who has taken all of that in and assigns partners to God has not disbelieved in His existence; he has underestimated His claim.

The commentators put it two ways that amount to the same thing: al-Rāzī took the failure as a failure to give God the honour due to Him, since they set up stones beside Him; al-Ṭabarī took it as a failure to know Him as He ought to be known. Both are failures of estimation rather than of existence.

The two are not in tension, and holding them together is what makes the charge usable. Honour is what is owed; knowledge is what produces it. A person who knows what the sūrah has spent seventy-three verses describing and still sets up partners beside Him has not missed an argument. He has heard it and assigned it the wrong weight.

**Why the Charge Follows the Parable**

The placement is exact. The parable of the fly has just established what the idols are: things that cannot create an insect and cannot retrieve from it.

Verse 74 draws the inference that the parable left implicit. A person who prays to something weaker than a fly has a correspondingly small estimate of the One that thing is being set beside. The two verses are one argument, and the second is the conclusion.

This is why the verse's closing names matter so much: God is *qawiyy*, strong, and *ʿazīz*, mighty. Both are stated immediately after a demonstration of how weak the alternative is, and the contrast is the sentence.

The inference is a measurement in the literal sense. An idol that cannot recover what a fly takes from it is not a lesser god; it is a thing without the capacity the worshipper is attributing to it. Setting that beside the Creator is not a comparison of two powers. It is a misjudgement of the distance between them, and the verse's word for the misjudgement is the idiom of measuring.

**The Parallel in Sūrah al-Zumar**

The same charge appears in one of the most famous passages of the Qur'an, in a description of the Resurrection: the whole earth will be in His grip on that Day and the heavens folded in His right hand, and the verse then says that they did not measure God with the measure due to Him.

The parallel confirms what the idiom is for. Measuring God is not a matter of correct doctrine in the abstract; it is a matter of matching one's estimate of Him to what He will be seen to be when the estimate can no longer be revised.

The wording of that verse makes the connection unmissable: *"They have not shown Allah His proper reverence—when on the Day of Judgment the ˹whole˺ earth will be in His Grip, and the heavens will be rolled up in His Right Hand"* (39:67). The adverb *when* is doing the work. The failure of estimation is located at the moment of the largest possible disclosure, which is the sūrah's point exactly: the estimate is wrong now because it will be shown to be wrong then.

**The Charge Is Not Only for Idolaters**

The wording is general, and there is no reason to confine it.

A person can avoid idols entirely and still hold an estimate of God that is too small — a God who is primarily a resource, or a God whose commands are negotiable, or a God whose warnings are understood as rhetoric. Every one of those is a failure of measurement, and the sūrah has described people in exactly those terms: the man on the brink who assumed the religion was a transaction (22:11), and the doubters who concluded from a delay that nothing was coming (22:47).

That is why the verse sits where it does, in the sūrah's closing movement, addressed to an audience that already accepts the argument. The idols are gone from the room by now; the underestimation may not be.

The remedy the verse supplies is not an exhortation to try harder but a pair of names. The charge is that they did not measure God; the correction is to be told what He is — strong, and beyond reach. Estimation is repaired by knowledge of the one being estimated, which is why the sūrah has spent its length giving that knowledge rather than pleading for reverence.

**Estimation Is Tested by Behaviour**

The sūrah has given a testable account of what a correct estimate of God produces, and it is worth assembling, because the verse's charge is otherwise abstract.

A person who measures God correctly prays to Him alone, since setting up partners is the definitional act of underestimation. He is patient under what happens to him, since impatience is the assumption that the arrangement could have been better managed. He does not demand that the punishment be hurried, since hurrying it is the assumption that God's schedule needs improving. And he does not treat his own life as his own, since a correct estimate of the Creator includes a correct estimate of the creature.

Every one of those failures has appeared in this sūrah, and each was presented as a failure of measurement rather than of information. The man on the brink knew that God existed; he simply held a small estimate of what that meant.

**The Verse's Grammar Is an Understatement**

The Arabic opens with *mā qadarū Allāha* — they did not measure God. The negation is of the verb rather than of the object: the failure is in the act of measuring, not in the existence of what was to be measured.

That distinction is worth holding, because it explains why the sūrah does not treat idolatry as simple ignorance. These are not people who have never heard of God. They are people who have heard, and who have assigned the hearing the wrong weight — a failure the Qur'an describes elsewhere with the same vocabulary of covering over.

The understatement is itself rhetorical. The verse could have said they insulted God, or denied Him, or set up rivals to Him. It says instead that they got the measurement wrong, which is the more damning formulation, because an insult at least acknowledges the size of what is being insulted.

**What the Two Closing Names Do**

*Inna Allāha la-qawiyyun ʿazīz* — God is Strong, Mighty.

*Qawiyy* is strength: the capacity to do what is intended without obstruction. *ʿAzīz* is might in the sense of being beyond reach — the one who cannot be coerced, opposed or worn down.

Together they state the correct measurement in two words. The failure described in the first half of the verse was a failure to hold both, and the remedy the verse offers is not an argument but a pair of names.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:75

> Allah selects messengers from both angels and people, for Allah is truly All-Hearing, All-Seeing.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verb Is "Chooses"**

*Allāhu yaṣṭafī mina al-malāʾikati rusulan wa-mina al-nāsi* — God chooses messengers from the angels and from the people.

*Yaṣṭafī* is from the root meaning to select, to single out, to purify by choosing. It is the verb used for picking the best part of something, and it implies both a chooser with the right to choose and a selection made on the basis of what the chooser knows.

The emphatic claim here is about who does the selecting. The audience's objection, recorded throughout the Qur'an, was not that God sends messengers but that He sends *this* one — a man who eats, walks in the market and has no angel with him. The verse's answer is to assert the right of selection before addressing its form.

**From Both Categories**

The verse names two pools: angels and human beings.

The inclusion of angels is what makes the sentence work as an answer. Nobody in the audience doubted that God could send an angel; the whole objection was that He had not. By naming both categories in one breath, the verse makes the point that the choice between them is His, and that the existence of one category does not settle the question of who should be sent from it.

Al-Ṭabarī took the verse as a straightforward statement that God sends both — Gabriel and Michael among the angels, and men among humankind. Al-Qurṭubī read it as a reaffirmation that neither kind of sending is novel, which is the sense the sūrah needs, since the objection being answered was precisely that this sending was unprecedentedly lowly.

**Why a Human Messenger Is Fitting**

The sūrahs that answer this objection directly give two reasons, and both are worth having.

The first is that an angel would settle the matter by force of appearance. *“They say, ‘Why has no ˹visible˺ angel come with him?’ Had We sent down an angel, the matter would have certainly been settled ˹at once˺, and they would have never been given more time ˹to repent˺”* (6:8). Faith extracted by a visible angel is not faith, and the testing period would be over.

The second is that an angel would have to appear as a man anyway: *“And if We had sent an angel, We would have certainly made it ˹assume the form of˺ a man—leaving them more confused than they are”* (6:9). Sūrah al-Isrāʾ supplies the principle that governs the whole question: *“Had there been angels walking the earth, well settled, We would have surely sent down for them an angel from heaven as a messenger”* (17:95).

The logic is that a messenger must be able to be an example to the people he is sent to, and an example must be imitable. A being of another order cannot show a human being how to be a human being.

**The Objection's Own Incoherence**

The Qur'an records the objection in its own words, and the form it takes is worth noting: *“What kind of messenger is this who eats food and goes about in marketplaces for a living?”* (25:7).

The taunt concedes the argument. A messenger who did not eat and did not work would be precisely the un- imitable being the objectors imagine wanting. The eating and the walking in the market are the qualifications, and the verse treats them as the evidence of fitness rather than as disqualifications.

**The Selection Is Not a Compromise**

One more point is worth drawing out, because it is the sting in the objection the verse answers.

The demand for an angelic messenger was not a request for more evidence. It was a claim that a human messenger is a second-best arrangement — that God, had He been able, would have done better.

The verse's grammar refuses that. God *chooses*, and choosing implies knowledge and freedom rather than constraint. To say that He would have sent an angel if He could is to say that the sending of a man was the best available option rather than the selected one, which is precisely the mismeasurement verse 74 described.

That is why the sūrah answers the objection the way it does — not by arguing that human messengers work, but by asserting the right of the One who sends them.

**Hearing and Seeing Close the Argument**

*Inna Allāha samīʿun baṣīr* — God is Hearing, Seeing.

The same pair closed verse 61, where the subject was a person wronged twice. Here the subject is the selection of messengers, and the attributes do different work.

*Samīʿ* covers what is said about the messengers — the mockery, the charge of sorcery, the taunts about eating and walking in markets. None of it is unheard. *Baṣīr* covers the selection itself: God sees whom He is choosing, and the choice is not made in the dark.

There is a quiet reassurance in the pairing as well. A messenger who is being mocked is told that the One who sent him hears what is said and sees what is done, which is the same assurance the sūrah gave the believers at verse 60.

**The Verse's Connection to Verse 74**

Read the two verses together and they form one argument. Verse 74 said that these people had not measured God correctly; verse 75 names one of the specific ways the mismeasurement shows itself.

To object to God's choice of messenger is to claim a better judgement about who should be sent than the One sending. That is what an underestimation looks like in practice, and the sūrah names it immediately after diagnosing it.

The two verses also close a loop that opened early in the chapter. At verse 31 the sūrah described the associator as a man who has fallen from the sky — a picture of helplessness rather than rebellion. What has been true of the objectors throughout is not that they aimed too high but that they aimed too low, both about God and about the people He sends.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:76

> He knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them. And to Allah ˹all˺ matters will be returned ˹for judgment˺.

**Expanded Commentary**

**What "Before Them and Behind Them" Covers**

*Yaʿlamu mā bayna aydīhim wa-mā khalfahum* — He knows what is before them and what is behind them.
