The phrase is a Qur'anic idiom for total knowledge, and the two directions are chosen to be exhaustive rather than merely paired. What is in front of a person is what he can see and is moving toward; what is behind him is what he has left and cannot see. Between them there is nothing a person has that is not covered.

The commentators differed over the application. Al-Rāzī took *what is before them* as the Hereafter and *what is behind them* as the present world, which fits the idiom's use elsewhere for the angelic knowledge of a person's future and past. Read that way, the clause states that God knows both halves of every creature's timeline.

Either reading gives the same result, and the sūrah wants the result rather than the mechanism: nothing about these messengers, or about anyone else, is outside the knowing.

**Why the Clause Follows the Selection of Messengers**

The pronoun refers most immediately to the messengers of verse 75.

That placement is pointed. God chooses messengers, and He knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them — which is to say that the selection was not made in ignorance of what each of them would face. The rejections, the expulsions, the years in which nothing appeared to happen: all of it was known before the selection was made.

For the Prophet hearing this, that is the substance of the verse. His mission is not an experiment that has gone unexpectedly badly. The One who chose him knew what he was walking into, and the choice stands.

The commentators also noted a wider referent, and the two readings are complementary rather than competing. On the narrower reading the pronouns mean the messengers; on the broader one they mean the angels and human beings named in verse 75, or simply everyone, since the idiom is used elsewhere for whole generations. The sūrah needs both: the Prophet is told that his case is known in full, and the audience is told that theirs is.

**The Sūrah's Attribute Pattern**

Verses 75 and 76 are part of a closing sequence in which the sūrah names the divine attributes in rapid succession: Hearing and Seeing (v. 75), knowing all and the return (v. 76).

The pattern is worth noticing because it is how the sūrah ends. After seventy-six verses of argument, legislation, history and parable, the chapter closes by stating what God is rather than by adding another ruling. The attributes are the ground of everything that came before them.

The sequence is not confined to the last two verses. From verse 59 the sūrah has been closing its paragraphs with names: Most Forbearing (22:59), Ever-Pardoning and All-Forgiving (22:60), All-Hearing and All-Seeing (22:61), the Truth and the Most High (22:62), Most Subtle and All-Aware (22:63), Self-Sufficient and Praiseworthy (22:64), Ever Gracious and Most Merciful (22:65), and then Hearing, Seeing, Knowing here. The reader who follows the closing words of the paragraphs from verse 59 to verse 76 is given a complete account of the divine character in eighteen verses — and it is placed at the end of the sūrah on purpose, because everything the chapter demanded is being rested on who is making the demand.

**"To God All Matters Are Returned"**

*Wa-ilā Allāhi turjaʿu al-umūr* — and to God all matters are returned.

The verb is passive and the subject is *al-umūr*: affairs, matters, the whole business of human life in all its detail. Not people only — people return to Him — but the matters themselves: the disputes, the plans, the injuries, the unfinished work.

That breadth is the verse's comfort and its warning. Every unresolved thing is going somewhere, and the somewhere is a knowing agent rather than an archive.

The same formula appears at the end of several sūrahs and in the middle of others, always in this form: *and to God all matters are returned*. The repetition across the Qur'an is what allows the reader to fill in the sense — the return is for judgment, and the judgment is by the One who knew the matter from both ends.

**The Verse as a Bridge**

Verses 74 to 76 form a unit: the failure to measure God, the right of selection, and the knowledge and return that make the selection meaningful.

They also prepare the sūrah's close. The two final verses are commands — bow and prostrate (v. 77), and strive for God as He deserves (v. 78) — and they are addressed to a community that has just been told who is addressing it. Knowledge of what is before and behind, and the return of all matters, are the premises on which a command of that weight can rest.

The bridge is also a reply to the sūrah's own opening. Verse 1 told humanity to fear its Lord because the Hour is a dreadful thing, and the whole chapter has been spent showing that the Hour is real. What verses 74 to 76 add is the standing implication: if matters are returned, then the things people do with them are not lost. A command to strive makes sense only against that background, and the sūrah places the premise immediately before the command.

**Knowledge and Return Belong Together**

The verse joins two clauses that are often separated, and the joining is the sūrah's final theological statement.

Comprehensive knowledge on its own would be surveillance. A return of all matters without knowledge would be an emptying. Held together they describe a judgment: everything a person has done is known, and everything a person has done is going to be answered for.

That is the premise the sūrah's closing commands rest on. Prayer, alms and holding fast (v. 78) are not undertakings a person would take up if his affairs were simply going to dissolve. They are the behaviour of someone who expects his matters to arrive somewhere and to be examined when they do.

**The Clause Answers the Sūrah's Hardest Question**

Every sūrah that promises a judgment owes its reader an answer to one objection: why is the judgment taking so long?

This sūrah has taken the question more seriously than most. It devoted three verses to the demand that the punishment be hurried (22:47), answered that the measures of time are incommensurable, and then documented the pattern in the ruins of nine communities (22:42–48). Verse 76 is the last word on it. All matters are returned, and the return has a destination that is not in question.

What is in question is only the interval, and the sūrah has been consistent about what the interval is for: the invitation, the testing, and the record that is being written while both proceed.

Those three answers were given separately and they belong together. Verse 47 said the delay is not a delay on the only clock that matters. Verse 70 said nothing is being lost while the wait continues, because it is all in a record. Verse 76 says where the record is going. A community that had been taunted for ten years about a punishment that had not arrived is given, in closing, a reason for the wait, an account of what is happening during it, and a destination at the end of it.

**Why the Sūrah Ends This Way Rather Than With a Threat**

The sūrah opened with the most severe threat in the Qur'an's repertoire — an earthquake at the Hour, mothers dropping children. It closes with attributes and commands.

The movement is deliberate. A reader who has followed the argument from verse 1 to verse 76 does not need another warning; he needs to know what is being asked of him, and by whom. Verses 77 and 78 supply the first; verses 74 to 76 have just supplied the second.

The last thing to notice is that the sūrah ends by addressing the believers rather than the deniers. The arguments against idolatry are finished; the parable has been told; the objection about messengers has been answered. What remains is a people who accept all of it and must now be told what to do — and the instruction they are given, in the two verses that follow, is to bow, to prostrate, to do good, and to strive. The threat was for the opening. The command is for the end.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:77

> O believers! Bow down, prostrate yourselves, worship your Lord, and do ˹what is˺ good so that you may be successful.

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Address Changes**

*Yā ayyuhā alladhīna āmanū* — O you who believe.

This is the first time in the sūrah that the believers are addressed by this formula. For seventy-six verses the sūrah has said *O humanity* (22:1, 22:49, 22:73) or spoken about the believers in the third person. Now, at the very end, it turns and speaks to them directly.

The shift is the sūrah's structure in miniature. The chapter has been addressed to everyone: the Hour announced to all people, the argument offered to doubters, the evidence set out for anyone who will look. Only after the argument is complete does the sūrah turn to those who accepted it and tell them what is now required.

The order matters. Worship commanded before the case is made would be a demand; worship commanded after it is an answer.

**Four Imperatives, in Ascending Order**

*Irkaʿū wa-sjudū wa-ʿbudū rabbakum wa-ifʿalū al-khayra* — bow, prostrate, worship your Lord, and do good.

The four commands are not a list of equivalent items, and the sequence is not arbitrary.

The first two are specific physical acts: *rukūʿ*, the bowing, and *sujūd*, the prostration. Both are named separately rather than being folded into the third, which is *ʿibādah*, worship in general. The specification is what gives the command its shape — the religion being described has a body, and the body does particular things.

The fourth command is the widest: *do the good*. It is not limited to ritual, and its placement last is significant. The three acts directed at God come first; the act directed at the world comes after, and cannot replace them.

**The Second Prostration of This Sūrah**

This verse is one of the two places in Sūrat al-Ḥajj at which the reciter bows, and the practice is attested in the ḥadīth literature.

ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir reported that he asked the Messenger of Allah ﷺ whether there are two prostrations in Sūrat al-Ḥajj, and was told: *“Yes, and whoever does not perform them should not recite them”* — recorded in *Sunan Abī Dāwūd* (1402), where al-Albānī graded the chain weak, though others have accepted it. A related report from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, also in Abū Dāwūd (1401), counts fifteen prostrations in the Qur'an, among them two in this sūrah. The first is at verse 18.

The fit between verse and practice is exact, and the first prostration makes the point better than the second does. At verse 18 the sūrah described everything in creation bowing down — the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountains, the trees and the animals — and the human being as the only creature that can stand. The reciter who bows there is joining the list.

**What the Bowing Means**

The prostration is the posture in which the highest part of a person — his face, the seat of his dignity and his recognition — is placed on the ground.

That is why the Qur'an treats it as the summary act of worship rather than as one gesture among many. Everything in the sūrah's argument leads to it: the creature made from dust, raised from the grave, given a record, and told that sovereignty on the final day belongs to another, has one fitting posture available to him.

The sūrah has also been insistent that the inward state is what matters (22:32, 22:37). Placing that insistence beside a command to perform a physical act resolves a question readers often force into a contradiction: the inward state is what the act is for, and the act is how the inward state is expressed. Neither is the other's rival.

**"That You May Prosper"**

*Laʿallakum tufliḥūna* — so that you may prosper, perhaps you will succeed.

The verb *aflaḥa* is from a root meaning to split open, used for a seed breaking and for a field yielding. Success in this vocabulary is not the acquisition of something but the fruition of something.

The particle *laʿalla* carries hope or purpose, and its presence at the end of a command is worth noting. The verse does not say that doing these four things mechanically produces prosperity. It says that this is the path on which prosperity is to be hoped for, which leaves the outcome with God.

**"Do Good" Is the Command That Has No Limit**

The fourth imperative is the one the sūrah leaves open, and the openness is deliberate.

Bowing and prostrating have forms; worship has its rites, which this sūrah legislated at length. Doing *al-khayr*, the good, has no specified form, no quota and no closing date. The sūrah has used the same word at verse 72, where the Fire was described as something worse than what enrages them, and the two uses frame the same idea: the good is the undetermined remainder of what a person is asked to do.

That is also why the command is placed last. A religion of specified acts alone would be completable, and a person could finish it. Appending an open obligation means the religion cannot be discharged by performance — which is consistent with a sūrah that has located the value of every rite in the state of the person performing it (22:32).

**Why This Comes Before the Final Verse**

The sūrah's last verse will command striving — a word that, in the sūrah's own context, has just been used for fighting (22:39), for emigration (22:58) and for the campaign against the signs (22:51).

Putting the command to bow and prostrate immediately before it is the sūrah's final ordering principle. The striving is real and it is costly, and the person who does it is first of all a person who bows. A community that reverses that order has misread the sūrah.

## Sūrah al-Ḥajj 22:78

> Strive for ˹the cause of˺ Allah in the way He deserves, for ˹it is˺ He ˹Who˺ has chosen you, and laid upon you no hardship in the religion—the way of your forefather Abraham. ˹It is Allah˺ Who named you ‘the ones who submit’ ˹in the˺ earlier ˹Scriptures˺ and in this ˹Quran˺, so that the Messenger may be a witness over you, and that you may be witnesses over humanity. So establish prayer, pay alms-tax, and hold fast to Allah. He ˹alone˺ is your Guardian. What an excellent Guardian, and what an excellent Helper!

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Strive for God With the Striving Due to Him"**

*Wa-jāhidū fī Allāhi ḥaqqa jihādih* — and strive for God with the striving that is His due.

The construction mirrors the phrase in verse 74, where the sūrah said that these people had not measured God with the measure due to Him. The same grammatical shape appears twice in five verses, and the pairing is deliberate: where the estimate of God falls short, the striving for Him falls short with it.

The commentators took the striving in several directions. Al-Qurṭubī read it as striving to do what God commands and to keep away from what He forbids, and to do both for neither worldly advantage nor reputation. Al-Ṭabarī took it as striving without fearing the blame of those who blame. Al-Rāzī read it as a call to strive as the earliest generation strove.

The readings are not in competition. What they share is the exclusion of a striving undertaken for anything else — for a tribe, a party, a reputation, or the pleasure of winning. The qualifying phrase *fī Allāh*, in God, is what does the work, and it is the same qualifier the sūrah used at verse 58 for emigration.

**The Striving Is Not Only Military**

It is worth saying this plainly, because the word has been narrowed in later usage and the sūrah has used it in more than one sense.

Three verses before the end, the sūrah used the same root for people who *strive against* the signs (22:51). In verse 58 it was used for leaving one's home. In verse 39 the permission was for fighting. The verb covers the expenditure of effort, and the object determines what kind of striving it is.

Some commentators read this verse as a call to inward effort and cited a report in which the Prophet ﷺ is said to have remarked, on returning from a campaign, that the community had returned from the lesser striving to the greater. [UNVERIFIED: that report is widely regarded by ḥadīth critics as weak or without a sound chain; I have not been able to confirm a reliable collection or number for it, and it is cited here only as an interpretive tradition, not as evidence.] The verse is sufficiently general without it: the striving due to God is whatever the situation requires, done for Him.

**"He Has Chosen You"**

*Huwa ijtibākum* — He has chosen you. The verb is from the same family as the *yaṣṭafī*, He chooses, of verse 75, which was used for the selection of messengers.

The echo is the sūrah's last structural move. God chooses messengers, and God chooses a community, and the two elections are described with the same vocabulary. What is being said to the believers here is that their standing is not an achievement they consolidated; it is a selection.

That is also why the clause precedes the command to strive. The striving is a response to being chosen rather than a bid for it.

**"And Has Not Laid Upon You Any Hardship in the Religion"**

*Wa-mā jaʿala ʿalaykum fī al-dīni min ḥaraj* — and He has placed no *ḥaraj* upon you in the religion.

*Ḥaraj* is the word for constriction, for being squeezed into a narrow place. Al-Ṭabarī glossed it as confinement or straitening. What the verse denies is that the religion is a narrow passage.

The jurists filled the phrase with content: the concessions granted under difficulty, such as shortening the prayers while travelling and praying seated or lying down when ill, and the general ruling that no sin is without a way out of it. Sūrah al-Baqarah states the principle in a form that covers every obligation: *“Allah intends ease for you, not hardship”* (2:185), and *“Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford”* (2:286).

The placement of this clause between the command to strive and the description of the creed is significant. A sūrah that has just demanded the striving due to God immediately removes the assumption that the demand is impossible.

**"The Creed of Your Father Abraham"**

*Millata abīkum Ibrāhīm* — the creed of your father Abraham.

This is where the sūrah closes its own circle. Abraham was commissioned at verse 26 to purify the House, commanded at verse 27 to call humanity to the pilgrimage, named at verse 31 as the model of the *ḥanīf*, and invoked at verse 43 among the messengers whose people denied them. The final verse names him as the community's father and its creed as his.

The word *millah* denotes a religion considered as a way rather than as a set of propositions, and the Qur'an attaches it to Abraham consistently: followers of his creed are commanded to follow it in sūrah after sūrah (3:95; 4:125; 6:161; 16:123). In nearly every occurrence the word *ḥanīf* appears with it, which returns the reader to verse 31 of this sūrah and to the turning that the whole chapter has been demanding.

**"He Named You Submitters Before, and in This"**

*Huwa sammākumu al-muslimīna min qablu wa-fī hādhā* — He named you the submitters aforetime and here.

Two readings were recorded. Some took the naming as Abraham's prayer, recorded in Sūrah al-Baqarah, that God make him and his progeny a community submitting to Him (2:128) — which would make the name ancient and petitioned for. Others took the subject as God: He named you submitters in the earlier scriptures and now in this recitation.

The second reading is the stronger one grammatically, since the sentence's subject is the same pronoun that governs the choosing. But both converge on the same claim: the name is not the community's invention and the identity is not recent. What the Qur'an is describing is a standing description that the earlier revelation also used.

**"So That the Messenger May Be a Witness Over You, and You Witnesses Over Humanity"**

*Li-yakūna al-rasūlu shahīdan ʿalaykum wa-takūnū shuhadāʾa ʿalā al-nās* — so that the Messenger be a witness over you, and you be witnesses over humanity.

The purpose clause is the verse's centre of gravity, and the sūrah has been building toward it since verse 17.

Sūrah al-Baqarah states the same arrangement in the same words: *“And so We have made you ˹believers˺ an upright community so that you may be witnesses over humanity and that the Messenger may be a witness over you”* (2:143). Al-Rāzī's note on the arrangement is the one to keep: God wants the witnesses over humanity to be upright and just, so that others can come to faith through them. Being a witness is therefore a function rather than a privilege, and it can be discharged badly.

Ibn Kathīr took the witnessing in a second sense as well — that the community is *witnessed*, pointed at, held up as an example. Both senses bind the same obligation: a community that is looked at is obliged to be lookable at.

**The Closing Commands**

*Fa-aqīmū al-ṣalāta wa-ātū al-zakāta wa-ʿtaṣimū bi-Llāh* — so establish the prayer, give the alms, and hold fast to God.

The three commands are the sūrah's last words of instruction, and they compress what the chapter has been arguing. The prayer is the posture of verse 77; the alms are the fourth item of verse 41 and the giving of verse 35; holding fast is the response of a person who has been told, seventy-seven verses running, that everything else he might hold is not his.

The verb *iʿtaṣimū* means to grip, to take hold of something for protection. The Qur'an uses the same image elsewhere of holding fast to a rope (3:103), and the image is of a person holding on rather than of a person standing on his own.

**The Last Words**

*Huwa mawlākum fa-niʿma al-mawlā wa-niʿma al-naṣīr* — He is your Master, and what an excellent Master, and what an excellent Helper.

The sūrah that opened with an earthquake closes with two nouns: *mawlā*, the protecting patron, and *naṣīr*, the ally who comes when called. Both are the words the sūrah used for what the idolaters thought they had — a patron (22:13) and a helper (22:71) — and both are here returned to their true owner.

The sūrah began by telling humanity to fear its Lord because the Hour is a dreadful thing. It ends by telling the believers who their Patron is. Between those two sentences lies the whole argument: the world is going to end, the dead will be raised, the idols are weaker than flies, the messenger has been chosen, and the One who holds all of it has taken this community as His own.
